Spie · SPIE-FR · the new Memo conveyor's card of 25 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Spie

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 44,231 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

B709th of 36

Memo topic 1, Tailwinds in the operating environment: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

SPIE's external environment is net positive and improving at the level that drives its earnings, and net negative at the level that drives its multiple. The structural driver (European, above all German, grid investment) is contractually and fiscally underpinned for two to four years, is accelerating in the order book now, and will reach revenue progressively from 2027 as labour and equipment allow. The legacy drags (French fibre, French tertiary building, Middle East) are mechanically fading or small. Competitive conduct has become more rational with Equans' margin repair, while competition for acquisition targets has intensified. The adverse forces (French fiscal and political risk ahead of the April 2027 election, the tax surcharge, the risk-off regime, the AI-financing crack) bite through the risk premium and the tax line more than through volumes. The Consumer should model an organic recovery from about 2% in FY26 to 3% to 4% in FY27 to FY28 led by Germany, a flat France, unchanged pricing assumptions and a slight timing haircut on high-voltage conversion, and should not rely on external forces to lift the multiple in the next six months.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0254C-0547C-0742C-0946C-3560C-3942
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

B+543rd of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: price competition for customers is flat to easing overall and easing where it matters most (grid, specialised niches), worsening only in French tertiary building; competition for acquisition targets is intensifying, which is the more important change for SPIE's model.

  • Easing. Equans, the main source of under-priced bidding in 2021 to 2023 (2.9% margin in 2023), is restoring margin (about 4.2% in 2025, 5% target for 2027) and was described by SPIE management as more price-disciplined. VINCI Energies (7.4% to 7.8% margin) and Eiffage (6.2%) bid selectively. In high-voltage and live-substation work, scarce crews let contractors choose jobs; Bernstein puts SPIE's German grid margins at 15% to 16%. Expert calls see SPIE and VINCI Energies slightly gaining share and Bouygues (Equans) losing.
  • Worsening. French residential contractors are bidding aggressively on tertiary projects; SPIE has chosen to give up volume (France organic -1.6% in 2025, -0.7% in H1 2026) to hold French margins (7.1% FY25; 6.2% H1 2026).
  • M&A market. Germany is now the battleground: Eiffage (Salvia, Eqos), VINCI Energies (30 to 40 deals a year at 6x to 10x EV/EBIT), Brookfield (15 German installers since 2023), Instalco (Fabri), Nordic and Danish roll-ups (InstallatørGruppen). This pressures the 5x to 8x entry multiples that underpin SPIE's returns.
  • For the model. Keep pricing assumptions stable to slightly positive in grid/nuclear, flat in maintenance, negative-volume/flat-price in French building; raise assumed acquisition multiples (7x to 9x EBITA for new bolt-ons rather than 5x to 8x).
C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

B478th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: availability, not cost, is the binding input; skilled-labour and equipment scarcity cap volume and delay revenue (a headwind), but the same scarcity supports price and the cost side is largely indexed (a tailwind). Net effect on margin is neutral to slightly positive; on revenue timing it is a headwind. The specific risk is margin leaking to subcontractors.

  • Labour. Wage inflation about 4% to 4.5% in Germany and 5% to 6% in the Netherlands; staff cost per employee accelerating (Deutsche Bank), but revenue per employee has outgrown it for five years. Germany's bottleneck ratio is 615 vacancies per 100 unemployed electrical engineers. SPIE's mitigants are strong (voluntary turnover 5.9% in 2025; about 1,200 referral hires; 504 German apprentices; SGS adds 800 technicians).
  • Subcontractors. Subcontracting and temporary labour exceed 56% of total expenses (2025 registration document); one-third of purchasing is subcontracting. Execution evidence is mixed in new growth areas: a Cologne data-centre project suffered from reliance on unfamiliar subcontractors (July 2026); a Dutch high-voltage project ran up to six months late with 20% to 25% overruns (October 2026). SPIE's direct-labour ratio in data centres and substations (65% to 70%) is below VINCI Energies' (75% to 80%).
  • Materials and equipment. Copper above USD 14,000 a tonne; transformers and switchgear on 128-to-160-week lead times from a few OEMs; buying close to contract start and daily-indexed pricing tools limit cost risk, but lead times shift revenue recognition.
  • For the model. No margin penalty from input costs in the base case; a timing haircut to high-voltage revenue conversion; a downside scenario of 20 to 40 bps margin leakage if subcontractor reliance rises in grid and data-centre work.
C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

C4213th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: macro bites hardest on SPIE's valuation (risk premium) rather than its earnings; at the earnings level it bites hardest in France and in industrial clients; at sector and industry level it is felt through a weak construction cycle and a risk-off market regime.

  • Company level. Organic growth tracks "GDP-plus" (Morgan Stanley) with electrification as an overlay; 70% to 75% maintenance and indexation cushion it. Rising rates barely touch earnings (negative working capital; fixed-coupon bonds; 50% of the term loan swapped) even with Bund yields at a post-2011 high of 3.44%. High energy prices squeeze heavy-industry clients but accelerate corporate electrification.
  • Sector level. Eurozone construction PMI 43.4 (contraction), input-price PMIs 66.9; Bilfinger's September 2026 warning on chemical-plant call-offs. SPIE's 80% recurring mix (Bilfinger 60%) and about 20% portfolio overlap limited the read-across.
  • Industry and market level. Risk-off regime: US 10-year about 5.10%, Brent USD 111, defensives leading cyclicals, breadth in the bottom fifth of three years, French spreads at decade highs. SPIE's shares fell 13% in three months while FY27 EPS estimates were unchanged: the macro force is acting on the multiple.
  • For the model. No macro haircut to EBITA beyond the France assumption; the valuation question (C-83) is where macro matters.
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–46

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: four further dynamics matter, net slightly negative over six months and neutral over two years.

  • Skilled-labour scarcity as a two-edged force (the dominant one): the Sector Digest's rule is that "the skilled technician is both the pricing power and the volume cap". For SPIE it supports price and keeps bidding rational, but it caps the organic acceleration and pushes work to subcontractors who capture some of the scarcity margin.
  • The financing of the AI build-out is cracking before its demand (Fitness for Fighting, week to 23-Oct-26: neocloud and hyperscaler credit spreads wider). SPIE's data-centre clients are mid-sized co-location developers who rely on external financing; the risk is order delays, not cancellations of existing grid work.
  • Peer sentiment and rotation. Bilfinger's warning (shares -44% over twelve months) and the Nordic installer re-rating (Bravida +63.5%, Instalco +67%) moved money towards volume-inflection stories and away from steady compounders; SPIE's multiple did not follow its estimates.
  • Currency and weather. Currency is negligible (89.5% of production in euros; translation -0.2% in H1 2026; Global Services Energy suffered -4.2% from the dollar in 2025). Weather is a recurrent Q1 noise item (one-third of revenue is outdoor work).
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

B606th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: overall threat low to moderate; standard entrants are the main nuisance, but in the acquisition market rather than for customers.

  • Adjacent entrants (moderate-low). Equipment makers (Schneider, Siemens, Eaton, Vertiv) expanding into integration services and automation projects; Bernstein notes they "increasingly compete through electrification and automation projects" that overlap with industrial services. They avoid dense field-labour models. Construction groups lack the specialised engineering and labour.
  • Standard entrants (moderate, M&A-side). Private-equity-backed consolidators (Brookfield, Nimlas, InstallatørGruppen, Nordic Climate Group) compete for targets; they rarely win SPIE's customers.
  • Orthogonal entrants (low). AI-native facility platforms, hardware-agnostic building-automation software, behind-the-meter power developers. Blocked by the need for licensed physical execution; hyperscaler microgrids could trim some grid-connection scope in data centres.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

D4222nd of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: material suppliers are a minor risk (fragmented base, indexation, late buying); labour suppliers (subcontractors and temporary staff) are the material source of risk because they are over half of costs and their availability and quality now vary in the growth segments.

  • Supply base. About 78,000 suppliers and subcontractors; 8,000 core partners account for EUR 3.3bn of purchases; external subcontracting about EUR 1.8bn; no single-source dependence except high-voltage equipment from a few global OEMs.
  • Subcontractors. Subcontracting and temporary labour above 56% of total expenses; expert evidence of execution errors and delays when inexperienced subcontractors are used (Cologne data centre; Dutch substations); SPIE subcontracts mechanical project managers for lack of in-house engineers.
  • Risk-control. Supplier charter, safety prequalification, central procurement databases; a seven-year copper supply agreement and daily indexation (Bernstein).
  • For the model. Base case no margin leakage; downside 20 to 40 bps if subcontractor costs rise faster than indexation in Germany and the Netherlands.
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0168C-0366C-0460C-0872C-3264C-3762C-4355
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

C+689th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: demand is splitting into a strengthening structural part and a weak cyclical/legacy part; the structural part now dominates the forward picture and has two-plus years of contracted visibility, while the cyclical part is near its bottom but politically fragile.

  • Structural and strengthening (Germany-led). Transmission and distribution work (cited at 34% of 2024 group revenue) is backed by a two-to-three-year high-voltage backlog and a record German order book. Customer budgets are explicit and rising: TenneT about EUR 67bn of grid spending from 2026; Amprion EUR 42.1bn for 2026 to 2030; E.ON EUR 48bn for 2026 to 2030; German transmission operators at least EUR 14bn a year by 2032; European grid spending about EUR 500bn in 2025 to 2030 against EUR 320bn historically. Germany's EUR 500bn infrastructure fund flows from 2027. Data centres (about 3% of group revenue; German capacity +70% by 2030), French nuclear (Grand Carénage; EPR2 decision expected late 2026) and EU-taxonomy-aligned work (50% of 2025 revenue) add to this. Customers are also outsourcing technical departments because of engineer scarcity and signing five-to-ten-year frameworks (expert calls, Netherlands and Germany).
  • Cyclical or structurally declining. French fibre roll-out run-off (about 1 pp drag on French organic growth; residual business about EUR 120m in 2026, so the drag mechanically fades through 2027); French tertiary building (new-home reservations -14.3% early 2026 spilled into commercial tenders); Dutch petrochemical clients (high energy prices, Chinese imports); German greenfield industrial capex; Middle East maintenance suspensions (under 1% of group revenue but -9.3% organic in Q2 for Global Services Energy).
  • Change, last six months against the prior 12 to 18. Group organic growth decelerated from 4.3% (2024) to 2.0% (2025) and 1.2% (H1 2026; Q1 -0.9% on weather, Q2 +3.1%). Germany accelerated to +7.3% in Q2; France fell -1.9% in Q2, which Bernstein reads as the bottom (Q3 about -0.5%, Q4 about flat).
  • Visibility. About 85% of each year's revenue is secured at the start of the year; maintenance and existing-asset support is 70% to 75% of revenue. The visibility caveat is timing, not existence: high-voltage revenue is gated by equipment lead times (switchgear and transformers 128 to 160 weeks) and connection permitting, and "regulated money pays design first and contractors later" (Sector Digest).
  • For the model. Group organic about 2% to 2.5% in FY26 (H1 1.2% plus an easier H2 comparison), 3% to 3.5% in FY27, 3% to 4% in FY28; the cone is about plus or minus 1.5 pp, skewed slightly up in 2027 to 2028 (German funding) and down in the next six months (French politics).
C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

A661st of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the dominant disruption force (electrification and AI-driven power demand) is strengthening and acts positively on SPIE; the substitution forces that could act negatively (behind-the-meter power, equipment makers integrating installation, AI-native facility software) are real but slow and mostly confined to the data-centre niche, which is only about 3% of revenue.

  • Positive and strengthening. Electrification (European electricity consumption +60% over the medium term; EU Electrification Action Plan targets a 46% electricity share by 2040 from 23%), grid hardening (40% of European distribution grids are over 40 years old), data-centre density and liquid cooling, German efficiency rules for data centres (power usage effectiveness below 1.2; 20% waste-heat recovery) all raise technical complexity, which favours multi-technical integrators with in-house capability (SPIE delivers about 75% of its FRA7 data-centre value in-house).
  • The second-tier position. The Industry Digest's judgement is that the market has paid the makers of scarce equipment (ABB, Siemens Energy) but not the channel and crews (SPIE -13.3% over three months, Rexel -11.8%), which gain volume and mix rather than price. That is the mechanism by which the disruption benefits SPIE: more volume, better mix, not a step-change in pricing.
  • Negative but slow. Behind-the-meter generation for data centres (gas turbines, fuel cells; Meta 2 GW, Oracle) bypasses grid connections; grid-enhancing software and storage may defer some substation work; equipment makers pre-integrate installation (Bloom Energy); some utilities take engineering in-house (Ameren's EPCM model). None is visible in SPIE's numbers; commissioning and grid connections remain regulated and labour-gated.
  • Disproved. Software-only displacement of installers: the skilled-labour bottleneck is the barrier.
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

C+6010th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: large change is under way in both directions; German and EU measures lift SPIE's 2027 to 2030 volumes, while French fiscal and political stress weighs more on the multiple and on tax than on revenue in the next 6 to 12 months.

  • Supportive. Germany's EUR 500bn infrastructure and climate-neutrality fund (EUR 300bn federal, EUR 100bn climate fund, EUR 100bn states) with benefits expected from early 2027; the German readiness-based grid-connection queue (from 1 April 2026) that prioritises viable projects; the EU Electrification Action Plan (July 2026); France's third energy programme (60% decarbonised energy by 2030) and its electrification plan doubling state support to EUR 10bn a year; EPR2 (six reactors, about EUR 72.8bn) with a decision expected late 2026; the EPBD recast (but member states missed the May 2026 transposition date and infringement procedures opened in July 2026, so it lengthens the tail rather than driving next year).
  • Adverse. French sovereign spreads at decade highs with spillover to Italy; instability ahead of the April 2027 presidential election; a French corporate tax surcharge (EUR 15.8m exceptional charge in FY25; normative group tax rate 30.2%; Morgan Stanley cut FY26 EPS about 5% for a higher tax rate); possible German cuts to grid operators' allowed returns, which operators would push down to service partners; speculative populist rollback of green subsidies.
  • How it bears. Direct French public-sector exposure is small (about 17% of French sales, 5% of group revenue, mostly mission-critical maintenance). The bigger French channel is private clients deferring non-recurrent investment (non-recurrent activity is about 25% of group revenue) and the country risk premium on the shares (Fitness for Fighting tilt: France Double Underweight).
  • For the model. Volume benefit from German funding from 2027; FY26 to FY27 tax rate 30% to 31% with upside risk; no material revenue haircut for French public budgets, but keep French organic at 0% to 1% through 2027.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

C+729th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: yes, strongly: German and European grid investment is accelerating SPIE's demand, it is the single most important organic driver, and SPIE is the purest listed exposure to it; the acceleration in reported revenue will be gradual because labour and equipment, not demand, set the pace.

  • Evidence. Germany organic +7.3% in Q2 2026 (H1 +3.9%) against a flat domestic industrial base; record German order book; high-voltage backlog two to three years; a five-substation TenneT contract; Bernstein rates SPIE the leading independent European supplier and the highest-purity listed grid exposure; Bilfinger has none. Germany is 35% of revenue (EUR 3,582m in 2025) and, per Bernstein, German margins rise from 7.9% to 8.1% by 2028.
  • Secondary drivers. Data centres (European market +15% a year to 2030; SPIE targets mid-sized co-location with high in-house content); nuclear life extension and EPR2 in France; battery storage (Tesla European framework; a 180-unit Belgian project worth over EUR 100m).
  • Magnitude. FactSet geographic consensus has Germany at EUR 4,088m (FY26), 4,639m (FY27) and 4,882m (FY28), i.e. growth of +14%, +13% and +5% including acquisitions; ex-acquisitions, a German organic rate of 5% to 7% would alone add 1.75 to 2.5 pp to group organic growth.
  • Limit. Germany's labour bottleneck and Morgan Stanley's view that infrastructure-fund benefits do not reach organic revenue before 2027.
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

C+6411th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: customer demand supports the thesis in its largest and most visible parts (utilities, transmission operators, nuclear, data-centre co-locators, defence and healthcare) and is weak in its smallest and most cyclical parts; the support is fragile mainly through customer financing and regulation, not through demand itself.

  • Customer base. Close to 30,000 customers; top ten 18% of revenue; no customer above 10%; energy and utilities 25%, regulated public bodies 14%, manufacturing 10%; about 80% recurring customers; maintenance retention above 90%; 85% of annual revenue visible at the start of the year.
  • Supportive behaviour. Transmission operators stepping up capex; utilities and industrials outsourcing technical departments for lack of engineers and seeking five-to-ten-year frameworks; clients accepting indexation and prioritising execution certainty; EDF extending nuclear life (contracts up to 17 years; a new five-year electrical framework).
  • Weak behaviour. French private clients deferring non-recurrent projects before the 2027 election; French tertiary owners buying on price; Dutch petrochemicals; German greenfield industrial capex softer than brownfield maintenance; Middle East suspensions.
  • Fragility. Utilities' capex depends on allowed returns and financing costs (German allowed-return review; Bund yields at 3.44%); co-location developers depend on AI-infrastructure credit, which is now widening; French demand depends on political clarity.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

C+629th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: on balance they help (European and German energy-transition policy, French nuclear and electrification policy), but the position is moderately fragile because French fiscal politics and German grid-return regulation can change quickly.

  • Helping. German infrastructure fund and readiness-based grid connections; German efficiency law for data centres; EU Electrification Action Plan and taxonomy (SPIE at 50% aligned; target above 50% to 2030); French energy programme and EUR 10bn-a-year electrification plan; EPR2; ETS2 carbon pricing on buildings; EU building-performance directive (slipping).
  • Hurting. French corporate tax surcharges and possible increases; French budget gridlock; possible German reductions in grid operators' allowed returns ("if grid operators face lower incentives they must cut internal costs aggressively", Jefferies); populist rollback risk (speculative).
  • Fragility. The German grid programme is legally and financially entrenched (security of supply, connection queues), so a reversal is unlikely; the French items are fragile and closer in time (2027 election).
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

F5527th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: the market is not a constraint; SPIE holds roughly 3% to 5% of a several-hundred-billion-euro European market, its fastest segments are growing high single digits, and the realistic mid-term organic growth it allows is 3% to 5%, with labour, not market size, the limit.

  • Size. Germany EUR 100bn to 120bn (SPIE about 3%); France roughly EUR 65bn to 70bn (implied by SPIE's EUR 3.35bn at about 5%); the Netherlands about EUR 35bn (SPIE about 6%); plus Belgium, Switzerland, Central Europe and global energy services. The order of magnitude is EUR 300bn to 400bn, against SPIE's EUR 10.4bn.
  • Growth. Core multi-technical services grow at roughly nominal GDP plus (about 3% to 4%); European grid spending is stepping up about 55% (EUR 500bn in 2025 to 2030 against EUR 320bn), i.e. high single-digit growth; European data-centre markets about 15% a year; nuclear life extension and EPR2 mid-to-high single digits (Morgan Stanley).
  • Implication. With about 40% of revenue in faster-growing segments, a blended organic rate of 3% to 5% is achievable; SPIE's own 3% to 4% target sits in the lower half because of its selectivity and its legacy French building and fibre exposure. Acquisitions add 3% to 5% a year with a vast pool of targets (over 1,000 in Switzerland alone; 91.5% of Germany is small firms).

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0660C-1063
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

C-6016th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: SPIE is at the trough of its organic cycle and turning up, early and on easy comparisons; total growth is already near the top of its range because of acquisitions.

  • Trough evidence. Organic growth fell from 4.3% (2024) to 2.0% (2025) to 1.2% (H1 2026); Q1 2026 was -0.9% (heavy snow; outdoor work is about one-third of revenue), Q2 2026 +3.1% with Germany +7.3% and Central Europe +7.3% (from -8.2%).
  • Turning forces. Weather catch-up (partly flatters Q2); French fibre drag fading; France expected to stabilise (Q3 about -0.5%, Q4 about flat); German high-voltage backlog converting; easy H2 comparison (H2 2025 organic about 1.6%, implied from FY 2.0% and H1 2.4%).
  • Countervailing. Global Services Energy -9.3% in Q2; the French fourth quarter is exposed to pre-election caution; Bernstein's earlier 4.0% Q2 forecast was missed (3.1%), so the recovery is real but not sharp.
  • Total growth. Revenue growth FY26 consensus +8.3% (acquired growth about 5% to 6%; Jefferies estimates acquisitions add about 6%), +7.2% FY27, +4.3% FY28. The FY28 figure implies brokers model no new acquisitions after 2026, so total growth falls back towards organic unless SPIE keeps buying.
  • Checkpoints. Q3 2026 sales on 30 Oct 2026 (Germany above 5%, France near -0.5%); FY26 results on 5 Mar 2027.
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

C+6310th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: tailwinds clearly dominate at value-chain and sector level over two to four years; headwinds dominate at country and market-regime level over the next 6 to 12 months. The picture is "fundamentals improving, multiple not following", which the Sector Digest describes as the setup where "the multiple does the work once the scarcity story is believed".

Theme by theme (the Sector's net force is B, MEDIUM, on 23-Oct-26):

  • Live grid (Big; the Sector's strongest theme). Strong tailwind: TSO and DSO capex step-up, scarce live-substation crews, a pricing window "into the early 2030s". Headwinds: lead times, labour, possible allowed-return cuts. SPIE is the cleanest test of the theme.
  • The data centre's power bill (Big, one wave across nine industry sectors). Moderate tailwind for SPIE (about 3% of revenue; deliberately selective). Risk: one hyperscaler pause or a financing squeeze hits all nine sectors at once.
  • Maintenance closing against extending (early). SPIE is mainly on the extending side (nuclear and grid life extension), but has exposure to closing assets in Dutch petrochemicals and, through ROFA, to German automotive-linked factory capex.
  • Law-dated renovation demand (EPBD, ETS2, German EnEfG). Slow tailwind; directives slip.
  • Building and construction cycle. Headwind in France (residential, tertiary pricing) and across the Eurozone (construction PMI 43.4).
  • Macro and country regime. Headwind: French sovereign and political stress, high yields, risk-off.

Overall: at value-chain level a strong tailwind (grid, electrification, nuclear, labour-scarcity pricing); at industry level a moderate tailwind (B, MEDIUM; SPIE named as a volume beneficiary); at sector level a moderate tailwind with a building-cycle drag; at country/market level a clear headwind that is acting on valuation. For the model: assume external forces deliver group organic growth recovering to 3% to 4% by 2027 to 2028 with Germany carrying it, and treat France as flat; do not assume any re-rating from the external picture inside six months.

Internal change forces/risks

D6219th of 36

Memo topic 2, Internal change forces: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Internally, SPIE is a well-run compounder refreshing its leadership without changing its formula, and leaning harder on acquisitions in Germany. The succession (Holzke, split chair, CFO continuity) is orderly and signals continuity; the targets have been raised in the conservative "floor-then-beat" style that has a clean record; standards are improving on margin, cash, safety and governance; and the balance sheet is being used to buy growth (about EUR 670m of annualised revenue in 2026) rather than to return cash. The two internal developments the Consumer must model with care are (1) ROFA, whose price and accretion claims do not reconcile with its stated margin and which adds cyclical industrial exposure, and (2) the below-EBITA leakage from the ORNANE conversion, the two new bonds, share-based dilution and tax, which will make EPS grow more slowly than EBITA in FY26 to FY27 unless the company buys back more stock than it has signalled.

2a

Internal risks

6 answers
Judgement60C-1460C-8645C-5974C-8168D-0355
Group judgement

Risks

A603rd of 35

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

SPIE's fundamental risks are moderate and diffuse; its sentiment risks are elevated and concentrated. The organic deceleration of the last twelve months comes from legacy and exogenous drags, not from the grid engine, and the trough appears to be passing. No single plausible fundamental shock removes more than about 10% of EBITA, thanks to the variable cost base, recurring revenue and self-funding; the tail worth modelling is a French political-fiscal shock combined with a project loss or a German industrial downturn hitting ROFA. Sentiment is the larger near-term danger (French sovereign stress, an AI-financing de-rating, peer warnings, a momentum regime and a crowded buy-side), but the shares have already de-rated to about 11.5x forward, so the remaining fall to a fair trough is about 15% to 20%. For the predictability work, the risk lies more in the timing and level of the multiple than in the earnings.

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

C6014th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: standards are improving on most measured dimensions; the one area of deterioration is execution in the new, project-heavy growth segments, where SPIE relies more on subcontractors.

  • Improving. EBITA margin +40 bps a year (2023 to 2025); cash conversion above 100% every year since 2015 except 2021 (108% in 2025); working capital -34 days; H1 2026 FCF turned positive (EUR 25.7m against -EUR 107.7m); total recordable accident frequency -40% (2019 to 2025); voluntary turnover 5.9% (6.6% in 2024); Fitch investment grade; Chairman/CEO split; fully independent, female-chaired committees; a 216-control internal framework with no material weaknesses reported by joint auditors PwC and EY; contract-signing thresholds requiring joint CEO-CFO approval on large bids.
  • Weaker. A Cologne data-centre project hit severe delivery setbacks from unfamiliar subcontractors (former SPIE project manager, July 2026); a Dutch high-voltage substation project ran up to six months late with 20% to 25% overruns because of personnel shortages and limited initial experience (October 2026 expert call, which nonetheless describes SPIE as "improving" in high-voltage). Central Europe's H1 margin fell 90 bps to 2.4%.
  • Governance gaps. No clawback or malus; executive ownership down to 0.4%; three-year LTIP vesting only.
C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

B+455th of 35

Judgement: the negatives are individually modest but concentrated below EBITA and in execution, which is exactly where the Consumer's EPS forecast is most exposed.

Net debt about EUR 800m higher than a year ago.
  • Share-based compensation. EUR 59.8m of IFRS 2 charges excluded from adjusted EBITA (about 7% of underlying EBIT per Deutsche Bank; executive LTIP EUR 20.7m). Bilfinger keeps these inside its adjusted EBITA.
  • Tax. Normative rate 30.2% in 2025 (German mix at 30.7%; French surcharge) with risk of further French increases.
  • Execution. Subcontractor-related setbacks in data centres and Dutch high-voltage work; Central Europe margin down; integration of the largest acquisition cohort since SAG during a CEO transition.
  • Legal and governance. A first-instance bribery conviction in April 2025 (EUR 1.5m fine each for two subsidiaries over Indonesian oil-and-gas conduct more than ten years old; under appeal); no clawback; executive ownership 0.4%.
  • Perimeter widening, which slightly lowers predictability.
C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

B+744th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system?

Judgement: highly congruent: goals (margin and cash), strategy (selective, decentralised, bolt-on), incentives (EBITA growth, cash conversion, relative TSR), ownership (employees the largest shareholder) and decisions (walking away from low-margin work) point the same way. Two gaps weaken the alignment at the top.

  • Congruence. Decentralised operating managers with local autonomy and central signing thresholds; LTIP weights 55% on three-year EBITA growth and cash conversion, 25% on TSR against the SBF 120 (zero below median), 15% on Scope 1 and 2 emissions, 5% on female managers; the CEO must hold 25% of vested shares, executive committee members 15%; employee share plan at a 20% discount with high take-up; voluntary turnover 5.9%; acquired managements retained but SPIE's negative-working-capital discipline imposed.
  • Gaps. No clawback or malus (about 60% of SBF 120 industrials have one); executive ownership only 0.4%; three-year vesting; adjusted EBITA excludes share-based pay, so the main incentive metric ignores the cost of the incentive.
  • Analogy. Closer to a Lifco-style decentralised compounder than to a centralised contractor, with less founder-level ownership.
C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

A681st of 35

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: no single plausible sudden risk exceeds about 10% of EBITA; the combination most worth modelling is a French political-fiscal shock coinciding with a project loss in the new high-voltage or data-centre work, a tail that could take 10% to 15% off EPS.

  • Project loss in new growth segments. Plausibility low to moderate (Dutch substation overruns of 20% to 25%; Cologne data centre). Size EUR 10m to 40m of EBITA (1% to 5%). Precedents: SPIE UK EUR 8m (2017); Caverion EUR 59m; Eltel EUR 55m. SPIE's avoidance of large fixed-price and hyperscaler EPC caps it.
  • French political or fiscal shock (sovereign crisis, election outcome, tax increase). Plausibility moderate in 2027. Size: a 5% fall in French revenue (about EUR 170m) at about 11% drop-through is about EUR 19m of EBITA (2.4%); a recurring or higher corporate surcharge could cost EUR 15m to 25m of net income (3% to 5% of EPS). Combined 5% to 8% of EPS.
  • German industrial downturn hitting ROFA. Plausibility moderate (automotive capex weak; Bilfinger's warning). Size: ROFA EBITA (about EUR 35m to 40m) halving costs about EUR 20m (2.5% of EBITA), plus a goodwill risk if the price was high.
  • German grid capex freeze or allowed-return cut. Plausibility low near term (security-of-supply imperatives, readiness-based queues). Size large (transmission and distribution is the growth engine).
  • Working-capital reversal. Plausibility low (needs negative organic growth). Size: about EUR 50m of cash per 5% revenue decline; EUR 975.5m of negative working capital is the stock at risk in a severe, multi-year contraction.
  • Wage spike with indexation lag. Plausibility low to moderate. Size 30 to 50 bps of margin for a year.
  • Legal or compliance. Bribery conviction under appeal (EUR 1.5m fines each); low size.
D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

C5511th of 35

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the drivers of the level of SPIE's earnings are diffuse (30,000 customers, about 1,000 branches, 70% to 75% maintenance, no customer above 10%), but the drivers of the change against 2019 to 2025 are concentrated in five items, two above EBITA (German grid conversion; the acquisition engine and its price) and three below it (financial cost, the French surcharge, the share count). The concentration is of two opposite kinds: the two operating drivers are the uncertain ones and carry the upside; the three below-EBITA drivers are the most certain items in the whole forecast (contractual coupons, already-issued shares, enacted tax) and are the reason my EPS sits below consensus. A forecast whose difference from history is concentrated in certain items is, paradoxically, a predictable forecast; the uncertainty is in whether consensus catches up, not in whether the items occur.

  1. The arithmetic of the difference, FY25 to FY27 (EBITA EUR 793.5m to about EUR 956m; adjusted EPS EUR 2.69 to about EUR 3.13 economic, about 3.23 company basis). EBITA growth of about EUR 163m: acquired contribution about EUR 75m to 80m (ROFA about EUR 36m, SGS about EUR 18m, BLOCK, Worley, Artemys, Invizo, nimeg, Clauser and the FY27 bolt-ons about EUR 20m); organic volume and price drop-through about EUR 60m (2.0% then 3.0% organic at 11% to 12%); mix, Central Europe's recovery and the fading fibre drag about EUR 25m. Below EBITA, against the FY25 structure: financial costs about -EUR 29m pre-tax (about -3% of EPS), the surcharge about -EUR 16m (about -3%), the share count +2.9% (about -3%). So roughly half of the EBITA change is acquired, a third is German-led organic drop-through, and the EPS change is three near-certain deductions of about 3% each against a 20% EBITA gain.
  2. Driver one: German transmission and distribution. German high-voltage and city-network work is over 30% of German revenue, so about 11% to 12% of the group; growing it at 8% to 10% a year contributes 1 pp of group organic growth, roughly a third of my 3% and most of the acceleration from 1.8%. The driver is demand-certain (TSO capex plans, RP5 allowed returns moving from 4.13% towards about 6.5%, legally entrenched connection queues) and timing-uncertain (transformer lead times of 2.5 to 4.5 years, infrastructure-fund disbursement running 20% below target in 2026, 60% of TenneT's Dutch projects 2.5 years late). The own-5 report's finding that bottlenecks "prevent a rapid peak" means the risk is a slower, longer curve, not a shorter one. Concentrated, but in the most durable end market SPIE has.
  3. Driver two: acquisitions and their price. The 2026 cohort (about EUR 670m of annualised revenue, about EUR 1.03bn of spend) and my assumed EUR 350m a year of bolt-ons supply about 55% of revenue growth to FY28. The revenue and EBITA from announced deals are near-certain; the return on capital is not (ROFA's implied multiple; German installer multiples at 7x to 9x against 6x to 7x historically; Brookfield's 15 German installers; Eiffage's four). This driver is concentrated in one decision-maker (capital allocation under a new CEO) and one number (the ROFA purchase price allocation).
  4. Driver three: French stabilisation. France (32% of revenue) moving from -1.6% (2025) to about -0.5%, +0.5% and +1.0% adds about 0.5 pp to group organic growth by 2028. It depends on fibre run-off arithmetic (near-certain) and on tertiary building and pre-election client behaviour (uncertain; Deutsche Bank models flat FY27 France, consensus +1%). Public-sector exposure is only 5% of group revenue (Jefferies), so the French fiscal squeeze bites through private clients and the surcharge, not public budgets.
  5. Drivers four to six, below EBITA: concentrated and certain. Financial cost: the 3.875% and 4.875% bonds, the term loan's 2027 refinancing at about 4% to 4.5% and rising lease interest are contractual; the own-3 report shows 86% of gross debt fixed, a 0.5% rate shift worth only EUR 3m, and the H1 2026 cost of net debt plus lease interest at EUR 52.9m, flat on H1 2025 because the new bonds had barely started; the full step lands in H2 2026 and FY27. My bottom-up run-rate for FY27 (bonds EUR 70m, term loan about EUR 21m, securitisation about EUR 9m, SGS funding about EUR 7m, interest income about -EUR 8m, leases about EUR 24m) is about EUR 123m to 127m on FactSet's definition, slightly above the second set's EUR 140m all-items figure only if the "other" line (pension interest, put-option discounting) is added; the risk to the financial line is therefore to the high side by perhaps EUR 5m to 10m, not to the low side. The surcharge: EUR 10.1m booked in H1 2026, extended in the draft 2027 budget, modelled permanently by two of the nine houses. Share count: 3,365,843 ORNANE shares issued 20 October 2026; about 1.75m to 1.85m shares to be created on 3 December 2026 at EUR 35.36; the anti-dilutive buyback at about EUR 41 cannot buy back as many as are issued at a 20% discount.
  6. Against 2019 to 2025. That period's differences were also concentrated but in different items: margin from selectivity, mix and inflation pricing (+260 bps), deleveraging from 3.3x, a 2% convertible and cash tax below P&L tax. All four have run their course; the new period swaps "margin plus deleveraging" for "German volume plus acquired volume minus financing cost". The Consumer should present the case as a change in the composition of growth, not a continuation.
  7. What could widen the set of drivers. ROFA's automotive exposure (about 30% of EUR 430m; a halving of ROFA's EBITA costs about 2% of group EBITA); Global Services Energy's Middle East suspensions (-9.3% organic in Q2 2026; about 4% of revenue, so immaterial to the group even at -10%); subcontractor leakage in data-centre and substation projects (20 to 40 bps in a bad case); a sudden demand shock, where the 2020 decremental margin of 27% would apply. None of these would change the EBITA path by more than about 5% in 18 months; together in a bad year, perhaps 8% to 10%.

Judgement: a large change in person but a small change in policy. The change signals continuity with a stronger German tilt and modernised governance; it introduces moderate key-person and integration risk during the busiest acquisition year since 2017, and it removes the last anchor shareholders.

  • Chief executive. Gauthier Louette (CEO since 2003, Chairman and CEO since 2010, statutory age limit) left at the 30 April 2026 AGM; Markus Holzke became CEO on 1 May 2026. Employee funds (8.0% of capital, 12.76% of votes) are the largest bloc; BlackRock holds 8.14%; no activist; no controlling holder. Louette previously rejected approaches from Eiffage and VINCI.

Executive and manager holdings fell to 0.4% of capital (peer average about 2%). The exit of anchor holders, with the shares at a low multiple, slightly raises the optionality of an approach from a larger French group, which is not a base case.
C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

F5227th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the strategy is unchanged in its essentials; one material choice has been added (a German industrial-services cluster around automation), and it is resourced by the balance sheet but articulated mainly as "commercial synergy", the least reliable kind.

  • Unchanged core. Multi-technical services for energy and communications, margin over volume, recurring maintenance, selective bidding (no very large hyperscaler or fixed-price EPC contracts), bolt-on acquisitions in fragmented markets, self-financing from negative working capital, Germany as the growth engine.

  • Insight for the Consumer. The cluster moves SPIE closer to Bilfinger's industrial terrain (Bernstein notes SPIE now "encroaches directly on Bilfinger's core market") and towards factory capex, which is more cyclical than grid maintenance. It improves group margin by mix (ROFA "high single-digit", SGS above 10%, versus 7.6%) but adds an industrial-cycle sensitivity that the grid narrative omits.

C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

C+5710th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: real price rises are being achieved in the scarce-skill segments and are evidenced by outcomes, not just claims; in maintenance they are mainly nominal pass-through; in French building they are being given back through volume.

  • Evidence of real pricing. Revenue per employee has grown faster than staff cost per employee and Eurozone wage inflation for five years (Deutsche Bank, September 2026); EBITA margin rose from about 6.3% (2022) to 7.6% (2025) through a period of 4% to 6% wage inflation; German high-voltage margins of 15% to 16% (Bernstein); expert evidence that industrial clients accepted 5% to 10% contract price rises in the Netherlands (Equans director, 2024) and that clients pay up because "the cost of project delay far outweighs" premium pricing (October 2026); Morgan Stanley says half of the organic growth target relies on pricing discipline and indexation.
  • Mechanism. Indexation in multi-year frameworks; two-to-three-month turnover on short maintenance jobs allows quick repricing; daily pricing tools on fuel and labour indices; selectivity.
  • Limits. Indexation lags can squeeze fixed-price work if inflation re-accelerates; in French tertiary building SPIE cannot price and sacrifices volume instead.
  • For the model. Price contributes about 1.5 to 2 pp of the 3% to 4% organic target; real price (above cost) contributes perhaps 10 to 20 bps of margin a year, concentrated in grid.
C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

B628th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: a clear step-up (about EUR 670m of annualised revenue announced in H1 2026 against a historical EUR 400m to 500m a year); strategically coherent with the German focus; discipline is proven on bolt-ons but unproven on ROFA, whose price does not reconcile with the stated multiple.

  • Scale. ROFA (third-largest deal since the 2015 IPO; EUR 611.8m for 89.7% in June 2026 plus EUR 71.0m for the remaining 10.3% on 10 July 2026), SGS (about EUR 180m revenue), BLOCK (EUR 42.4m for about EUR 50m revenue), Worley Power Services (EUR 34.3m for about EUR 70m revenue), Invizo, nimeg (EUR 10.4m), plus Artemys (about EUR 82m revenue, closed early 2026). FactSet shows EUR 765m of net assets acquired in H1 2026 (EUR 234m in FY25).
  • Record. Over 90 bolt-ons since 2013 adding EUR 6.4bn of revenue; Morgan Stanley puts the incremental return at about 27% over seven years; deals from 2019 to 2022 earned a 17% ROCE; integration typically frees about 1x EBITA of working capital within twelve months; Worksphere (EUR 222m, 2022) delivered at least EUR 9m of synergies and reached the group margin within two years. The counter-record: SAG diluted margins from 2015 to 2020 and leverage peaked at 3.3x in 2017.
  • The ROFA arithmetic. About EUR 683m for 100% of a business with about EUR 430m revenue at a "high single-digit" margin implies EBITA of roughly EUR 34m to 40m and an EV/EBITA of roughly 17x to 20x, not the "high single-digit multiple" Morgan Stanley cites (Morgan Stanley's own "EBITA +5%" also implies about EUR 40m). Either ROFA carries substantial net cash or prepayments, or the multiple is far above SPIE's 5x to 8x norm. A similar tension sits in the "mid-single-digit EPS accretion" claim: on about EUR 40m of EBITA, debt-funded at about 4%, after-tax accretion is nearer 2%.
  • Integration. Local management retained; Robur platform used for cross-selling; integration overlaps the CEO transition (Bernstein's main caution).

C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–54

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: several smaller positives add up to a better-quality, cheaper-financed and cleaner-reporting company.

  • Investment grade (Fitch BBB-, April 2026): lowers the cost of debt for future bolt-ons, enables sustainability-linked issuance and widens the investor base.
  • Removal of the ORNANE (October 2026): eliminates the non-cash fair-value swings that cut reported net income by EUR 132.9m in 2025 and makes reported EPS meaningful again, at a cost of about 2% dilution and about EUR 15m a year of extra interest (Deutsche Bank).
  • One SPIE cross-selling and in-house delivery in data centres (about 75% of value).
  • Digital and AI in facility management: Smart FM 360 at 10,114 active contracts in 2024 (+23%); sensor-based maintenance and AI field tools in pilot.
  • Talent engine: referral hiring, 5% apprentice share, employee ownership at about half of eligible staff.
  • A 2027 Capital Markets Day (flagged in the earnings review) to set a 2030 roadmap; given the record of setting floors and beating them, it is a plausible upgrade catalyst.
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

D5011th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: there are no controlling actors and none has signalled change; the shareholder base (employees, BlackRock, a fragmented float) favours continuity.

  • Employees (8.0% of capital, 12.76% of votes through double voting rights) are aligned with management's model. BlackRock (8.14%) is the only external holder above 5%. No activist is documented; short interest is low (no disclosed net short above the 0.5% threshold). Peugeot Invest and Bpifrance, the historical anchors, have left the board.
  • The bylaws carry no poison pill. In a low-multiple, sector-consolidating market (VINCI Energies at about EUR 21.6bn revenue; Equans at about EUR 19bn), the absence of an anchor holder is a latent change force, but no actor has indicated interest.
C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

D6023rd of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: moderately strong, broad and well resourced, but they are continuity improvements rather than a transformation; their EBITA effect is positive and fairly certain, while their EPS effect is partly offset by financing, dilution and tax.

  • Strength. The raised 2028 margin target, the acquisition step-up (about +6.5% of revenue annualised), investment grade and the ORNANE clean-up are real; together they underpin EBITA growth of about 10% in FY26 and high single digits after.
  • Breadth. Changes span strategy (German industrial cluster), operations (selectivity, One SPIE), governance (split roles), finance (rating, bonds) and people (talent engine).
  • Resourcing. Ample: FCF of about EUR 550m to 650m a year, liquidity of EUR 1,562m at end-June 2026, leverage headroom once seasonal cash returns.
  • Offsets. The negatives in C-86 fall below EBITA; ROFA's economics are unverified; the CEO is six months into the role.
C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

D4816th of 35

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: a reasonably well-founded staircase whose steps are mostly known rather than novel; the most novel step is the arrival of German public grid money in revenue from 2027, the most decision-relevant near-term steps are the Q3 sales and FY26 results, and the largest single event risk sits in the middle (the French presidential election, spring 2027).

Next 6 months (to April 2027).

  • Q3 2026 sales, 30 Oct 2026: France stabilising (about -0.5%), Germany above 5%, organic about 3%. Novelty low; well-founded (Bernstein's France-bottoming call; easy comparison).
  • SGS closing and first ROFA quarter; the ORNANE removed from the reported P&L. Novelty low; well-founded.
  • EPR2 final investment decision (expected late 2026): a multi-year French nuclear ramp. Novelty moderate; moderately founded.
  • FY26 results, 5 Mar 2027: margin about 7.8%, FCF above EUR 600m, leverage back below 2.0x, dividend about EUR 1.24, possibly 2027 outlook and an update of 2028 ambitions. Novelty low to moderate; well-founded.

Months 6 to 18 (April 2027 to April 2028).

  • German infrastructure-fund money reaching orders and revenue from 2027 (Morgan Stanley, Jefferies); German organic growth sustained above 5% to 7%. Novelty moderate-high (the first time public money, not only utilities, drives SPIE's German growth); founded on legislation and TSO plans, timing uncertain (tender processes).
  • French fibre drag ending and France returning to positive organic growth; novelty moderate; moderately founded, contingent on the election.
  • French presidential election (April to May 2027): a binary for the French risk premium. Not a positive step unless the outcome calms sovereign spreads.
  • H1 2027 results (July 2027): first evidence of ROFA and SGS accretion and cross-selling under Holzke. Novelty moderate; uncertain.
  • A 2027 Capital Markets Day with a 2030 roadmap (possible margin ambition above 8% and EBITA well above EUR 1bn). Novelty moderate; plausible given the floor-and-beat record.

Months 18 to 36 (2028 to 2029).

  • Delivery of the 2028 targets (8.0% margin, EBITA above EUR 1bn, cumulative FCF above EUR 2bn); peak German grid capex phase; EPR2 construction ramp towards 2030; continued bolt-ons. Novelty low (already targeted); well-founded on backlog and policy.

Missing step. A capital-return step (buybacks beyond dilution) would be novel and is not signalled.

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

C+6311th of 35

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

C+6311th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The industry structure is supportive and only moderately fragile, and SPIE sits in its most favourable part. Germany's extreme fragmentation and France's rational oligopoly both suppress price wars; labour scarcity and prequalification keep bidding disciplined in grid and nuclear work; competitors (VINCI Energies, Eiffage, a margin-repairing Equans) put margin first; customers are large, regulated or mission-critical and increasingly outsource. SPIE is the advantaged consolidator (negative working capital, prequalification, density, recruiting reach) rather than the largest player, and the precedents say such firms outperform operationally; its weak points against the template are low organic growth and a rising dependence on subcontractors in new project segments. The two links the Consumer should treat as fragile are the acquisition market (entry multiples rising as financial consolidators and French conglomerates compete in Germany) and the customer-financing link (German allowed returns; AI-infrastructure credit). Neither threatens the base business; both could slow the growth rate.

C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

A783rd of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: there are effectively no intermediaries; SPIE sells directly (about 90% of services) through close to 1,000 local branches and multi-year frameworks, which helps margin and data capture; the route is robust.

  • Direct model. Local branches provide proximity and technician utilisation; frameworks and maintenance contracts generate pull-through work (additional works above 50% of base contract value).
  • Potential intermediaries to watch. Integrated facility-management providers (CBRE, JLL, ISS) pushing into hard services; utilities shifting to EPCM models that keep engineering in-house (Ameren precedent in the US); equipment makers bundling installation; hyperscalers buying power equipment directly. None currently affects SPIE materially, and SPIE's avoidance of hyperscaler EPC limits exposure.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

C+589th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: competitor behaviour helps SPIE on price and hurts it on acquisition costs; SPIE's position is secure in grid, nuclear, technical facility management and German markets, and weakest in French building.

  • Helping. Equans' price discipline (Dutch contracts repriced, margins up 3% or more); VINCI Energies' selectivity; Bilfinger's absence from grid; Bravida and Instalco focused on Nordic markets.
  • Hurting. Eiffage and VINCI Energies acquiring in Germany (Salvia, Eqos); VINCI Energies' scale lets it pursue large grid and data-centre builds above EUR 100m that SPIE avoids (a 150 to 200 bps organic growth ceiling against VINCI Energies' 4% to 6% target, per the Leadership report's benchmark); French residential players bidding in tertiary.
  • Fragility. Low in the core: SPIE's best-in-class margin (7.6% against VINCI Energies 7.4%, Eiffage 6.2%, Bilfinger 5.5%, Equans about 4.2%) gives it room. Moderate in growth segments where it is a newer player (Dutch high-voltage).

C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

A602nd of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: disruption is acting most strongly on demand (positively), on labour supply (constraining) and on equipment suppliers (lengthening lead times); it leaves SPIE's position mostly strengthened, with fragility at the subcontractor and customer-financing links.

  • Demand (customers). Electrification, data-centre density and decarbonisation mandates raise volume and technical complexity: positive.
  • Competitors. Labour scarcity filters out sub-scale firms from complex work; consolidators bid up targets: mixed.
  • Regulators. Pushing grid and efficiency investment, but French fiscal stress: mostly positive.
  • Suppliers. Transformer and switchgear makers sold out for two to three years; copper above USD 14,000: delays revenue conversion, little margin effect given indexation and late purchasing.
  • Labour and subcontractors. The scarcest link; subcontractors may capture scarcity rents: the main fragility.
  • Customers' financiers. AI-infrastructure credit and utilities' cost of capital: an emerging fragility for data-centre and grid orders.

C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

D5823rd of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: SPIE is the largest listed pure-play European multi-technical services company and number one or two in most of its countries, but it is about half the size of the two conglomerate-owned leaders and its share in any one country is small; it is a scale leader in a market where scale is local.

  • Europe. VINCI Energies (about EUR 21.6bn revenue in 2025) and Equans/Bouygues (about EUR 19bn) are roughly twice SPIE's EUR 10.4bn; Eiffage Énergie Systèmes (EUR 8.1bn) is next; Bilfinger (EUR 5.4bn) and Assemblin Caverion (about EUR 3.7bn) are smaller. SPIE ranks in the top four in its five largest markets.
  • Germany (35% of revenue): about 3.0% share, marginally ahead of VINCI Energies (2.9%), then Equans (1.4%) and Eiffage (1.1%); 91.5% of the market sits with small local firms (Morgan Stanley).
  • France (32%): number four with about 5% share behind VINCI Energies, Equans and Eiffage; over 15,000 French customers.
  • Netherlands (about 15%): largest technical services provider after Worksphere (2022), about 6% share, SPIE's highest in any country.
  • Niches: leading independent in German high-voltage and live substations; a key EDF nuclear maintenance partner.
  • Distance to the top. In Germany it is already number one by a hair; in France the gap to number one is large and structural (VINCI Energies' French revenue is roughly 2.5 times SPIE France's), and SPIE is not trying to close it.
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

D3824th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: two very different structures. Germany is extremely fragmented (top five under 15%; top four 9%), which is SPIE's acquisition runway and a guard against price wars; France is concentrated (top five about 45%) and steep, which keeps pricing rational but caps share gains. The Netherlands sits between.

  • Germany. EUR 100bn to 120bn market; top five well under 30%; shares of the leaders are 1% to 3% each, so the distribution among the top ten is flat, not steep. Consolidation has been slow for decades (top four 9% today).
  • France. Top five about 45%; VINCI Energies (France is 40% of its EUR 21.6bn, about EUR 8.6bn), Eiffage Énergie Systèmes (largely French) and Equans are each well ahead of SPIE France (EUR 3.35bn, about 5%). The top five likely hold several times the share of numbers six to ten, i.e. steep.
  • Netherlands and Nordics. SPIE holds 6% in the Netherlands; the Nordic top three hold about 23%.
  • Implications. German fragmentation means competition is local and labour-constrained, not price-led by a dominant rival; French concentration means competitors are large, disciplined conglomerates whose behaviour sets the price, and the swing factor is the smaller residential contractors spilling into tertiary work.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

C6413th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: shares among the leaders are stable, moving only slowly through acquisitions; new entrants have taken little customer share but are now significant buyers of the targets SPIE needs.

  • Stability. Local, relationship-driven markets with 80% to 90% repeat business and over 90% maintenance retention change slowly. SPIE's share gains have been bought (Germany from 13% of group revenue in 2015 to 35% in 2025, mainly by acquisition; the German market share is still only about 3%). Expert procurement views see SPIE and VINCI Energies slightly gaining and Bouygues (Equans) slightly losing.
  • New entrants, last three to five years. Not greenfield operators (entry is blocked by prequalification and by a three-to-five-year training pipeline for grid crews) but financial consolidators: Brookfield (15 German installers since 2023), Nordic Climate Group, InstallatørGruppen (48 acquisitions since 2023, now entering Switzerland), Nimlas (over 140 companies), Instalco (via Fabri). Their customer share is small; their effect is on acquisition prices.
  • For the model. No share-loss assumption; acquired growth should assume rising entry multiples.
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

C6613th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: consolidation is real but slow, and it accrues to advantaged consolidators (those with recruiting reach, prequalification, density and cheap self-funding) more than to the largest; SPIE is among the most advantaged, not the largest.

  • The mechanism. Listed consolidators trade on 10x to 14x EV/EBITA and buy small private firms at 5x to 8x (sometimes about 3.5x in electrification niches); each accretive bolt-on adds about 10 bps a year to consolidated margin (Morgan Stanley). Labour scarcity pushes small owner-managed firms (often unable to grow beyond 20 to 30 employees) to sell.
  • Why slow. The Wisdom Library's caution applies: "fragmentation is usually structural, not a roll-up opportunity". After decades, Germany's top four hold only 9%; SPIE's 90-plus deals since 2013 have produced a 3% German share. Consolidation is a source of earnings growth, not of market power.
  • SPIE's advantages. Negative working capital (-34 days, versus positive days at Bilfinger and Bravida) funds deals without equity; the integration playbook frees about 1x EBITA of working capital; a high-voltage and nuclear prequalification base; employee ownership and referral hiring. VINCI Energies is larger but its bolt-on pace is steady (2% to 4% of revenue a year); Equans is restructuring; Bilfinger is in recovery after its warning.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

C6614th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the large competitors put margin first and are currently benign; prices fall with volume only at the small-contractor end and in building-related segments, not in grid or regulated niches.

  • Margin-first leaders. VINCI Energies (operating margin 7.4% to 7.8%, "highly selective bidding"); Eiffage Énergie Systèmes (6.2%, +40 bps); Equans (from 2.9% in 2023 to about 4.2% in 2025, targeting 5% by 2027, "license to buy bolt-ons" only for best divisions); Bilfinger (5.5% EBITA, targeting 8% to 9% by 2030). Morgan Stanley: all major competitors "focus on margin quality over raw contract volume".
  • Where prices fall with volume. French tertiary building (residential contractors bidding aggressively as housing fell); Nordic installation tenders (about 15 bidders against a historical 5; zero-margin bids from small firms; 8.5% of Nordic companies in financial distress per Alvarez and Marsal). In grid and specialised niches, scarcity of qualified crews prevents it.
  • Labour as a price floor. Persistent technician shortages "prevent irrational price wars" (Business Foundations); "pricing increasingly follows execution capacity" (expert, October 2026).
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

B668th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the attitude has become more stable over three years (the main destabiliser, Equans, is in margin-repair mode), and no large competitor has the motive for a shark attack; instability is confined to the small-firm tail and to the M&A market.

  • Change. In 2021 to 2023 Equans (then 2.9% margin, under new ownership) was the plausible price disruptor; it now prioritises margin and cash. VINCI Energies' bolt-on pace is "highly stable, non-accelerating". Bilfinger is absorbed by its own recovery.
  • Shark-attack risk. Low among leaders: their parents (VINCI, Bouygues, Eiffage) are judged on margin and cash and face their own French political exposure. The plausible trigger would be a deep European construction recession forcing building-focused contractors into tertiary and industrial maintenance tenders, which would hurt SPIE's French Building Solutions most (Morgan Stanley's bear case models price wars driving SPIE's margin to 5%, which looks extreme against the record).
  • Instability where it matters for SPIE. The M&A market: well-funded consolidators and VINCI/Eiffage chasing German targets could lift multiples quickly.
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

C6413th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: supportive, and only moderately fragile; the support rests on durable foundations (fragmentation, prequalification, labour scarcity, regulated and mission-critical demand) and its fragile parts (French building pricing, acquisition multiples) are small in profit terms.

  • Supportive features. Fragmented supply against large, regulated customers; rational conglomerate competitors; labour scarcity acting as a price floor; prequalification barriers in high-voltage and nuclear; an asset-light, negative-working-capital model that self-funds consolidation; switching costs on multi-year maintenance (over 90% retention).
  • Fragile features. French tertiary building (price-led tenders, residential spill-over); dependence on subcontractors who could capture scarcity margin; acquisition multiples bid up by financial consolidators; a regulatory dependence (German allowed returns; French budgets).
  • How fragile. Losing the French building support would cost volume, not much margin (SPIE already sacrifices volume there); losing cheap acquisitions would slow EPS growth by perhaps 2 to 3 pp a year but not damage the base. The grid support is the least fragile part.

C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

B+664th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: limited and slowly rising, concentrated in data-centre power and in prefabrication; there is no substitute for maintaining and upgrading existing energy and building assets.

  • Behind-the-meter generation (gas turbines, fuel cells, nuclear co-location) lets data centres bypass five-to-ten-year grid queues; FuelCell Energy says data centres are 97% of its pipeline. It shifts work from grid connection to on-site microgrids (which still need electrical engineering). Jefferies notes developers still strongly prefer grid connections.
  • Grid-enhancing technologies and storage could defer some substation reinforcement (Synapse); SPIE also installs storage (Tesla framework).
  • Prefabrication and modular systems reduce on-site labour; they also ease the labour ceiling.
  • In-house client teams are shrinking, not growing, because of engineer scarcity (a tailwind).
