Strong business SRCA and BTE; management; financial profile

C+6311th of 36

Memo topic 3, Value chain, structure, model, financials, management: the Rater's score

4a

Business quality

C+5912th of 358 answers
Judgement59C-4160C-4258C-4355C-4457C-4558C-4630C-4758
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

C+5912th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

SPIE's competitive advantage is a stack of moderate advantages that reinforce each other and are mostly widening: self-funding from negative working capital, prequalified scarce skills in grid and nuclear, local density, a recruiting and retention edge, sticky maintenance contracts and a proven acquisition engine. None is a fortress, but together they explain a durable margin premium (7.6% against 4% to 7.4% at the large peers) and cash conversion above 100% for a decade. Pricing power is demonstrable where skills are scarce and absent where buyers buy on price; SPIE captures rather than shares its scale benefits, so its advantage depends on scarcity persisting, which the training pipeline and grid demand make likely for several years. The market is not a constraint on growth; labour is. The one advantage that is narrowing at the margin is the acquisition arbitrage, as entry multiples rise.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

C6012th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: SPIE has no single strong moat; it has a stack of moderate, mutually reinforcing advantages, of which the funding advantage (negative working capital) and the prequalified scarce-skill base (high-voltage, nuclear) are the strongest and are widening, while the acquisition-arbitrage advantage is strong but narrowing at the margin. Barriers keep entrants out of its niches; advantages over existing rivals show in a persistent margin and cash premium.

  • Funding and cash (production/financial; strong; stable to widening). Working capital -34 days of revenue at end-2025 (EUR -975.5m; EUR -999.6m in 2024) against positive days at Bilfinger (net trade assets 8.3% of revenue), Bravida (+15 days) and a sector average of about +12 days; capex about 0.6% to 0.8% of revenue (Bilfinger 0.8%, Bravida 1.2%). Growth and acquisitions are self-funded. Caveat: FactSet's trade definition (receivables plus inventory less payables) shows positive 12% to 13% of revenue, so the negative position depends on contract liabilities, advances and tax and social payables; it is a structural billing advantage, but it reverses if revenue shrinks.
  • Prequalification and scarce skills (regulatory/knowledge; strong; widening). High-voltage live-substation work, nuclear (EDF contracts up to 17 years) and complex automation need safety and technical qualifications and crews that take three to five years to train; demand for them is rising faster than supply. Evidence: 15% to 16% German grid margins (Bernstein).
  • Local density (scale/production; moderate; widening in Germany). Close to 1,000 branches improve technician utilisation, response times and local purchasing; acquisitions densify the German network.
  • Recruiting and retention in a labour-scarce market (culture; moderate; widening relative to small firms). Employee ownership (8% of capital; about half of eligible staff), referral hiring (about 1,200 permanent hires in 2025), voluntary turnover 5.9%, record apprenticeships.
  • Customer lock-in (customer; moderate; stable). Multi-year maintenance and frameworks are two-thirds of sales; retention above 90%; switching mid-contract is costly. But tenders are price-led in commercial buildings.
  • Acquisition engine (capital allocation; strong but narrowing at the margin). 90-plus deals since 2013, cheap self-funding, a working-capital integration playbook; competition from consolidators and conglomerates is raising entry prices, and ROFA's price suggests SPIE itself is paying more for scale.
C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

C5812th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: SPIE is large in absolute terms (EUR 10.4bn revenue, about 55,000 staff, about 30,000 customers) but its scale confers advantage locally (density, recruiting, prequalified crews, cheap funding) more than nationally; it is half the size of VINCI Energies and Equans and twice Bilfinger.

  • Relative size. VINCI Energies about EUR 21.6bn; Equans about EUR 19bn; SPIE EUR 10.4bn; Eiffage Énergie Systèmes EUR 8.1bn; Bilfinger EUR 5.4bn; Assemblin Caverion about EUR 3.7bn; Bravida SEK 28.2bn (about EUR 2.5bn); Instalco SEK 13.6bn. SPIE has 18,843 staff in France and 17,984 in Germany.
  • What it confers. A stronger recruiting profile (training at scale; 2,650 German hires in 2025); purchasing leverage over local wholesalers; the ability to serve multi-site and national accounts; investment-grade funding for acquisitions; a balance sheet that clients prefer for multi-year frameworks; best-in-class labour efficiency on EBIT per employee (Business Foundations regression).
  • What it does not confer. National price-setting power (shares of 3% to 6%); immunity from local tender pricing; protection against labour scarcity (it is the binding constraint for everyone). Morgan Stanley and Bernstein both note scale advantages are "local, not national".

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

D5718th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: demonstrably not inferior in its core (maintenance, grid, nuclear, technical facility management), and arguably leading in German grid and complex multi-technical work; not superior where buyers choose on price (French tertiary building), and not yet proven in new high-voltage builds outside Germany.

  • Evidence against inferiority. Retention above 90% in maintenance; about 80% of customers recurring; Germany's Net Promoter Score 59.5; customers accepting 5% to 10% indexation to secure capacity; EDF's long nuclear contracts and new five-year framework; TenneT's five-substation award; commissioning partners describe SPIE as "a highly reliable technical partner" (expert call, October 2026); One SPIE delivers 75% of data-centre value in-house, reducing interface risk for clients; Smart FM energy-performance contracts deliver measurable savings (Generali -11% energy over four years).
  • Attempts to disprove that fail only partly. France: SPIE loses tenders on price by choice, which shows its utility is not high enough to command a premium in commoditised tertiary work. New-build high-voltage in the Netherlands: six-month delays and 20% to 25% overruns linked to inexperience (October 2026). Cologne data centre: setbacks from subcontractor reliance.
  • For the model. No share loss in core; selectivity-driven volume loss in French building continues.
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

C+589th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: yes, moderately and demonstrably, in the segments that matter for profit growth; the evidence is applied (margins rising through high wage inflation; revenue per employee outgrowing staff costs; indexation accepted; retention intact), not conjectural.

  • Applied evidence. EBITA margin 6.7% (2023) to 7.2% (2024) to 7.6% (2025) while wages rose 4% to 6% in core markets; revenue per employee outgrew staff costs and Eurozone wage inflation for five years (Deutsche Bank); German high-voltage margins of 15% to 16%; Equans' Dutch experience of 5% to 10% price rises accepted, and SPIE's Dutch segment margin +90 bps to 7.8% in H1 2026; retention stayed above 90% while prices rose.
  • Mechanism. Indexation clauses; short repricing cycles (two to three months for short maintenance jobs); daily pricing tools; selectivity (walking away rather than discounting).
  • Limits. Pricing power is bifurcated: absent in French tertiary building; constrained where indexation lags spikes on fixed-price work. Management's claims of having "priced services ahead of labour inflation" are corroborated by outcomes.
C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

D3021st of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: little; SPIE's strategy and culture are explicitly "margin over volume" and "selectivity", which capture scale benefits rather than share them. The partial exception is energy-performance contracting, where savings are shared with clients.

  • SPIE does not message lower prices from scale; it prices ahead of wage inflation, avoids low-margin hyperscaler work and declines low-margin French tenders. Scale benefits (purchasing, density, utilisation) flow to margin.
  • De facto sharing: in energy-efficiency and Smart FM performance contracts, clients receive part of the savings; in multi-site frameworks, clients gain coordination benefits.
  • Implication. This is not a Costco-type reinforcing loop; SPIE's moat does not widen through customer surplus. It relies instead on scarcity and switching costs, which are durable while labour is scarce but would weaken if capacity loosened.
C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

C+588th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: moderately; several mechanical forces lift SPIE's margin and growth each year without heroics (fibre drag running off, mix shifting to grid, bolt-on accretion, indexation, self-funding), worth perhaps 10 to 20 bps of margin and 1 to 2 pp of organic growth a year; offsetting mechanical drags sit below EBITA.

  • Positive gravity. (1) French fibre: a run-off business (about EUR 120m left in 2026) whose drag shrinks towards zero by 2027 to 2028. (2) Mix: Germany and grid grow faster than the group and carry higher margins (high-voltage 15% to 16%; ROFA high single digit; SGS above 10%). (3) Bolt-ons add about 10 bps of margin a year (Morgan Stanley). (4) Negative working capital turns growth into cash automatically. (5) Indexation and labour scarcity protect price. (6) Employee ownership compounds retention. (7) Regulated, multi-year customer capex plans (TSOs to 2030 to 2032) carry volume forward.
  • Negative gravity. Share-based dilution (about 1% to 1.5% a year before buybacks); rising financing costs as acquisitions are debt-funded; a tax rate drifting above 30%; acquisition multiples rising; wage inflation that must be repriced every year.
  • Against peers. Bravida and Instalco depend on a construction recovery (cyclical gravity); Bilfinger on chemical-plant utilisation (negative gravity); SPIE's gravitational forces are more structural.

Management is a disciplined, credible operator-allocator team in transition: the culture, incentives and processes are congruent and the record on margin, cash and deleveraging is strong, but organic growth is a persistent weakness by design and alignment at the top is thin. C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

D5024th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: ambition is solid but bounded: SPIE aims to be Europe's leading independent multi-technical consolidator and to exceed EUR 1bn of EBITA by 2028, but it deliberately caps growth through risk aversion; its ambition is about compounding quality, not outgrowing peers.

  • Stated ambition. 2028: EBITA above EUR 1bn, 8.0% margin, 7% to 9% total annual growth (3% to 4% organic), cumulative FCF above EUR 2bn; taxonomy-aligned revenue above 50% to 2030; independence (Louette rejected Eiffage and VINCI approaches).

SPIE's ambition sits between: higher margin than VINCI Energies, lower growth.
  • Shift under Holzke. The German industrial cluster (ROFA, SGS) and an acquisition pace about 50% above the historical run-rate signal somewhat higher ambition; he built Germany fivefold in eleven years.
  • The cost of the bound. The Leadership report's benchmark puts SPIE's ultra-conservative bidding at a structural growth ceiling about 150 to 200 bps below more volume-oriented peers.
C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

D6218th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: a smart, robust strategy that works across environments and widens the moat (density, prequalification, self-funding), but it is not bold on organic growth; its 2026 boldness (industrial automation) is the part most open to question.

  • Smart and robust. Maintenance-heavy mix (70% to 75%), selectivity, indexation, avoidance of large fixed-price and hyperscaler EPC contracts, self-funded bolt-ons in fragmented markets, rebalancing from France (48% of revenue in 2015) to Germany (35% in 2025), focus on scarce-skill segments (high-voltage, nuclear, data-centre co-location). It held up through COVID (cash conversion still strong) and the 2022 to 2024 inflation (margin up).
  • Moat-widening. Each German bolt-on densifies the network and adds recruits (SGS: 800 technicians and Eastern European pipelines); prequalified grid crews become scarcer and more valuable.
  • Growth as a priority. Secondary to margin by design; organic growth averaged 1.8% (2015 to 2025).
  • Dynamic. Capable of capitalising on grid and data-centre demand (Q2 2026 Germany +7.3%), on downturns (acquisitions get cheaper; working capital cushions), and on inflation (indexation).
  • Questionable element. ROFA's factory automation adds cyclical industrial capex and a price that needs verifying; cross-selling synergies are the hardest kind to deliver.

Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

C+5511th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

SPIE's economics are strong where it counts for a compounder (cash conversion above 100%, negative working capital, very high returns on tangible capital, reinvestment at above the cost of capital through acquisitions) and moderate elsewhere (post-tax ROCE including goodwill about 13% to 14%; EPS growth that trails EBITA growth; adjusted earnings flattered about 9% by excluding share-based pay). Margins lead the large peers and are still rising, but the pace is slowing towards the 10 to 20 bps a year the 2028 target implies; operating leverage is low, which narrows the cone of EBITA outcomes, and financial leverage is modest and largely fixed-rate. For the model: EBITA growth of about 10% in FY26 and 7% to 9% in FY27 to FY28 is well founded; EPS growth should be built bottom-up through rising net interest (debt-funded acquisitions, two new bonds), a tax rate of 30% to 31%, the ORNANE share issuance and continuing employee-plan dilution, which together put sustainable EPS growth nearer 7% to 9% than consensus's 15.6% and 11.2% for FY26 and FY27.

C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

D4521st of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: SPIE generates high single-digit to low double-digit EPS growth on mid-single-digit revenue growth, driven by margin and acquisitions; EBITA growth is durable at 8% to 10% a year, but EPS growth will run below EBITA growth in FY26 to FY27 because of financing, dilution and tax.

  • Delivered. FY25: revenue +4.8% (organic +2.0%, acquisitions +3.2%, currency -0.1%), EBITA +11.4%, adjusted net income +9.0%, adjusted EPS +7.7% (EUR 2.50 to 2.69). The gaps show the leakage: EBITA to net income (higher tax at 30.2%, interest) and net income to EPS (shares +1.2%). Over five years Deutsche Bank cites about 11% EPS CAGR (second-fastest in the peer group).

  • The line to challenge. Consensus EPS grows faster than EBIT in FY26 and FY27, which requires below-EBIT costs to fall as a share. Part is plausible (FY25 adjusted net income carried a EUR 15.8m exceptional French tax charge), but the opposing forces are larger: net debt about EUR 800m higher than a year earlier, a EUR 600m 3.875% bond (May 2026), a EUR 500m 4.875% bond and 3.36m new shares from the ORNANE (October 2026), a possibly recurring French surcharge. Deutsche Bank (EUR 2.76 FY26, 3.02 FY27, 3.29 FY28) sits 11% to 13% below the median, partly on definitions and partly on these items.

C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

This supports a fair multiple above today's 11.5x to 11.7x, provided acquisition returns hold.

  • Capacity. FCF about EUR 550m to 650m a year less dividends of about EUR 200m to 250m leaves EUR 350m to 450m a year for acquisitions before using leverage headroom (target below 2.0x). Morgan Stanley: without M&A, the reinvestment rate would fall to 5% to 10% because capex is so low.

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

C4512th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: clean in cash terms (FCF exceeds adjusted net income; no capitalised-cost games; conservative receivables policy) but flattered in accounting terms by the exclusion of share-based pay, which on its own lifts adjusted EPS by about 9%; reported earnings have been noisy but the main noise source (the ORNANE) has now gone.

  • The gap. FY25 adjusted EPS EUR 2.69 against reported basic EUR 1.05. The bridge: the ORNANE fair-value charge (EUR 132.9m, non-cash), amortisation of acquired intangibles (EUR 58m in H1 2025, EUR 46m in H1 2026), IFRS 2 share-based charges (EUR 59.8m), the French exceptional tax contribution (EUR 15.8m), restructuring.
  • Flattering items. Excluding EUR 59.8m of share-based pay (about EUR 42m after tax) adds about 9% to adjusted net income; Bilfinger includes it. Share-based pay also dilutes the share count (about 1.5% a year before buybacks).
  • Not flattering. Capitalised costs are minimal (capex below 1% of revenue); receivables over 12 months overdue are written off in full and those 6 to 12 months overdue by half; losses at completion are provided immediately; joint auditors with no material weaknesses. Percentage-of-completion judgement is the Key Audit Matter.
  • Tax and leakage. Normative tax 30.2% (2025), reported 38.6%; Germany's mix (30.7% rate) pushes it up as Germany grows; a French surcharge may recur. Minorities are small (EUR 19.6m). Pensions of EUR 582m carry a modest interest cost.
  • Post-ORNANE. From Q4 2026 reported EPS loses the fair-value swings; the cost is 3.36m shares and a higher coupon.

C-45To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?C+589th of 35full answer under 4a Business quality
P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

A543rd of 35

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: price will contribute about 1.5 to 2 pp a year to revenue (about half of the 3% organic growth) and only about 5 to 10 bps a year to margin. SPIE's pricing power is mostly pass-through power (indexation, short repricing cycles, scarcity that lets it walk away), with genuine above-cost pricing confined to German high-voltage grid, nuclear and complex co-location work. The important change from 2022 to 2024 is that wage inflation is normalising, so price will do less of the organic work: the 3% to 4% organic target now needs about 1.5 to 2 pp of real volume a year, which SPIE has rarely delivered outside Germany.

  1. The evidence that pricing power is applied, not claimed. Revenue per employee outgrew staff cost per employee and Eurozone wage inflation over 2020 to 2025 (Deutsche Bank, 1 Sep 2026); EBITA margin rose from about 5% (2020) to 7.6% (2025) through wage inflation of 4% to 6%; 2023 organic growth of 8.4% was about two-thirds price (Morgan Stanley) and came with +40 bps of margin; German high-voltage margins of 15% to 16% (Bernstein); retention above 90% while prices rose; an Equans director cited Dutch clients accepting 5% to 10% rises (September 2024); North-Western Europe's margin rose 90 bps to 7.8% in H1 2026.
  2. The mechanisms and their lags. Short jobs reprice every two to three months (Jefferies); multi-year frameworks carry indexation formulas on labour and fuel; materials are bought after contract award and passed through; daily indexed pricing tools. The lag is collective-agreement timing (some countries' wage settlements land before the indexation date), which can cost 30 to 50 bps for a year if wages spike. I disagree with the AlphaSense pricing report's statement that "all" contracts carry full indexation: fixed-price project work exists (data-centre and substation builds, French building) and is where the Cologne and Dutch high-voltage setbacks occurred.
  3. Price contribution to revenue, by year. Wage inflation is normalising: Germany about 2.5% to 3%, France about 1.5%, the Netherlands 6% to 7% in a catch-up (Morgan Stanley, March 2025). Weighted price/indexation of about 2% in FY26, 1.5% to 2% in FY27 to FY28, about 1.5% thereafter: about EUR 170m to 200m of revenue a year. Volume therefore has to supply 1% to 1.5% of my 3.0% to 3.3% organic assumption. Germany (transmission and distribution volume, data centres) can do that; France (building selectivity, fibre run-off) cannot until 2027 to 2028.
  4. Price contribution to margin. Real price (above cost) adds perhaps 5 to 10 bps a year at group level, concentrated in grid, nuclear and co-location: German grid work is perhaps 10% to 12% of group revenue; if its price runs 1 to 2 pp above cost, that is 10 to 20 bps of segment margin and about 2 to 4 bps of group margin a year, plus smaller contributions elsewhere. In maintenance and technical facility management price is pass-through (margin neutral). In French tertiary building SPIE gives volume, not price (France organic -1.6% in 2025, -0.7% in H1 2026, -1.9% in Q2 2026) and holds margin (7.1% in FY25; 6.2% in H1 2026, +10 bps).
  5. Durability across conditions. - German transmission and distribution upcycle: durable into the early 2030s (crews take three to five years to train; TSO budgets to 2030 to 2032; eight-year TenneT bundled contract). Threats: equipment lead times (128 to 160 weeks) delay revenue, not price; a cut in grid operators' allowed returns (fourth period 4.13% to 5.79%; fifth period proposed 6.5%, E.ON lobbying for 8%) would be pushed down to contractors. - French pre-election period: price holds because SPIE walks away; volume is the casualty. - A recession with negative organic growth: precedents (2009 flat production; 2020 -4.8% revenue with margin troughing at 5.1%) show price held in maintenance and fell in new-build work; Morgan Stanley's bear case of a 5% margin assumes price wars that the 2020 record does not show. - Data-centre co-location under tighter financing: SPIE avoids hyperscaler packages; risk is delay, not price.
  6. Peers. Bravida lacks automatic indexation on many Swedish service contracts; Instalco has 57% cost-plus or partnering contracts (protection without a spread); Equans repriced Dutch contracts and lifted its margin to about 4.4% in 2025 from 2.9% in 2023; VINCI Energies (7.4% to 7.8%) is the only large peer with comparable discipline. SPIE's pricing is better than the Nordic installers', similar to VINCI Energies'.
  7. The monitorable. The spread between revenue per employee growth and staff cost per employee growth (Deutsche Bank's metric) at the FY26 results on 5 March 2027. A negative spread would be the first sign that pass-through is failing.
5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7555P-0153
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

C-5514th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: low to moderate; the cost base is about 80% to 85% variable, the contribution margin on extra revenue is about 11% to 12% against a 7.6% average, so a 1% change in revenue moves EBITA by about 1.5%; the range of outcomes from volume alone is narrow, and margin risk comes more from pricing and execution than from volume.

  • Cost structure (FY25). Operating expenses EUR 9,892m: purchases EUR 1,416m (14%), external services EUR 4,296m (43%, including about EUR 1.8bn of subcontracting), employment EUR 3,678m (37%; salaries 38% of opex per Jefferies), with subcontracting and temporary labour above 56% of total expenses (registration document). Fixed costs (branch and regional overhead, central functions) are roughly 4% of revenue (about EUR 400m).
  • Observed drop-through. FY24: +EUR 128m EBITA on +EUR 1,192m revenue (10.7%); FY25: +EUR 81m on +EUR 479m (17%, helped by mix and acquisitions); H1 2026: +EUR 21m on +EUR 178m (11.7%; Deutsche Bank 11.9%).
  • Sensitivities. A 2 pp volume shortfall cuts adjusted EPS about 2.9%; 50 bps of margin moves EPS about 7.4% (Change Forces report, calculated). A ±5% revenue swing moves EBITA by roughly ±7% to 8% (about ±EUR 55m to 60m; margin ±25 to 30 bps).
  • Peer read. Bravida (about 90% variable) grew organically 9% in Q2 2026 with a flat 5.9% margin: volume does not automatically raise margins in labour-intensive installers. SPIE's selectivity and mix give it better drop-through. Labour flexibility (subcontractors) cushions downturns, which is why Morgan Stanley's bear case of a 5% margin looks extreme.
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

C-5317th of 35

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: SPIE carries contribution-margin leverage, not fixed-cost leverage. Extra organic revenue drops through at about 11% to 12% against a 7.8% average margin, so 3% organic growth on its own adds about 11 bps of EBITA margin a year; that mechanism alone delivers most of the path to the 8.0% target for 2028. The leverage is asymmetric: in a sudden shock it ran at about 27% on the way down (2020), against about 11% on the way up. Over FY26 to FY30 it shapes the line items into a slower, steadier version of 2020 to 2025: EBITA growth of 8% to 10% a year instead of about 11% to 14%, margin gains of about 10 to 12 bps a year instead of about 40 to 50 bps.

  1. What the cost base actually is. FY25 operating costs of about EUR 9.9bn split into purchases EUR 1,416m (14%), external services EUR 4,296m (43%, including about EUR 1.8bn of subcontracting) and employment EUR 3,678m (37%). I disagree with the AlphaSense operating-leverage report's reading that 41% of costs are "fixed": that figure treats all own-staff cost as fixed. In practice there are three layers. (a) Truly fixed costs (branch, regional and central overhead) of roughly 4% to 5% of revenue, about EUR 450m to 500m; the former CFO put headquarters and management at about 10% of costs on a wider definition. (b) Own labour (37%), semi-variable over six to twelve months through about 13% staff turnover, overtime and temporary staff, but deliberately hoarded in a shortage because skilled crews take three to five years to rebuild. (c) Purchases and subcontracting (about 58%), variable within weeks to months.
  2. Measured drop-through, up and down. Up: FY24 +EUR 128m EBITA on +EUR 1,192m revenue (10.7%); FY25 +EUR 81m on +EUR 479m (17%, flattered by mix and acquisitions); H1 2026 +EUR 21m on +EUR 179m (11.7%; Deutsche Bank 11.9% against analysts' 9% to 10.5%). Down: 2019 to 2020 production fell from EUR 6,927m to EUR 6,642m and EBITA from EUR 416m to EUR 339m, a decremental margin of 27% (EUR 77m on EUR 285m). The asymmetry is the key fact: when volume falls suddenly, own staff are kept and the loss drops through at two to three times the upward rate; in a gradual slowdown subcontractors are released first and decremental margins should sit near 15% to 20%.
  3. The arithmetic that matters for the target. Margin change from organic volume is about (incremental margin minus average margin) times organic growth: (11.5% - 7.8%) x 3% = about 11 bps a year. At 1.5% organic it falls to about 5 bps; at 4% it rises to about 15 bps. Between FY25 (7.64%) and FY28 the 8.0% target needs about 36 bps, or 12 bps a year. Organic drop-through at 3% supplies about 33 bps of that; acquired mix (ROFA "high single-digit", SGS slightly above 10%) a few bps; Central Europe's normalisation and fading French fibre drag the rest. The 8.0% target is therefore a near-mechanical outcome of 3% organic growth; at the 1.8% historical average it would be missed by about 15 bps. This links the margin case directly to the organic case, which is SPIE's weakest record (P-06).
  4. Within-year leverage and a mix trap in 2027. H1 margins run about 3 points below H2 (6.0% against 9.1% in 2025) because fixed costs are spread over seasonally lower outdoor activity. ROFA and SGS earn their margins more evenly across the year. Consolidating them therefore lifts H1 margins and slightly dilutes H2. I expect H1 2027 EBITA margin of about 6.5% (+30 bps) and H2 2027 of about 9.2% (roughly flat). The Consumer should not read the H1 2027 step as an acceleration of underlying operating leverage, nor a flat H2 2027 as a stall.
  5. How it shapes the line items, against 2019 to 2025.
Line 2019 to 2025 (history) FY25 to FY30 (my base case)
Revenue growth 6.8% a year (FactSet, acquisition-heavy) about 7.3% a year (organic about 3%, acquired about 4%)
EBITA margin 6.0% (2019), 5.1% (2020), 6.7% (2023), 7.2% (2024), 7.6% (2025) 7.84%, 7.93%, 8.05%, 8.12%, 8.18%
EBITA growth about 11% a year (EUR 416m to 793.5m) about 8.7% a year (to about EUR 1,206m)
EBITDA margin (brokers' basis) 10.4% (2025) 10.6%, 10.7%, 10.85%, 10.9%, 11.0%
D&A excluding PPA amortisation 2.1% (2023), 2.4% (2024), 2.7% (2025) of revenue 2.75% to 2.8% of revenue (leases rising with acquisitions)
  1. Why the next four years gain less margin than the last five. About half of the 2020 to 2025 gain was not repeatable: recovery from the 2020 trough (5.1%), the 2022 UK exit, Worksphere synergies, and the 2022 to 2023 inflation in which pricing drove about two-thirds of 8.4% organic growth (Morgan Stanley) and indexed contracts expanded margin. The remaining engine (selectivity, grid mix, drop-through) is real but slower.
  2. Peers. Bravida grew organically 9% in Q2 2026 with a flat 5.9% margin (labour-intensive installers do not convert volume into margin automatically); Bilfinger's 2026 warning shows the downside version (engineers kept on the payroll while customers deferred call-offs, guidance cut to 4.6% to 5.0%). SPIE's better drop-through comes from selectivity and mix, not a lower cost base. Fasadgruppen's Q1 2026 (-10.8% organic, -0.7% EBITA margin) shows how fast a weather-hit quarter turns negative when crews are retained, and how fast it reverses (Q2).
  3. Cone and skew. From volume alone the cone is narrow: a 2 pp shortfall in organic growth costs about EUR 25m of EBITA in a gradual slowdown (3%) and EUR 45m to 60m in a sudden one (6% to 7%). The skew over 12 months is slightly down (French pre-election quarter, Middle East, German industrial clients of ROFA), over 24 to 48 months slightly up (German public grid money from 2027).

Financial leverage

C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?C-5817th of 35full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

F4029th of 35

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: financial leverage is modest and safe, but it is moving the wrong way for EPS in FY26 to FY27. Net financial items rise from about EUR 111m (FY25) to about EUR 127m (FY26) and EUR 140m (FY27), or from 14.0% to 14.6% of EBITA, before falling to about 10% by FY30 as cash pays debt down. Leverage peaks at about 1.75x at end-2026 (company basis) and falls to about 1.5x (2027), 1.25x (2028) and about 1.0x (2029) even with EUR 350m a year of bolt-ons. The balance sheet is not a risk; the coupon step-up is a 2% to 3% drag on FY27 EPS that consensus has not absorbed.

  1. What changed in 2026. (a) Net debt rose about EUR 820m in six months to EUR 1,967.1m at end-June 2026 (2.1x), mostly ROFA (EUR 611.8m for 89.7% in June, plus EUR 71.0m for 10.3% in July), BLOCK, Worley, Artemys. (b) A EUR 600m 3.875% sustainability-linked bond due 2031 (May 2026). (c) The EUR 400m 2% ORNANE due 2028 was redeemed early on 20 October 2026: par was refinanced by a EUR 500m 4.875% sustainability-linked bond due March 2032 (yield 4.95%), and the conversion value above par was settled in 3,365,843 new shares. (d) Lease interest is rising (EUR 9.9m in H1 2026 against EUR 6.5m in H1 2025). (e) A EUR 600m term loan matures in October 2027, half swapped; refinancing it in the bond market at about 4% to 4.5% adds a few million a year from FY28.
  2. My path for net financial items (all financial items on the adjusted basis: cost of net debt, lease interest, pension interest, other; excluding ORNANE fair-value effects): FY25 about -111 (FactSet), FY26 -127, FY27 -140, FY28 -138, FY29 -132, FY30 -125. Components in FY27: cost of net debt about EUR 80m on average net debt of about EUR 1.85bn (seasonal peak about EUR 2.05bn in June) at about 4.3%; lease interest about EUR 23m; pension interest about EUR 22m (EUR 582m obligation); other (discounting of minority put options of EUR 215.1m and earn-outs, fees, currency) about EUR 15m to 18m. Deutsche Bank's path (FY26 +EUR 10m year on year; FY27 flat plus about EUR 15m from the refinancing) is close to mine; most other brokers' figures pre-date the 20 October refinancing.
  3. Net debt path (company basis, excluding IFRS 16), with EUR 350m of acquisitions a year from FY27: end-2026 about EUR 1,740m (about 1.75x; about 1.7x pro forma for a full year of ROFA and SGS), end-2027 about EUR 1,650m (1.5x), end-2028 about EUR 1,490m (1.25x), end-2029 about EUR 1,290m (1.0x), end-2030 about EUR 1,050m (0.8x). I disagree slightly with the first set's year-end 2026 estimate of EUR 1.6bn to 1.7bn: the H2 cash calls (ROFA's remaining EUR 71m, SGS at about EUR 150m to 180m, the EUR 0.32 interim dividend of about EUR 54m) absorb about EUR 300m of the seasonal inflow. Including leases (about EUR 0.7bn) and pensions (EUR 0.58bn) the FactSet-style figure is about EUR 3.0bn at end-2026.
  4. What leverage does to EPS. Amplification is small: EBITA/pre-tax profit is about 1.17x, so 1% on EBITA is about 1.2% on EPS. Each EUR 10m of financial cost is about EUR 0.04 of EPS (1.2%). The debt is mostly fixed: a 100 bp move in rates on the floating or refinancing portion (about EUR 1bn by 2028) moves EPS by about 1.3% (Deutsche Bank: 110 to 120 bps of FY28 accretion per 100 bps of debt cost). Sustainability-linked step-ups (a premium if Scope 1 and 2 emissions are not cut 50% by end-2030) are a small tail.
  5. The ORNANE trade-off, quantified. SPIE swapped EUR 400m of 2% debt and a volatile derivative for EUR 500m of 4.875% debt plus 3.37m shares (2.0% of the capital). Pre-tax interest rises about EUR 13m a year from FY27 (about 1.6% of EPS) and the share count about 2%: together about 3.5% to 4% of FY27 EPS, matching Deutsche Bank's 4% cut. In return reported earnings lose the fair-value swings (EUR 132.9m charge in FY25, EUR 15.9m in H1 2026). This is a cleaner P&L bought with EPS; the market will see the cost before the benefit.
  6. Balance sheet over four years. Equity rises from about EUR 2.1bn to about EUR 3.6bn to 3.8bn by 2030 (retained reported earnings after the 40% payout, IFRS 2 credits, the ORNANE conversion adding about EUR 140m in 2026, employee issuance); the 40% payout, buybacks limited to offsetting dilution and EUR 350m of bolt-ons leave leverage falling to about 1x by 2029. This is a latent capital-return lever: at about 1x leverage, EUR 1bn of headroom to 2.0x appears by 2029, about 10% of today's market value. My base case does not use it (management has not signalled it); it is upside optionality worth 1% to 2% of EPS a year from 2028 if spent on buybacks or bolt-ons at sensible prices.
  7. Peers. Instalco (3.3x net debt/EBITDA in 2025, acquisitions slowed, minorities absorbing about 9% of net profit) and Fasadgruppen (leverage 3.25x, SEK 504m rights issue, dividend cancelled) are the precedents of acquisition-funded leverage turning into dilution. SPIE is at the opposite end: investment grade (Fitch BBB-), negative working capital, leverage falling. Its risk is the coupon, not the balance sheet.
  8. Where I differ from the first set. The first set described financial leverage as amplifying EPS "only slightly" (score 62). That is right for gearing, but it understated the near-term direction: rising interest is one of three below-EBITA drags that together reverse consensus's expected improvement in EBITA-to-net-income conversion (see P-08).

Cost-out scope NTM

2 answers
C-7740P-0342
C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

D4019th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: much of the cost base flexes with activity (subcontractors, temporary staff, purchases), which protects margins in a downturn; little is cuttable as a permanent saving, and SPIE does not need or plan a cost programme. Structural savings would come from procurement, digital tools and back-office integration of acquisitions, worth perhaps 20 to 40 bps over three to four years.

  • Purchases (14%): project-linked and largely passed through; procurement scale and central databases give small savings.
  • External services and subcontracting (43%): fully variable within months; reducible in a downturn; in growth, replacing subcontractors with own staff would improve quality and margin but requires hiring.
  • Employment (37%): semi-variable; overall staff turnover of about 13% a year (voluntary 5.9%) gives natural flexibility; overtime and temporary staff adjust quickly; core skilled staff should not be cut given scarcity.
  • Overheads: lean by design (decentralised branches, small holding); acquisition integration (Worksphere: at least EUR 9m of synergies) and back-office consolidation are the levers; ROFA's synergies are mainly commercial.
  • Precedent. SPIE's margin gains came from selectivity and mix, not cost programmes; exits (UK 2022, Belgian IT 2024) removed low-return units.
P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

D4224th of 35

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: little, and that is by design. SPIE has no cost programme, the former CFO says it has no cost-cutting culture, and its overhead is lean. Structural efficiency of about 15 to 25 bps of EBITA margin (EUR 20m to 30m by FY28) is plausible over three to four years, mostly from integrating the 2026 acquisitions, normalising Central Europe and back-office consolidation; almost all of it is already inside the company's 8.0% target and inside my margin path. The real value of the cost base is defensive: in a downturn subcontractors and temporary staff can be cut within months, which caps the damage, but only after the first quarter or two.

  1. Where efficiency can come from, sized and timed. - Integration of ROFA, SGS, BLOCK and Worley (2027 to 2028): management describes synergies as "primarily commercial" with "limited cost synergies". Back-office integration, purchasing and working-capital discipline: perhaps EUR 5m to 10m by 2028. The Worksphere precedent (at least EUR 9m within 18 months on about EUR 222m revenue, by merging offices and organisations into SPIE Nederland) is the upper bound for a business folded into an existing country platform; ROFA is a stand-alone platform, so expect less. - Central Europe (2027): EBITA margin fell 90 bps to 2.4% in H1 2026 on the weather-hit Q1, after 6.0% in FY25 (5.2% in FY24). Returning to about 6% by FY27 to FY28 on a segment of about EUR 1.0bn to 1.2bn is worth about EUR 10m to 15m, but this is utilisation and execution recovery, not cost elimination. - Procurement, digital tools and shared services (2027 to 2030): central purchasing databases, Smart FM 360 and AI field tools; perhaps 5 to 10 bps over four years. - Bolt-on accretion: Morgan Stanley's "about 10 bps a year" mostly comes from working-capital release (about 1x EBITA within a year, a cash effect) and margin uplift through selectivity; I count at most 3 to 5 bps a year of it as margin.
  2. What is not cuttable. Core skilled technicians (cutting them in a shortage destroys the capacity that supports price; Bilfinger is now cutting 1,500 positions under Program Agile, which shows the cost of having to do so); central overhead, which is already small (decentralised, about 1,000 branches).
  3. Downturn flexibility, the more important use. Subcontracting and temporary labour exceed 56% of total expenses (2025 registration document); they flex within weeks. Own staff flex through turnover of about 13% a year. In 2020 the first-shock decremental margin was 27%, then cost containment worked: EBITA fell to EUR 339m (5.1%) but cash held. For the model: in a gradual slowdown, assume decremental margins of 15% to 20% after one to two quarters.
  4. Why it matters for the third set. Because there is no cost lever of size, margin delivery depends on volume, mix and price (P-01, P-04). The forecast cannot be rescued by a cost programme if organic growth disappoints, but nor does it depend on one. Predictability is therefore tied to organic growth, which is the variable to test.
  5. Where I differ from the first set. The first set put structural savings at 20 to 40 bps over three to four years. I put the cost-elimination part at 15 to 25 bps and treat Central Europe's recovery as utilisation rather than cost. The difference is small, but it moves the margin case further onto organic volume.
5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4758P-0563
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?C+588th of 35full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

A632nd of 35

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

The Investment Analyst's heading After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: SPIE's operating loops are reinforcing and make revenue, EBITA and cash self-sustaining at about 7% total growth with a narrow cone; the balancing loops act on two places only: the speed of organic volume (labour and equipment caps) and the return on reinvested cash (rising acquisition multiples). The net shape is "EBITA compounds, the return on each new euro falls". The one loop that could be reinforcing and is switched off is the reflexive valuation loop: at 11.5x to 11.7x earnings, buying back stock would earn more than a ROFA-priced acquisition, but management buys back only to offset dilution.

  1. The self-funding acquisition loop (reinforcing, weakening gain). Negative working capital (EUR -975.5m, -34 days, end-2025) plus cash conversion above 100% fund bolt-ons without equity; acquired working capital is optimised by about 1x EBITA within a year (Bernstein), which lowers the effective price; acquired EBITA adds to the cash that funds the next deal. Evidence now: EUR 765m of net assets acquired in H1 2026 (EUR 234m in FY25) while leverage stays near 2x. The gain of the loop is falling: at 5x to 8x EBITA each EUR 350m bought EUR 45m to 70m of EBITA; at the 8x to 9x now being paid for German installers (former CFO: multiples have "crept up toward 8x"; Eiffage, VINCI Energies with 23 deals in nine months of 2026, Brookfield, Instalco via Fabri) it buys about EUR 38m to 44m; at ROFA's implied multiple it buys about EUR 18m to 20m. For the model: each EUR 350m of acquisitions adds about EUR 480m of revenue and about EUR 38m of EBITA (about 9x), and about 1% to 1.5% to EPS after funding cost; I have used exactly this.
  2. ROFA as the test of the loop. EUR 682.8m for 100% of about EUR 430m revenue at a "high single-digit" EBITA margin (about EUR 34m to 40m) is 17x to 20x EBITA; after a 1x working-capital release, about 16x to 19x. Debt-funded at about 4.2% pre-tax, the first-year EPS accretion before PPA amortisation is about 1% (EUR 36m EBITA less about EUR 29m interest, after tax), not the "mid-single-digit" Morgan Stanley cited (2 March 2026). The post-tax return on capital is about 3.5% to 4% at entry, against a cost of capital near 8%. Either ROFA carried substantial net cash (to be disclosed with the purchase price allocation at the FY26 results) or cross-selling must lift its EBITA by about 50% to earn its cost of capital. This is the first time the loop has been run at a price that does not self-fund on its own numbers.
  3. The labour loop (reinforcing on price, balancing on volume). Scarcity (615 vacancies per 100 unemployed electrical engineers in Germany) supports price and margin, which fund employee ownership (8.0% of capital; 51% participation in 2025), referral hiring (1,243 hires in 2025) and apprentices, which lower turnover (5.9% voluntary) and protect capacity. The same scarcity caps volume: organic growth stays near 3% even with a two-to-three-year grid backlog. Where it can flip: if subcontractor costs rise faster than indexation in new project segments (Cologne data centre; Dutch high-voltage substations up to six months late with 20% to 25% overruns); MYR Group's 290 bp margin loss from subcontracted productivity is the precedent. For the line items: margins up slowly, organic revenue capped at 3% to 4%; downside of 20 to 40 bps if leakage spreads.
  4. The grid reference loop (reinforcing, gated). Prequalifications and references (the eight-year TenneT contract for five 380/110 kV substations) win more awards and attract crews; Germany's organic growth reached +7.3% in Q2 2026. Equipment lead times (transformers 128 to 160 weeks; Deutsche Bank cites up to six years for high-power units) and connection permitting gate conversion into revenue. For the line items: German organic growth of about 5% to 6% a year in FY27 to FY28 rather than the 7% to 8% the order book alone would suggest; revenue lags orders by one to two years.
  5. The French loop (balancing on revenue, reinforcing on margin, ending in 2027 to 2028). Selectivity and fibre run-off shrink revenue (the fibre business falls from about EUR 150m to about EUR 120m in 2026) while margin holds; there is no sign of fixed-cost under-absorption (France 7.1% in FY25). Once fibre is small (2027 to 2028) the loop goes neutral and France can grow at 0.5% to 1%. For the line items: France flat in FY26, +0.5% FY27, +1% FY28, margin about 7.1% to 7.3%.
  6. The negative working-capital loop (reinforcing while revenue grows). Organic growth releases about EUR 9m of cash per EUR 100m of revenue growth; it reverses only if revenue falls (a 5% decline absorbs about EUR 50m). Not the base case, but it means FCF is pro-cyclical in a way the high-conversion record hides.
  7. The reflexive valuation loop (switched off). At about 11.5x to 11.7x forward earnings and an FCF yield near 8% to 9%, a buyback earns a higher initial return than any acquisition at above 12x, let alone ROFA's 17x to 20x. Bravida started a SEK 100m buyback in October 2026 when it judged targets poor. SPIE's Q1 2026 buyback (1.25m shares, EUR 59m) only offset the employee plan. If management switched on this loop in 2027 to 2028 (leverage falling towards 1.5x), it would add 1% to 2% to EPS a year; I do not assume it.
  8. The customer-financing loop (balancing, small). Data-centre co-locators face tighter credit (asset-backed issuance slowing at about 8% all-in debt cost; neocloud spreads wider); data centres are about 3% of SPIE's revenue. Utilities' cost of capital (Elia's EUR 2bn funding gap; Bund yields at 3.44%) matters more but is mitigated by regulated returns. For the line items: a timing risk to orders, not to revenue in FY26 to FY27.
  9. Net effect on the forecasts. The loops justify a narrow cone for revenue and EBITA (total growth 6.5% to 8.5% a year; margin 7.8% to 8.2%) and a wider cone for return on capital and EPS (because each new acquisition is less accretive and financed at higher coupons). They also explain why EPS grows more slowly than EBITA in FY26 to FY27 without any operational problem.

Case simplicity

Group judgement

Risks

B626th of 35

Layer 3 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The financial forecasts are predictable in two speeds and unpredictable in a third. At the speed the company controls and guides, revenue, EBITA, margin and cash, the case is well founded on demonstrated strengths, cross-confirmed by the first and second sets and by every AlphaSense report, concentrated in two durable drivers (German grid conversion; the acquisition engine) whose risk is timing rather than existence, and bounded by a cone of about 10% around FY27 EBITA with a slight upward skew; the company's semi-annual disclosure is adequate to track it. At the speed the market quotes, adjusted EPS, the forecast is predictable in direction (below consensus by 7% to 9% on the company's own basis for FY26 to FY27) because the three items that cause the gap are contractual, enacted or already issued; the residual uncertainty (the 2027 surcharge rate, the financial run-rate, ROFA's return on a price near twice what the accretion models assumed) is dated to the 5 March 2027 results, and the skew against my base is slightly down to 18 months and up thereafter as leverage falls towards 1x. At the third speed, the multiple, nothing in the forecasts governs: 78% of the share price's recent movement is idiosyncratic, and the idiosyncratic part is French sovereign yield, with a binary date in April to May 2027. The Consumer should write the predictability topic as: operating forecasts high and defensible; EPS forecasts moderate and below consensus for mechanical reasons the company can explain in March; the multiple exogenous until the French election, hedgeable against Eiffage or VINCI, and already near a trough band in the model's own fair multiple. The one unresolved fundamental risk that could change the operating speed is ROFA: a cyclical, project-based platform bought at a price the sell-side has not yet examined.

D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

D6423rd of 35

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the expected change at the EBITA and cash lines rests almost entirely on demonstrated strengths and is plausible; the expected change in organic growth rests half on a demonstrated strength (German grid execution and pricing) and half on one not yet shown (sustained volume growth outside Germany); and the sell-side's expected change in EPS rests on things that are not strengths at all (falling financial costs and a falling tax rate) and is implausible. The 2026 step into factory automation at ROFA's price relies on a capability SPIE has never demonstrated: earning a return on a large, cyclical, project-based platform through cross-selling. The thesis as I hold it does not need that capability; consensus EPS does.

  1. Demonstrated, and sufficient for the EBITA path. (a) Margin discipline: 5.1% (2020) to 7.6% (2025) through 4% to 6% wage inflation, every annual target since 2023 met or beaten, H1 2026 +20 bps with drop-through of 11.9% against analysts' 9% to 10.5%. (b) Pass-through pricing: revenue per employee outgrew staff cost per employee for five years (Deutsche Bank); indexation on all current contracts (Bernstein, May 2026); Equans and VINCI Energies confirm sector-wide discipline. (c) Cash: conversion above 100% every year since 2015 except 2021; working capital -34 days; H1 2026 FCF positive for the first time. (d) German grid leadership: a two-to-three-year high-voltage backlog, the eight-year TenneT five-substation contract, the Sprendlingen live rebuild running to schedule (handover March 2027), 15% to 16% segment margins (Bernstein). (e) Bolt-on integration: Worksphere (EUR 434m of revenue, 10% organic growth post-deal, +130 bps margin, -9 days of working capital), Robur (platform for ROFA and SGS), over 90 deals since 2013. (f) Labour retention: voluntary turnover 5.9%, SGS adding 800 technicians with Eastern European recruiting channels. These six strengths deliver my FY26 to FY28 EBITA of EUR 872m, 956m and 1,042m with a margin of safety, because the margin path needs only about 11 bps a year and the acquired cohort's margins (ROFA "high single digit", SGS above 10%) are above the group's.
  2. Half-demonstrated: organic growth of 3%. The record is 1.8% a year (2015 to 2025) and below the peer median in 18 of 33 quarters; post-pandemic volume ran 2% to 2.5% at best with price doing two-thirds of the work (Morgan Stanley). What has changed is the mix of end markets: Germany (35% of revenue, +5.3% in 2025, +7.3% in Q2 2026) is backed by TenneT's EUR 14bn of 2027 capex (from EUR 5bn in 2025), Amprion's EUR 42.1bn to 2030 and E.ON's EUR 48bn, and the fading French fibre drag removes about 1 pp of French decline by 2027. So 3% in 2027 to 2028 is plausible on demonstrated German execution plus arithmetic, without any new capability. What is not demonstrated is 1% to 1.5% of real volume growth outside Germany; North-Western Europe's Dutch high-voltage work has run six to twelve months late with 20% to 25% overruns (expert calls, September and October 2026), and the T-D1 report's experts attribute the Cologne data-centre setbacks to a crew of two employees and the rest subcontractors. Hence my organic path (2.0%, 3.0%, 3.3%) sits below the target and below Jefferies (4% to 5%) and Bernstein (3.7% to 3.9%), and the precedents support me: Bilfinger's 2012 to 2015 targets were set on acquisition-led growth and missed (ING, 2012); Eltel's single-market bets failed; SPIE's own 2015 to 2020 period combined SAG's dilution with flat organic growth.
  3. Not demonstrated: earning a return on ROFA at the price paid. Deutsche Bank computes a 9.1% post-tax return on cumulative acquisition spend over 2016 to 2025, "barely in line with" a 9% cost of capital, against management's 17% for the 2019 to 2022 cohort; Jefferies documented the fall in ROIC after SAG (EUR 850m, 2017). ROFA is a stand-alone platform in factory automation (80%) and intralogistics (20%), with about 30% of revenue from automotive clients (Volkswagen, Porsche, BMW, Audi) whose European capex Rothschild Redburn expects to fall sharply after 2028, and with project-funded rather than maintenance-funded negative working capital (Morgan Stanley: "less efficient" than SPIE's). The stated synergies are "primarily commercial", the least reliable kind, and are unquantified. If ROFA's enterprise value is 15x to 18x EBITA (D-01, point 2), the entry post-tax return is about 4% and ROFA must raise its EBITA by half to earn its cost of capital. SPIE has done something like this once (SAG, which took five years and diluted margins) and done the opposite with Worksphere (a bolt-in to an existing country platform at a normal price). The relevant precedents for an installer moving up the value chain into project automation are unfavourable: Instalco's Boas Industriservice (DNB Carnegie, 2023: higher capex and working capital, margin compression if cross-selling fails) and Bilfinger's industrial-services build-out. My base case therefore gives ROFA no synergy and no organic growth and keeps the group ROCE dipping to about 12% in FY26 to FY27; that is the conservative reading and still delivers the EBITA path, because ROFA's EUR 35m to 40m of EBITA is only 4% of the group.
  4. Not a strength at all: what consensus EPS requires. Consensus has EBITA-to-adjusted-net-income conversion rising from 57.7% (FY25) to 60.6% (FY26) and 62.1% (FY27). That requires falling financial costs (against two new bonds, a EUR 820m rise in net debt and Deutsche Bank's EUR 15m of extra interest), a falling share count (against 3.37m new ORNANE shares and about 1.8m shares from the December 2026 employee plan) and a flat tax rate (against an extended surcharge). SPIE's five-year EPS record (about 11% a year, second-fastest in its peer group) was built on deleveraging from 3.3x and a 2% convertible; those tailwinds have reversed. The 15.6% consensus EPS growth for FY26 is the number in the case with the least support from delivered strengths.
  5. The CEO. Holzke's group-level record is six months old; his first acts (project reviews, unchanged model, reiteration rather than a raise in July despite a beat, ROFA's price) are consistent with continuity and a floor-setting style. The T-D1 report's experts and Bernstein both flag the simultaneity of the largest acquisition cohort since SAG with the succession as the test. I do not change the weights for him; I widen the cone on acquisition returns, not on EBITA.
  6. Net. Plausibility is high for revenue, EBITA, margin and cash; moderate for organic growth; low for consensus EPS; and unproven for ROFA's return. The thesis I carry (EBITA above EUR 1bn in 2028, FCF rising to EUR 855m by 2030, EPS of EUR 3.13 to 3.16 in FY27) relies only on demonstrated strengths; the consensus thesis relies on two it does not have.

D-04

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

B+625th of 35

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the cone is narrow for revenue, EBITA and cash (FY27 EBITA about -6% to +4% around my EUR 956m to 959m), moderate for adjusted EPS (FY27 about -10% to +8% around EUR 3.10 to 3.16 on the economic basis) and wide only for the multiple, which is being set by French sovereign yields rather than by anything in the forecasts. The skew differs by line and by horizon: EBITA is skewed slightly up (the floor-and-beat record; Bernstein already at 7.9% for 2026); EPS is skewed slightly down against my base over 12 months (the surcharge, a higher financial run-rate, ROFA's cycle) and up beyond 18 months (unmodelled bolt-ons, the capital-return option as leverage falls towards 1x); and against consensus the EPS skew is decisively down, with consensus FY27 EPS sitting at or above the top of my cone. On the company's own definitions the case is among the more predictable in its sector; on the number the market quotes, EPS, it is predictable in direction and moderately uncertain in degree.

  1. The FY27 cone, line by line.
FY27 Bear Base (model) Bull Consensus
Organic growth (FY26 / FY27) 1.7% / 1.0% 2.0% / 3.0% 2.5% / 4.0% about 2.5% / 3.3%
EBITA (EUR m) about 905 (margin 7.65%; 20 bps of subcontractor leakage; no new deals; ROFA EBITA halved) 959 (7.93%) about 1,000 (8.1%; German public money early; EUR 500m of deals) 946
Net financial items (EUR m) about -150 -140 -132 implied about -105 to -120
Adjusted EPS, economic basis (EUR) about 2.80 3.13 to 3.16 (3.10 after my input changes) about 3.40 n/a
Adjusted EPS, company basis (EUR) about 2.90 about 3.23 about 3.50 3.46 (range 3.02 to 3.72)
Free cash flow, company basis (EUR m) about 560 635 about 680 657

The width of the EBITA cone (about 10% end to end) is set by what 85% start-of-year visibility, 70% to 75% maintenance and a 56% subcontracted cost base allow; the bear case needs three things to go wrong at once (France slipping before the election, ROFA's automotive clients deferring, a project loss in high-voltage or data-centre work). The EPS cone is wider (about 18% end to end) because the below-EBITA items add their own variance: the surcharge rate for 2027 (31.4% or 41.2%, a EUR 4m to 5m swing), the term loan's refinancing rate, the Share For You 2026 take-up, and whether the company and the brokers add the surcharge back. The FY26 cone is narrow (economic EPS EUR 2.82 to 2.98) because H1 is reported and the bonds' effect on H2 is arithmetic.

  1. Why consensus sits outside the cone. Reconstructing the stale brokers from the FactSet table: Bernstein's FY27 adjusted net income of EUR 594m on about 169m shares needs net financial items of about EUR 105m, about EUR 30m below the FY25 run-rate on the same basis, with no ORNANE shares and no surcharge; Oddo (EUR 3.72), RBC (3.55), Jefferies (3.46), UBS (3.42) and CIC (3.37) show the same signature. Only Deutsche Bank (3.02, which also deducts IFRS 2 charges) and the unnamed 3 September estimate (3.24) have absorbed the refinancing. The FY27 consensus of EUR 3.46 is therefore not a forecast of a better outcome than mine; it is a forecast built on a capital structure that no longer exists. That makes the direction of the gap's closure near-certain and its timing the only question (see I-02).
  2. Skew over the next twelve months. Down for EPS against my base: (a) the surcharge is more likely to persist than lapse (D-01); (b) the financial line's risk is on the high side (D-03); (c) ROFA's automotive clients are cutting capex (Volkswagen restructuring, European automotive capex "heading toward a violent cliff" after 2028, Rothschild Redburn); (d) Q1 2027 falls in the French pre-election quarter and the Middle East suspensions persist. Up for EBITA against my base: (a) H1 2026 drop-through of 11.9%; (b) Bernstein's 7.9% for 2026; (c) Q3 2026 previews already at scope +8.8% and organic 2.5% to 3%; (d) Central Europe's weather catch-up (+8% organic expected in Q3). The two skews offset at the net income line, which is why I leave the FY26 and FY27 EBITDA margin inputs unchanged and move only the tax rate and share count.
  3. Skew over 18 to 48 months. Up: leverage falls to about 1.5x by end-2027 and about 1.0x by 2029 on my path (Bernstein has net debt at EUR 853m by 2028), creating about EUR 1bn of headroom to 2.0x, about 14% of today's market value, that management has not committed to use; each EUR 300m of buybacks at about 11x to 12x earnings adds about 1.5% to EPS, and Deutsche Bank's scenario of EUR 1.0bn to 1.35bn of annual M&A gives 14% FY28 EPS accretion. Down: the surcharge becoming permanent (-2.7% of FY28 EPS), German automotive capex compressing after 2028, acquisition multiples rising further. Net, the long-horizon skew is positive and mostly unmodelled by consensus.
  4. The asymmetry the cone hides. The 2020 precedent (revenue -4.8%, EBITA -EUR 77m, a 27% decremental margin) shows that in a sudden shock the cone's lower edge is two to three times further away than the upward drop-through suggests; a gradual slowdown is handled by releasing subcontractors within a quarter or two. The plausible sudden-shock triggers over 18 months are a French sovereign crisis freezing private capex and a data-centre financing freeze (ABS spreads widening, Morgan Stanley, 22 October 2026); the first would cost about 2% to 3% of EBITA for a 5% fall in French revenue, the second is a timing risk on about 3% of revenue. Neither is in my bear case; together they would take the FY27 EBITA bear case to about EUR 870m (-9%).
  5. Predictability, scored by line. Revenue: high (85% visible; scope known). EBITA and margin: high (narrow cone, record of beats, drop-through measured). Free cash flow: high (structural, with the single caveat that negative working capital reverses if revenue falls). Adjusted EPS: moderate in degree, high in direction (below consensus). Return on new capital: low (ROFA). The multiple: low and exogenous (D-07). The case's predictability is therefore good where the company guides and poor where the market looks; the memo should say so in those words.
D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

D4719th of 35

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: helpful enough on the operating thesis (quarterly organic growth by segment is exactly the variable that decides the German grid conversion and the French stabilisation), unhelpful on the margin and on everything below EBITA between the half-years, and silent on the three things the Consumer most needs to watch (order intake and backlog, ROFA's own performance, and the composition of the EPS bridge). The next six months contain one rich disclosure event (5 March 2027) bracketed by two thin ones (30 October 2026 and late April 2027), so monitoring is possible but lumpy: the operating thesis can be tracked quarterly; the EPS thesis can be checked only once, in March.

  1. What SPIE discloses and when. Quarterly: revenue by segment with organic, scope and currency effects, qualitative comment on trends, guidance reiteration. Semi-annually: segment EBITA margins, the net financial expense composition (cost of net debt, lease interest, pension interest, fair-value items), the normative tax rate and the exceptional contribution as a separate note, adjusted net income and its bridge, FCF, net debt and leverage, acquisitions with consideration and provisional PPA, the diluted share count. Annually: the universal registration document with cost structure (subcontracting over 56% of expenses), customer concentration, working capital days, the LTIP terms. Ad hoc: acquisitions (price usually disclosed only in the half-year report, as ROFA's was), bond issues, the employee plan result (3 December 2026 for Share For You 2026).
  2. What it does not disclose. Order intake and backlog in figures (only "record order book" and "two to three years" of high-voltage backlog, both via brokers); business-line revenue or margin (grid, data centres about 3%, nuclear, technical facility management); project-level margins (expert calls note management refuses them); ROFA, SGS or any acquired entity's standalone revenue, order intake or margin after consolidation; quarterly margins; any EPS, interest, tax or share-count guidance; the fixed rate on the EUR 300m of swaps; the split of organic growth into price and volume. The T-D1 report's gap list ("exact pricing and volume split", "ROFA's exact operating margins", "subcontractor labour cost inflation") is the right list.
  3. The six-month monitoring calendar, with what each event can and cannot show. (a) 30 October 2026, Q3 sales: can show whether Germany is above 5% to 6%, France near -0.5%, Central Europe catching up and Global Services Energy near -10%; whether scope is near +8.8% (which tests that ROFA and SGS consolidated as assumed); whether management firms FY26 margin guidance (it firmed FY24 at Q3 2024). Cannot show margin, ROFA's margin, interest or the share count. (b) 3 December 2026, Share For You 2026 result: the number of shares created (my assumption about 1.8m); this fixes the FY27 average share count within 0.3%. (c) 5 March 2027, FY26 results: the one event that tests the EPS thesis: net financial items against about EUR 120m to 130m (FactSet basis), the FY26 surcharge against about EUR 14m to 18m and its treatment in adjusted EPS, the diluted share count (about 172m average, about 174.6m year-end), ROFA's purchase price allocation and acquired cash (the ROFA price question), H2 margin (about 9.2%), FCF (about EUR 565m to 610m), leverage (about 1.7x), FY27 guidance wording, and any tax guidance for 2027. (d) Late April 2027, Q1 sales: optically strong organic growth against the weather-hit Q1 2026 (-0.9%); the French pre-election quarter; no margin.
  4. What is helpful about the operating disclosure. The segment organic figures are published on a consistent basis, quarterly, with scope separated, so the German acceleration and the French bottoming can be read without adjustment; management's commentary has a verifiable record (the Q1 2026 weather explanation was validated in Q2); the half-year margin by segment lets the Consumer check the German grid mix (Germany 5.8% in H1 2026, 7.9% in FY25) and Central Europe's recovery (2.4% in H1 2026 against 6.0% in FY25). The "set a floor, firm it up" guidance style means a firming at Q3 or H1 is itself a signal.
  5. What is unhelpful, and the workarounds. Because the net financial line, the share count and the tax line are disclosed only semi-annually, the EPS cut I expect in consensus cannot be confirmed by company data until March; the workaround is arithmetic from the bond prospectuses and the 20 October press release, which the Consumer already has. Because ROFA is not disclosed, its contribution must be inferred from the German segment's scope effect and margin; the workaround is the PPA note in March and expert calls with German integration staff. Because there is no backlog figure, the German grid conversion must be read from TenneT's, Amprion's and 50Hertz's published capex and from peers (VINCI Energies' German revenue +12% in the first nine months of 2026; Eiffage's EQOS awards). Because the price and volume split is undisclosed, Deutsche Bank's revenue-per-employee against staff-cost-per-employee spread (published annually) is the only pass-through monitor.
  6. Peer comparison. Bravida and Instalco report quarterly margins and order backlogs (Bravida SEK 24.2bn, +48%); Bilfinger reports quarterly orders received and a book-to-bill (1.05 in 2025); VINCI reports quarterly order intake for Energies. SPIE's quarterly disclosure is thinner than all of these on margins and orders, and richer than Bilfinger's on segment organic growth. For a stock whose debate is now about the EPS bridge, the semi-annual rhythm is the main handicap.
D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

B549th of 35

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the trace is clear and quantified from the company's targets to EBITA, because the 2028 framework is built from blocks (3% to 4% organic, acquired growth, 8.0% margin, EBITA above EUR 1bn, FCF above EUR 2bn, leverage below 2.0x, 40% payout) and the drop-through mechanism is measurable; it stops at EBITA, because SPIE gives no guidance on interest, tax or share count, and the two years in which those three lines move most are exactly FY26 and FY27. The external transmission is clear in direction and sequence (TSO capex plans to tenders to SPIE's German organic growth to German margin to group EBITA) and unclear in timing (equipment lead times, permitting, fund disbursement), with a one-to-two-year lag that the company does not quantify. The weakest link in the whole chain is the last one, EBITA to EPS, which is where the forecasting dispute with consensus lives.

  1. Internal trace, company targets to the lines. Organic 3% to 4%: traces to segment organic growth (quarterly), and through an 11% to 12% incremental margin to about 11 bps a year of margin; the company does not state the incremental margin but it is measurable from the record (10.7% in FY24, 11.7% to 11.9% in H1 2026). Acquired growth: traces to the scope effect by segment (quarterly) and to announced deal revenue (ROFA about EUR 430m, SGS about EUR 180m, BLOCK about EUR 50m, Worley about EUR 70m, Artemys about EUR 82m); the company gives deal revenue and a margin description but not EBITA or price until the half-year report. Margin 8.0%: traces to segment margins semi-annually, with the identifiable contributors being the German grid mix, acquired margins above the group's, Central Europe's recovery from 2.4% and the fading French fibre drag. EBITA above EUR 1bn: the product of the two. FCF above EUR 2bn cumulative: traces to about 100% cash conversion, working capital at -30 to -34 days, capex at 0.8% of revenue and lease payments at about 1.7%; on my path the target is beaten by about EUR 400m and the company will almost certainly raise it. Leverage below 2.0x: traces to the net debt path (about 1.75x end-2026, 1.5x end-2027). Payout 40%: traces to the dividend. None of these blocks is vague; the FY26 annual guidance ("strong total revenue growth and continued EBITA margin expansion") is vague, but the first set was right that the mid-term blocks do the work.
  2. Where the trace breaks: EBITA to EPS. The company reports adjusted net income and adjusted EPS, with the surcharge excluded from the normative rate, but guides on none of the components. The three components that change in FY26 to FY27 (net financial items from about EUR 111m to about EUR 140m on the second set's basis; the surcharge; the share count from 170.1m to about 175m average) are each disclosed after the fact, semi-annually, and each is treated differently by different brokers (Deutsche Bank deducts IFRS 2 and the surcharge; Bernstein and most others follow the company). The result is a 23% spread in individual FY27 EPS estimates (EUR 3.02 to 3.72) for a business whose EBITA estimates are within 4% of each other. For the memo, the EBITA-to-EPS bridge must be shown line by line on both bases, and the Consumer should present the model's EUR 3.16 (economic) and about EUR 3.26 (company basis) for FY27 against the EUR 3.46 consensus with the three deductions named.
  3. External transmission, the German grid chain. Policy and regulation (RP5 allowed return about 6.5% from 2026, draft electricity decision mid-2027, final late 2027; the EUR 500bn fund disbursing from 2027) to TSO and DSO capex (TenneT EUR 14bn in 2027 from EUR 5bn in 2025; Amprion EUR 42.1bn 2026 to 2030; E.ON EUR 48bn to 2030) to contract awards (TenneT's eight-year bundled substation contract; Omexom's ten-year 145-substation framework in the Netherlands) to equipment delivery (transformers 2.5 to 4.5 years; switchgear 52 to 65 weeks) to SPIE's German organic growth (5.3% in 2025, 7.3% in Q2 2026) to German margin (grid 15% to 16%; segment 7.9%) to group EBITA. Each link is observable; the lag between award and revenue is one to two years and the company does not state it. The transmission to EPS then passes through the three below-EBITA items, which the external chain does not touch. The equipment makers (Siemens Energy, ABB) sit upstream with pricing power; the own-5 report's experts put grid procurement price inflation at 5% to 8% in 2026, which SPIE passes through under indexation, so the chain transmits volume and mix to SPIE, not price.
  4. External transmission, the French chain. French sovereign spreads (137 to 171 bps over Bunds in October 2026) to the 2027 budget (surcharge extension; EUR 9bn of higher social contributions) to private client capex deferral (non-recurrent work about 25% of group revenue) to French organic growth, and in parallel to SPIE's tax line and to its multiple (correlation -0.88 with French ten-year yields in 2026). This chain is clear and short; its effect on EBITA is small (France 28% of EBITA; public-sector exposure 5% of group revenue) and its effect on the multiple is large.
  5. External transmission, the acquisition chain. German installer multiples (7x to 9x) to the price of the next EUR 350m of deals to EPS accretion per deal (about 1% to 1.5% at 9x, debt-funded) to the FY28 to FY30 EPS path; consensus models none of it, so this chain is invisible in consensus and fully present in my forecasts.
  6. Milestones and measures the Consumer should pin to the memo. Germany organic above 5% in Q3 2026 and above 5.5% in 2027; France at -0.5% in Q3 2026 and flat to positive from Q4 2026; FY26 EBITA margin at or above 7.8%; H2 2026 margin about 9.2%; FY26 net financial items about EUR 120m to 130m (FactSet basis); FY26 surcharge about EUR 14m to 18m; year-end 2026 diluted shares about 174.6m; ROFA's acquired net cash in the PPA; H1 2027 margin about 6.5% with the caveat that half of the step is ROFA's seasonality; the 2027 Capital Markets Day's 2030 targets. These are the measures that decide the forecasts; the company publishes all of them, but only the organic ones quarterly.
