C-82

What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

B+385th of 35

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: these risks are the more likely source of near-term share-price damage, and the environment makes them live: French sovereign stress, the AI-financing crack, peer warnings and a momentum regime that is selling exactly SPIE's profile.

  • French sovereign and political stress. Spreads at decade highs with Italian spillover; Goldman Sachs judges France's fiscal challenge harder than in 2011 to 2015. The market applies the French discount to the ticker although France is 32% of revenue. A spread spike could cost 10% to 15% independent of earnings.
  • AI and data-centre de-rating. The Industry Digest groups SPIE with the "data centre's power bill" wave; neocloud and hyperscaler credit is widening. A hyperscaler pause would hit SPIE's multiple even though data centres are about 3% of revenue.
  • Peer events. Bilfinger's September 2026 warning (shares -20% on the day, -44% over twelve months) already weighed on the group; a principal-contractor write-down at Bravida (atNorth, Green Mountain) would revive "contractor risk" fears sector-wide.
  • Regime and technicals. Risk-off with Momentum and Fragility tilts Underweight; SPIE trades 12.1% below its 200-day average with the 50-day below the 150- and 200-day, relative strength at the 17th percentile and price near its 52-week low (EUR 40.10). Momentum-driven selling can push the multiple below fundamental value.
  • Positioning. 85% of brokers rate Buy (10 of 11 visible); an asymmetry the Wisdom Library warns about ("the bear case lives in qualitative framing"): the bear arguments (France, labour, acquisition returns) are qualitative and unmodelled, so a first downgrade with a quantified bear case could move the shares more than the news warrants.

C-70

Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

B648th of 35

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: a consistent "set a floor, firm it up during the year, meet or beat it by 0 to 10 bps" pattern under Louette, plus early raising of mid-term targets once achieved; it is the Wisdom Library's "sandbagging precision" archetype. Holzke has no record yet; in July 2026 he reiterated rather than raised FY26 guidance despite a margin beat.

  • Record. The 2025 target of 6.7% was reached in 2023 (two years early); FY24 "at least" target firmed to 7.1% in Q3 2024, delivered at 7.2% (7.19%); FY25 firmed to "at least 7.6%" in July 2025, delivered at exactly 7.6%; the 2028 target raised from "at least 7.7%" to 8.0% one year after it was set; organic growth in 2023 beat the initial floor (reported as 8.4% against at least 6.0%).
  • Nature. Louette: "when we give guidance, we stick to it"; targets phrased as minimums; upgrades come in the half-year or Q3 update, not at the start of the year.
  • Implication. Expect FY26 delivery at or slightly above 7.8% and a possible upgrade of mid-term ambitions at the 2027 Capital Markets Day; do not expect a raise of organic targets.
C-69

What is the company's record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and sell-side estimates over the last one, three and five years, including in comparable environmental conditions?

C+6410th of 35

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: a reliable beater on margin, cash and leverage and a frequent slight misser on revenue and organic growth, over one, three and five years; the record held through the 2022 to 2024 inflation and the 2020 shock.

  • Last year. FY25: margin 7.6% exactly on guidance; organic 2.0% in line with Jefferies' 2%; leverage 1.3x against consensus 1.7x; FCF strong. H1 2026: revenue -0.3% against consensus (EUR 5,156.6m against 5,171m); organic +1.2% against +1.0%; EBITA EUR 321.4m against 317m (+1.4%); margin 6.2% against 6.1%; FCF EUR 25.7m against -EUR 44m; leverage 2.1x against 2.2x. Misses: Q3 2025 organic (German and North-Western European phasing); Q1 2026 organic (weather); France and Global Services Energy in H1 2026 (France -0.7% against +0.6% consensus; Global Services Energy -6.7% against -4.0%).
  • Three years. Margin targets met or beaten every year (6.7% in 2023, two years early; 7.2% against at least 7.1%; 7.6% against at least 7.6%).
  • Five years. Margin +260 bps (2020 to 2025); cash conversion above 100% except 2021; EPS growth second-fastest in the peer group with the second-fewest negative revision days (Deutsche Bank).
  • Comparable conditions. In the 2022 to 2023 inflation SPIE expanded margins through indexation; in 2020 revenue fell 4.8% and margin troughed near 5%, but cash held.
P-06

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

Judgement: weight the margin, cash and leverage forecasts heavily and lean them slightly up; weight the organic growth forecast moderately and lean it slightly down; weight the acquisition contribution heavily on revenue but lightly on returns; and weight consensus EPS lightly and lean it down, because the record that makes SPIE's operating forecasts trustworthy does not extend to the below-EBITA lines, which are now moving against EPS for reasons outside the operating record.

The record therefore holds in inflation and in a sharp shock. It has not been tested in a period when financing costs rise against the company, which is the situation now.

The EPS forecast deserves a wider cone and a downward lean against consensus, not against history: my FY26 to FY28 EPS growth of 7.5%, 8.3% and 13.4% is in line with SPIE's own record of high single-digit to low double-digit EPS growth; it is consensus (15.6% and 11.2% for FY26 and FY27) that departs from the record.

Group judgement

Financial forecasts

F4427th of 35

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

SPIE's operating forecasts are well founded and their cone is narrow; its EPS forecasts are where the case is exposed, and they are exposed downwards against consensus, not against history. The operating engine (about 3% organic growth led by German grid work, about 4% acquired growth, contribution-margin leverage of about 11 bps a year, pass-through pricing with a little real price in grid and nuclear) delivers revenue of about EUR 12.1bn in FY27 and EBITA above EUR 1bn in FY28 at an 8.05% margin, matching the company's targets with a modest margin of safety; FCF grows to about EUR 855m by FY30 and leverage falls to about 1x by 2029. Below EBITA, 2026 reversed the three tailwinds that flattered EPS for five years: net financial costs rise by about EUR 30m (ROFA's debt, the 3.875% and 4.875% bonds replacing cheap debt), the French surcharge recurs (EUR 10.1m in H1 2026, extended in the 2027 draft budget) and the share count rises about 3% by FY27 (3.37m ORNANE shares, employee-plan creep). The result is adjusted EPS of EUR 2.89, 3.13 and 3.55 for FY26 to FY28, 7%, 10% and 5% below consensus, whose implied conversion of EBITA into net income (60.6% to 65.1%) has no precedent in SPIE's own figures (57.7% in FY25). For the third set, the predictability question is therefore two-speed: high for revenue, EBITA and cash; moderate for EPS, with its centre below consensus. For the fourth set, the key fact is that the next likely revisions are EPS cuts with unchanged EBITA, most visibly at the FY26 results on 5 March 2027, set against a share price that has already de-rated on French and regime risk.

P-08

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

F3228th of 35

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: below EBITA, three forces turn against EPS at the same time in FY26 to FY27 (financial cost up about EUR 30m, the French surcharge recurring at about EUR 15m to 20m a year, and the share count up about 3% from FY25 to FY27), so adjusted net income converts only about 57% of EBITA against 60% to 62% in consensus. This makes adjusted EPS grow about 7.5% (FY26) and 8% (FY27), 2 to 3 points below EBITA growth, before the drags reverse in FY28 to FY30 (interest falling with debt, the surcharge assumed to lapse) and EPS growth rises to 9% to 13%. Reported EPS, by contrast, roughly doubles from EUR 1.05 (FY25) to about EUR 2.38 (FY27) because the ORNANE noise has gone. Free cash flow stays at 110% to 120% of adjusted net income and grows about 10% a year.

  1. The FY25 base and its ambiguity, resolved for modelling. FY25 adjusted net income of EUR 457.6m reconciles to EBITA of EUR 793.5m in two ways that fit the published figures equally well: (a) net financial items of about EUR 111m (FactSet), the 30.2% normative tax rate, minorities of EUR 2.3m, and the EUR 15.8m French exceptional contribution deducted inside adjusted net income; or (b) net financial items of about EUR 138m and a 30.2% rate that already contains the surcharge (one source in the first set's files says the 30.2% rose "due to Germany's high 30.7% tax and French surcharges"). The evidence does not settle which. Both give the same FY25 conversion (57.7%), and both imply that if the surcharge recurs, it is a cost to adjusted EPS. I model (a): net financial items from EUR 111m and an all-in tax rate of 32.6% for FY25. The Consumer should use my net financial items and tax rate as a pair, not mix one with another source's.
  2. The surcharge is not a 2025 one-off. SPIE booked a further EUR 10.1m exceptional tax charge in H1 2026; the 2027 draft French budget extends the surcharge for large companies (Safran's 2027 tax rate revised from 27% to 35%; Bernstein: firms above EUR 3bn of revenue face an average 36.1% rate). I assume about EUR 18m in FY26 and EUR 16m in FY27, and that it lapses from FY28 after the 2027 elections (a 50/50 call; if it persists, FY28 EPS is about 2.7% lower). If SPIE adds the surcharge back in its own adjusted EPS (as several brokers do), its headline adjusted EPS will be about EUR 0.10 above my figures in FY26 and FY27; the economic cost is the same.
  3. The line-by-line bridge (my base case).

  3. Other below-EBITA items, for reported earnings. PPA amortisation EUR 58.1m in H1 2025 and EUR 46m in H1 2026, so about EUR 100m a year in FY26 and FY27 as ROFA's intangibles (brand, customer relationships, backlog; allocation under way) replace older SAG-era amortisation, then about EUR 80m to 95m. IFRS 2 charges about EUR 60m to 68m a year (EUR 59.8m in FY25; about 7% of EBITA; Bilfinger keeps its far smaller EUR 4.1m charge inside its adjusted EBITA, so SPIE's adjusted margin is about 0.6 pp flattered against Bilfinger). ORNANE fair-value effects end in October 2026 (EUR -132.9m in FY25, EUR -15.9m in H1 2026). Associates negligible (EUR 0.1m in H1 2026). Minorities small (EUR 2.3m), but put options to buy out minorities of EUR 215.1m (end-2025) are a future cash call that sits in acquisition spending.
  4. Reported net income and EPS. About EUR 360m (FY26), 415m (FY27), 495m (FY28), 555m (FY29), 620m (FY30); reported basic EPS about EUR 2.13, 2.38, 2.82, 3.17, 3.52, against EUR 1.05 (FY25), 1.63 (FY24), 1.45 (FY23). Insight for the fourth set: screens and investors using reported earnings will see SPIE's trailing P/E fall from about 39x (FY25 reported) to about 17x on FY27 reported EPS, which may widen the buyer base once FY26 is published (5 March 2027).
  5. Free cash flow (company definition: after capex, leases, interest and tax; before acquisitions and dividends). FY25 EUR 523.7m; my FY26 EUR 565m, FY27 635m, FY28 720m, FY29 790m, FY30 855m (110% to 120% of adjusted net income; consensus EUR 610m, 657m, 715m). FY26 to FY27 FCF grows more slowly than EBITDA because cash tax now equals the P&L tax (Deutsche Bank: the historical cash-tax advantage has run out), coupons rise and lease payments rise with acquisitions (about EUR 190m in FY26 to EUR 243m in FY30). FCF per share about EUR 3.63 in FY27: an 8.9% yield at EUR 40.76. The company's cumulative target of more than EUR 2bn over 2025 to 2028 will be beaten comfortably (about EUR 2.4bn in my path).
  6. Against the last four to six years. 2019 to 2025: below-EBITA costs fell as a share of EBITA (deleveraging from about 3x, a cheap 2% convertible, cash tax below P&L tax), so adjusted EPS grew about 11% a year (Deutsche Bank) against EBITA growth of about 11%. FY25 to FY27: all three reverse, so EPS lags EBITA by 2 to 3 points a year. FY27 to FY30: debt falls and (I assume) the surcharge lapses, so EPS grows about 1 point faster than EBITA again. The four-year adjusted EPS CAGR (FY26 to FY30) is about 9.7%; the five-year (FY25 to FY30) about 9.3%.

Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

F5034th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: there is plausible scope for EBITA margin to reach 8.0% to 8.5% by 2028 to 2029 (+40 to 90 bps), from many small areas rather than one, congruent with the strategy; precedents support 8% to 8.5% but nothing in the sector shows a large multi-technical group sustaining above 9%.

  • Sources. (1) Mix towards high-voltage grid (15% to 16%), nuclear, data centres, cleanrooms and automation; (2) Central Europe margin normalisation (2.4% in H1 2026 against 6.0% for FY25); (3) Global Services Energy recovery after Middle East suspensions (segment margin 8.2% to 10.2%); (4) French operating leverage as fibre drag fades and building stabilises; (5) acquisition accretion (about 10 bps a year; SGS above 10%); (6) real pricing (10 to 20 bps a year); (7) digital service attach (Smart FM); (8) cash: working capital already best-in-class, so little more.
  • Precedents. VINCI Energies at 7.4% to 7.8%; Bilfinger targeting 8% to 9% by 2030; Instalco above 8% in 2017 to 2021; Equans repairing from 2.9% to 5%; Bernstein's normalised SPIE margin 8.4%.
  • Congruence. All are extensions of "margin over volume", Germany-first and bolt-on strategy. The incongruent element is ROFA's factory-automation cyclicality, which could subtract in a downturn.
C-80

To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

D4425th of 35

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: yes, organic growth has decelerated below SPIE's own normal rate, but the deceleration sits in legacy and exogenous parts (French fibre and building, Middle East, weather), not in the input force (grid investment), which is accelerating. Q2 2026 suggests the trough has passed.

  • The deceleration. Organic growth 4.3% (2024), 2.0% (2025), 1.2% (H1 2026); the last twelve months to June 2026 ran about 1.4%, below the 2015 to 2025 average of 1.8% and well below the 3% to 4% target. Revenue growth on all sources slowed from +13.7% (2024) to +4.8% (2025) and +3.6% (H1 2026).
  • Where it came from. France (-1.6% in 2025; -0.7% in H1 2026; fibre run-off about 1 pp; building selectivity); Global Services Energy (-4.4% in 2025; -6.7% in H1 2026); Central Europe's weather-hit Q1 (-8.2%); German high-voltage phasing in Q3 2025.
  • The input force. German organic growth +5.3% (2025), +7.3% (Q2 2026); a record order book; TSO capex rising; data-centre and nuclear demand intact.
  • Risk. If Q3 2026 (30 Oct) shows Germany below about 5% while France stays negative, the market will read the Q2 rebound as weather catch-up only; the Leadership and Risks reports name organic growth falling below 1.5% as the trigger for a working-capital re-run.

Forecasts and upgrades

Probably upgrades this financial year to

D3919th of 36

Memo topic 7, Scope for upgrades over 6 to 12 months: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

The fourth set as a whole

C-4416th of 35

Layer 4 · The fourth set as a whole

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The gap between our assessment and what guidance, consensus and the narrative reflect is unfavourable for the next six to nine months and favourable thereafter, and the two halves have different causes. Guidance reflects less than we expect on EBITA and cash and will be met and probably firmed, which is favourable but already assumed by a sell-side with 85% buy ratings. Consensus reflects more than we expect on EPS for FY26 and FY27, by 7% to 9% on the company's basis, for three mechanical reasons (shares issued, coupons contracted, tax enacted) that two of nine houses have absorbed and the median has not; the cuts will arrive between the 30 October Q3 release and the 5 March 2027 results, with negative revision breadth, at the same time as the operating prints confirm the case. The narrative knows the German grid story and the French discount thoroughly, the EPS bridge partially, and the ROFA price and the margin-organic coupling not at all; investors, unobservable directly, have already de-rated the stock 20% on the French factor without waiting for any of this. Beyond 18 months the gap turns favourable: consensus models no acquisitions, sits below the company's 2028 EBITA target, and has no capital-return option, while the Capital Markets Day, the RP5 decision and falling leverage all point the other way. For the memo's lifecycle topic, SPIE is bottoming on the multiple, turning up at EBITA, and about to stall for two quarters at EPS; the thesis broadens only after March 2027 (ROFA verified, EPS rebased) and more clearly after the election. For the fair-multiple and return topics, the model's guideline 10.49x already sits in the ten-year crash-to-conservative band, so the +4.8% 18-month and +20.3% three-year returns are trough-multiple returns; at the ten-year median of 11.8x the 18-month value would be about EUR 46 to 48 rather than EUR 43, and the memo should show that sensitivity, because the multiple, not the earnings, is where this case's reward and its timing both sit.

I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

B+624th of 35

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement: guidance will be met at every event over the next 18 months with about 85% probability and raised, in SPIE's form of raising (a floor firmed up during the year, then a new mid-term roadmap), at two of them with about even odds: the H1 2027 results in July 2027 and the 2027 Capital Markets Day. The probability of a reduction is about 5% at any single event and about 10% to 15% cumulatively, and would need a French shock combined with a project loss. The reason the odds are this favourable is that SPIE guides only on the lines it controls (growth, margin, cash, leverage); it does not guide on EPS, so the near-certain EPS cuts in consensus (I-02) and a stream of "met or raised" guidance will coincide. Of the 2028 targets, margin (8.0%) and EBITA (above EUR 1bn) are more likely to be met or beaten than not, cumulative FCF (above EUR 2bn) will be beaten by a wide margin, and the organic average (3% to 4% over 2025 to 2028) is more likely to be missed and reframed than met; the Capital Markets Day will, as in March 2026, raise the margin and cash ambitions and quietly restate the organic one.

  1. The record that sets the base rate. The 2025 margin target (6.7%) was reached in 2023; FY24's "at least 7.1%" was firmed at Q3 2024 and delivered at 7.2%; FY25's "at least 7.6%" was firmed at H1 2025, 20 bps above consensus, and delivered exactly; the 2028 target was raised from "at least 7.7%" to 8.0% one year after being set; cash conversion printed 122% and 108% against a 100% guide. Organic growth is the exception: 1.8% a year against repeated 3% to 4% ambitions. Louette's rule was "when we give guidance, we stick to it"; Holzke's one data point (July 2026) was a reiteration despite a margin beat and the strongest H1 cash flow in the company's history, which suggests a CEO who will firm later and raise less often, not one who will reduce.
  2. Event by event. (a) Q3 2026 sales, 30 October 2026. Expected: organic about 2.5% to 3% (Germany about 6%, France about -0.5%, North-Western Europe about 3.8%, Central Europe about 8%, Global Services Energy about -10%), scope about +8.8%, revenue about EUR 2.8bn. Guidance reiterated: about 60%; FY26 margin firmed to "at least 7.8%" or similar: about 35% (the Q3 2024 precedent, and H2 is where 60% of EBITA lands); reduced: about 5% (would need France below -2% or a German surprise). (b) FY26 results, 5 March 2027. Delivery: margin about 7.8% to 7.9% against the "continued expansion" guide (met, about 90%); cash conversion above 100% (met, about 90%); leverage about 1.7x (met). New FY27 guidance: qualitative "strong growth, continued margin expansion, about 100% cash conversion" (about 75%); a quantified floor (about 20%); a cautious or reduced tone (about 5%, the French election quarter being the reason given). This is also the event at which the ROFA purchase price allocation, the tax guidance for 2027 and the net financial run-rate are disclosed; none of those is "guidance" in SPIE's vocabulary, but they are the numbers the market will trade. (c) Q1 2027 sales, late April 2027. Optically strong organic growth against Q1 2026's -0.9%; guidance reiterated (about 90%). (d) H1 2027 results, late July 2027. Margin about 6.5% (+30 bps, half from ROFA's flatter seasonality); the pattern (July 2025) says a firming of FY27 margin guidance is the most likely raise point: about 50%. The risk is the opposite reading in H2 2027, when the margin is flat at about 9.2% because the seasonality effect reverses; the Consumer should pre-empt this in the memo. (e) 2027 Capital Markets Day (date not announced; most likely H2 2027). A 2030 roadmap: margin ambition above 8% (perhaps "at least 8.5%"), EBITA well above EUR 1bn, a higher cumulative FCF target, leverage policy unchanged, possibly a capital-return statement. Probability that the mid-term ambition is raised on margin and cash: about 65%; that the organic ambition is restated to a lower or wider range: about 55%; that targets are set below what the market expects: about 15% (Holzke's first roadmap as group CEO could be deliberately conservative, which the "floor and beat" record would then make a buying point). (f) Q3 2027 sales and FY27 results (October 2027; March 2028). The 2028 targets become one year away; "at least 8.0%" firming is likely by March 2028 (about 60%).
  3. The 2028 targets, one by one. Margin 8.0%: my 8.05%; Bernstein 8.2%; consensus 7.9%; met or beaten about 70%, with the shortfall case being organic growth stuck near 2% and ROFA's margin falling in an automotive downturn (D-01, point 3). EBITA above EUR 1bn: my EUR 1,042m with EUR 350m a year of bolt-ons, about EUR 1,005m to 1,015m without them, consensus EUR 988m; met about 80%, but the margin of safety without new deals is thin, which is itself a reason management will keep buying. Total growth 7% to 9% a year: met about 60% (my 7.3% is at the bottom; without bolt-ons it is below the range). Organic 3% to 4% average over 2025 to 2028: my average about 2.6%; met about 30%. Cumulative FCF above EUR 2bn: my about EUR 2.4bn; beaten about 95%. Leverage below 2.0x at year-ends: met about 95%.
  4. What would drive a raise. German organic growth sustained above 6% as TenneT's capex triples; Central Europe and North-Western Europe margins normalising; ROFA and SGS consolidating at their stated margins; a strong H2 2026 cash print; the floor-and-beat habit itself.
  5. What would drive a reduction. A French political or fiscal shock deferring private capex in Q4 2026 or Q1 to Q2 2027 (France -2% to -3%), combined with a project loss in Dutch high-voltage or German data-centre work or an automotive capex freeze hitting ROFA; a sudden-shock decremental margin of 27% would then apply. A weather-hit Q1 2027 is not a reduction risk (the 2026 precedent was absorbed).
  6. The secondary horizon (18 months to three years). The 2030 roadmap will be set in 2027 against a backdrop of TSO capex peaking in 2028 to 2030, RP5 returns finalised in late 2027, the surcharge's fate decided by the election, and leverage approaching 1x. The likelier error is that management sets the 2030 ambition too low (its habit), not too high; a margin ambition at or below 8.5% and an organic ambition of 3% would be "met" with high probability and raised in 2028 or 2029. The scope for a genuinely new element, a capital-return policy beyond dilution offset, is the one that would change the case's shape; Morgan Stanley reports management saying other measures are "not off the table" once the pipeline is satisfied; I give it about 30% by the Capital Markets Day and about 50% by 2029.
  7. Against our assessment. My FY26 to FY28 EBITA path sits at or slightly above the company's targets, my cash path well above, my organic path below. The gap between our assessment and guidance is therefore favourable on the lines guidance covers, and the company's behaviour (floors, firming, raising mid-term ambitions once reached) makes that gap likely to be acknowledged in public rather than merely delivered in private. The limitation is that this is the gap the sell-side already expects (85% buy ratings; Bernstein at 7.9% for 2026), so a "met or raised" outcome is partly priced into estimates, if not into the share price.

I-02

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

D3022nd of 35

Layer 4 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: consensus EPS will be cut and consensus EBITA held or nudged up, at the same events, for the next two reporting rounds; the net breadth of EPS revisions, which has been flat to positive for twelve months (FY27 +4%; EBITDA +9%), will turn negative within three months and stay negative into mid-2027, and the dashboard's sell-side momentum letter will fall from C to D. The cuts are mechanical, not operational: about 2% to 3% for FY26 and about 5% to 7% for FY27 as seven or eight of the eleven brokers update their share counts for the ORNANE conversion and their interest lines for the two bonds, with a further 2% to 3% for those that choose to deduct the surcharge. FY27 consensus will settle near EUR 3.25 to 3.30 on the company basis, about 1% to 2% above my company-basis figure and 4% to 5% above the model's economic figure, so most of the gap closes from the consensus side. Beyond 18 months the direction reverses: FY28 and FY29 consensus EBITA and EPS are more likely to be raised than cut, because consensus models no acquisitions after 2026, sits below the company's EUR 1bn 2028 EBITA target, and will have to absorb a 2030 roadmap.

  1. Where consensus stands and why it is stale. FY26, FY27 and FY28 EPS medians of EUR 3.11, 3.46 and 3.73 (means 3.09, 3.44, 3.66); eleven brokers on revenue and EBIT, nine to eleven on EPS; FY29 rests on one broker and should be ignored. Revisions to FY27: EPS 0% (one month), -1% (three months), 0% (six months), +4% (twelve months); EBITDA +9% over twelve months; sales +4%; target price -2% over one month; buy share 85%, up 18 points over three months (the Jefferies upgrade, 10 September 2026) and down 7 over one month; median target EUR 57 against a price of EUR 40.76. The individual FY27 estimates show which models have been updated: Deutsche Bank (EUR 3.02, 14 October, after the refinancing, deducting IFRS 2 charges) and the unnamed 3 September estimate (3.24) are the only two below EUR 3.37; Bernstein (3.47 to 3.49, 19 to 20 October, "estimates unchanged despite refinancing"), Oddo (3.72), Morgan Stanley (3.63, 31 July, before the refinancing, built on a ROFA price far below the one paid), RBC (3.55, the only Hold), Jefferies (3.46), Portzamparc (3.44), UBS (3.42) and CIC (3.37) all carry pre-October share counts and interest lines. The T-I1 report's conclusion ("most brokers continue to run outdated capital structures") is confirmed by the numbers.
  2. The mechanics of the coming cuts. (a) Share count: 3,365,843 ORNANE shares (2.0%) from 20 October 2026, plus about 1.8m from the December employee plan less about 1.5m bought back in spring 2027: about -2% to FY27 EPS for any model still at about 169m to 170m shares. (b) Interest: Deutsche Bank's EUR 15m of annual extra interest from the ORNANE refinancing alone is about -2% of pre-tax profit and about -2% of EPS; adding the EUR 600m 3.875% bond's full-year effect where it is missing takes it to -3% to -4%. (c) Tax: Morgan Stanley has already cut FY26 by 5% for the surcharge; Bernstein and Morgan Stanley model it "ad infinitum" for French contractors; the FY26 results will show it extended into 2027, and brokers that follow the company's add-back will leave their adjusted EPS unchanged but will have to explain a lower reported figure. (d) Offsets: H2 2026 margin strength (Bernstein's 7.9%), SGS and Clauser consolidation, and a slightly higher EBITA line. Net: FY26 EPS to about EUR 3.00 to 3.05 (-2% to -3%); FY27 to about EUR 3.25 to 3.30 (-5% to -6%); FY28 to about EUR 3.60 to 3.70 (-1% to -3%, with M&A additions offsetting). My model: FY27 EUR 3.16 economic (about 3.10 after the input changes), about 3.26 company basis.
  3. Timing. First wave at and after the 30 October Q3 release, when models are rolled and the 20 October share count is entered: expect three to five FY27 cuts of 2% to 5% within two weeks, and a consensus drift of about -2% to -3% by mid-November. Second wave at the 5 March 2027 results, when the company discloses net financial items, the surcharge, the share count and the ROFA purchase price allocation: expect the remaining models to converge and FY27 consensus to reach about EUR 3.25 to 3.30 by April 2027. Third wave at the H1 2027 results, when ROFA's first full half and the H1 margin step are visible: expect FY27 to be held and FY28 to be raised as bolt-ons are added. The 2027 Capital Markets Day then resets FY28 to FY30.
  4. Breadth and the dashboard. With nine to eleven contributors and seven or eight needing to cut, the net breadth of EPS revisions will be clearly negative (perhaps -60% to -80% net) over the next three months, the first negative stretch since the ORNANE-era confusion of 2025 (the FY27 series ranged from EUR 1.77 to 3.49). EBITDA and sales breadth will stay neutral to positive. Target prices will drift from EUR 57 towards EUR 50 to 54 as EPS is cut and discount rates are raised (Bernstein cut its DCF value 5.7% on 12 October for a 4.95% cost of debt and a 30 bps higher WACC). The buy share could slip from 85% to about 70% to 75% if Morgan Stanley (Overweight since April 2026, with the highest FY27 estimate after Oddo) or one of the unnamed houses moves to neutral on a tax-and-interest cut; RBC is already at Hold with a EUR 45 target. The dashboard's revision-momentum score (-1) and earnings momentum letter (C) will worsen to about -5 and D.
  5. The nature of the cuts, which matters for the share price. These are cuts to the EPS line with unchanged or rising EBITA, for reasons the company can explain in a single slide (coupons, shares, tax). The precedent is Deutsche Bank's own note (15 October: -4% FY27 EPS, Buy reiterated, target EUR 57): the house that cut did not change its view. The risk is that a market which has already de-rated the stock 20% on France reads the first consensus EPS cuts in a year as the start of an operating problem; the mitigant is that the operating prints (Q3 organic, H2 margin, FCF) will be good at the same events. A second, opposite optical effect arrives on 5 March 2027: reported EPS roughly doubles (EUR 1.05 to about 2.13) as the ORNANE fair-value charge disappears, which screens will show as "+100% EPS growth" while adjusted consensus is cut; generalists and quantitative buyers may respond to the former.
  6. The secondary horizon (18 months to three years): more likely up than down. Three reasons. (a) Consensus FY28 EBITA (EUR 988m) is below the company's "above EUR 1bn" target and below my EUR 1,042m because brokers model no deals after 2026; every bolt-on announced in 2027 (SPIE has bought every year since 2013; Clauser in October 2026 is the first of the next cohort) adds to FY28. Deutsche Bank's EUR 2.4bn of deployable capital over two and a half years and Jefferies' EUR 1.5bn to 2028 are the sell-side's own unmodelled upside, worth 12% to 15% EPS CAGR against 6% to 8% organic. (b) The 2027 Capital Markets Day will give the sell-side a 2030 framework; the March 2026 precedent (margin target raised one year after being set) says the framework will be set to be beaten. (c) The capital-return option as leverage falls towards 1x is in no model. Against these: the surcharge becoming permanent (-2.7% of FY28 EPS), German automotive capex falling after 2028 (ROFA), and acquisition multiples rising. On balance, FY28 to FY29 consensus EPS is more likely to be 3% to 6% higher in two years than lower, after an interim dip.
  7. Against our assessment. Near term the gap is unfavourable: our EPS is below consensus and consensus is going to come down to meet it, with negative breadth, at the very events where guidance is met. Beyond 18 months the gap is favourable: our EBITA and FCF are above consensus, and consensus will rise towards them as deals are announced and the roadmap is set. The memo should present the consensus path as a dip and recovery, with the dip dated (November 2026 to April 2027) and its cause named as financing and tax, and the recovery dated to the H1 2027 results and the Capital Markets Day.

Current multiple

C5721st of 36Memo topic 11, The fair multiple: the Rater's score1 answer
D-0732
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

F3232nd of 35

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: SPIE's share price has moved mostly on its own over the last six months (the dashboard peer basket explains 22% of daily variance, down from 35% in August; correlation 0.47; beta 0.71; SPIE -14.6% against the basket +5.0%), and the idiosyncratic 78% is not company fundamentals but a country factor: Deutsche Bank measures a -0.88 correlation between SPIE's valuation and French ten-year yields in 2026 and a 20% compression in the forward P/E since June while the European infrastructure-services peer average was flat. The multiple is therefore neither company-driven nor industry-driven; it is French-sovereign-driven, which for the memo is the worst of the three for predictability (an exogenous, binary variable with an April 2027 date) and the best for asymmetry (the de-rating is unrelated to delivery, so delivery is not what reverses it, and political clarity could reverse it without any change in earnings).

  1. The co-movement evidence. Rolling six-month R-squared against the ten dashboard peers: 30% (November 2024), 14% (October 2025, the trough), 33% to 35% (March to August 2026), 22% (23 October 2026). Beta: about 1.0 two years ago, 0.71 now. Pairwise, the highest correlations are with Bilfinger (0.33), Instalco (0.33), MBB (0.31) and Bravida (0.30), and the lowest with Fasadgruppen (0.05), Arcadis (0.11) and Renew (0.12); no single peer explains more than 11% of SPIE's variance. The six-month return gap (-14.6% against +5.0%) is the largest divergence in the 24-month series, and it opened after June 2026, when the basket was lifted by Bravida (+63.5% over twelve months) and Instalco (+67%) and SPIE was pulled down by France.
  2. What the idiosyncratic 78% is. Three candidates, with the evidence for each. (a) French sovereign risk: Deutsche Bank's -0.88 correlation; Jefferies' finding that VINCI (-12% year to date) and Eiffage (-20%) track OAT spreads; Bernstein's analysis of the 2024 snap-election sell-off (VINCI -15.4%, Eiffage -20.4%, consensus earnings down only 0.7% and 1.6%); Morgan Stanley's note that SPIE and Elis de-rated on French political risk; Rexel at a 38% discount to capital-goods peers with 19% French revenue. This explains most of it. (b) Sector rotation: money moving from steady compounders to volume inflections (Bravida, Instalco) and to equipment makers (ABB, Siemens Energy) rather than installers; this explains why SPIE did not rise with the basket, not why it fell. (c) Company-specific fundamentals: FY27 consensus EPS is unchanged over six months, EBITDA consensus is +2%, the buy share is 85%; nothing company-specific has been revised down. So the multiple's fall is not a verdict on the forecasts; it is a verdict on the postcode.
  3. How far the multiple is company-driven. Very little in the last six months. Over longer windows SPIE's multiple did respond to delivery (the five-year median of 12.3x and two-year median of 13.7x were reached as margins rose from 5.1% to 7.6%), and the ten-year percentile of the current P/E (49th) says the stock is at its own long-run average, not at a crash level, once the French factor is netted out. The dashboard's valuation letter C and the model's guideline fair multiple of 10.49x (between the ten-year 10th percentile "crash" band at 9.9x and the 35th "conservative" at 11.2x) both encode a near-trough French-risk multiple.
  4. Implications for predictability and for the memo. (a) Over the next six months the share price will be set by OAT spreads, the 2027 budget's passage in December and the election run-up, not by Q3 sales or the FY26 results; delivery of my EBITA path will not, on its own, re-rate the stock, and an EPS cut at the March results could compound the French discount if it is read as fundamental weakness. (b) Over 6 to 18 months the April to May 2027 election is a binary for the multiple: a stabilising outcome would release a re-rating of perhaps 10% to 20% (back to the five-year or two-year median) with no change to earnings; an unstable one would hold the multiple near 10x or lower. (c) The French factor can be hedged, which is a point for the memo's risk topic: SPIE against Eiffage (70% French revenue) or VINCI (45%) isolates the German grid and acquisition case from the sovereign case. (d) The low beta to peers (0.71) means a sector de-rating (a Bravida project write-down, a data-centre financing freeze) would hurt SPIE less than its peers, and a sector re-rating would help it less.
  5. Peer read. Bravida and Instalco are the sector's momentum names (technical B, earnings A and B, valuation D and C) and have decoupled upward; Bilfinger decoupled downward on its own warning (-42% over twelve months); Renew, with the purest regulated-maintenance model, is flat with a record order book and a 100% buy share. SPIE sits with Renew in the "evidence improved, shares did not" group, with the difference that SPIE's drag has a name and a date.

Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the ratings map uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

B+586th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: SPIE fits the operational pattern of outperformers (scarce crews on repricing contracts, growing customer assets, self-funded consolidation, cash discipline) but not the share-price pattern of the last two years, which rewarded volume inflections and turnarounds rather than steady compounders. The precedents say its main risks are execution on large projects and acquisition-led growth masking organic weakness, and its main upside is the multiple re-rating once rising estimates are believed.

  • Rules of thumb (Sector and Industry Digests). Outperformers own scarce crews on contracts that reprice, serving customers whose assets grow (life-extended nuclear and grid are "decades of work"; chemical plants that close are a utilisation problem escalation cannot fix). "Rising estimates under a flat multiple is the setup where the multiple does the work once the scarcity story is believed." "In a building turn, volume returning to a pruned cost base drives earnings" (Bravida, Instalco 2026). "Regulated, back-end-loaded money pays design first and contractors later, and rewards visibility with a multiple, not a margin." "A subcontractor-heavy cost base leaks the scarcity margin to the sub-tier."
  • Financial outperformance precedents. SPIE itself: margin +260 bps (2020 to 2025) through selectivity and mix; cash conversion above 100% for a decade; second-fastest EPS growth and second-fewest downgrades in its peer group over five years (Deutsche Bank). Comfort Systems USA: data-centre work compounding backlog and margin.
  • Underperformance precedents. SPIE 2015 to 2020 (SAG dilution, leverage 3.3x, 2017 UK write-down); Bilfinger 2026 (framework call-offs collapsed; shares -44% over twelve months); Caverion and Eltel (project write-downs of EUR 59m and EUR 55m); Fasadgruppen (roll-up with about 1% organic growth, leverage to 5.3x, rights issue, -56% over twelve months); Instalco 2022 to 2025 (margin from 8% to 5.9%, leverage 3.3x).
  • Fit. Operationally SPIE matches the outperformer template (grid crews, indexation, negative working capital, investment grade), and its acquired growth is funded from cash, not debt or equity, unlike Fasadgruppen and Instalco. On share price it is the Industry Digest's example of the unrewarded "second tier" (-13% over three months, about 13x earnings) while peers with volume inflections re-rated (Bravida +63.5%, Instalco +67%). Its structural weakness against the template is organic growth (below the peer median in 18 of 33 quarters).

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

B+665th of 35

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: about 15% to 20% to a fair trough (EUR 32 to 35), most of it from the multiple; 25% to 30% only in a severe case that combines a crash multiple with a 15% EPS cut. The shares have already given up more than half of the distance from their two-year median multiple to the crash band.

  • Starting point. EUR 40.76; 24-month-forward P/E 11.55x, implying forward EPS of about EUR 3.53; 28th percentile of five years (median 12.34x), 16th of two years (median 13.71x), about the median of ten years (Master Dashboard bands: 10th percentile 9.9x "crash", 35th 11.2x "conservative", median 11.8x "prudent", 75th 12.9x "defendable").
  • Fair trough multiple. About 9.9x to 10x (ten-year 10th percentile; five-year low 9.73x). Jefferies' downside case used 12.5x on depressed earnings (EUR 35); Morgan Stanley's bear case (flat organic, margin to 5%) gives EUR 32.
  • Scenarios. (1) Multiple to 9.9x on unchanged EPS: EUR 34.9 (-14%). (2) Multiple to 9.9x with EPS 8% lower (ORNANE dilution, interest and tax about 4%; weaker France and organic about 4%): EUR 32.2 (-21%). (3) Severe: 9.7x with EPS 15% lower (French recession plus a 60 bps margin miss): EUR 29.1 (-29%).
  • Reference upside. Back to the five-year median (12.3x) on consensus: about EUR 43.5 (+7%); to the two-year median (13.7x): about EUR 48 (+18%); broker median target EUR 57.
  • Why the trough multiple is "fair". The business is better than in the years that set the ten-year low (margin +260 bps, leverage far lower, investment grade), so a sub-10x multiple would need a lasting French or macro shock.

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

D4718th of 35

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The narrative ahead is a sequence of confirmations rather than surprises: organic growth turning (Q3 2026, FY26), German public money reaching revenue (2027), French drag fading, acquisitions proving accretive, and the 2028 targets being met. Each step is reasonably well-founded and together they support rising estimates at the operating level; the market has, however, stopped paying for rising estimates (Sector Digest: "evidence improved, shares did not"), so the narrative needs a catalyst that changes the risk premium, which most plausibly comes from French political clarity after spring 2027, a visibly faster German organic rate, or a capital-return step. Until then the staircase supports the earnings case more than the share price.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–70

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: highly congruent on the operating picture, and congruent by design on EPS, because the second set built its below-EBITA case on the first set's own findings and the four AlphaSense template reports and six commissioned reports corroborate almost every operating claim. The incongruities that remain are few, and three of them matter: (1) the price paid for ROFA, which the whole sell-side accepts as a "high single-digit multiple" while the company's own disclosed consideration implies something near twice that; (2) the first set's separation of "conservative margin guidance" from "demanding organic guidance", which the second set's drop-through mechanism shows to be one coupled question; and (3) the second set's 50/50 assumption that the French surcharge lapses after 2027, which the surcharge report now contradicts. None of the three breaks the case; each moves it in the same direction, towards lower EPS and lower returns on new capital than consensus assumes, with unchanged EBITA.

  1. Where the sets align, and the evidence that confirms them. The first set's six cross-cutting judgements (margin-plus-acquisitions engine; organic trough turning; EBITA path to EUR 1bn well founded, EPS exposed; de-rating caused by France and the regime, not earnings; ROFA adds cyclicality at an unverified price; the "FALSE FRIEND" verdict carries little weight) are each taken up by the second set and each is confirmed by the AlphaSense reports: organic growth averaged 1.3% to 1.8% over 2015 to 2025 with price two-thirds of post-pandemic organic growth (Morgan Stanley, May 2024); Equans' discipline and a rational pricing environment (Morgan Stanley, September 2026; Bouygues raised Equans' FY26 margin target to at least 5.2%); the German grid backlog of two to three years and lead times of 128 to 160 weeks for transformers (Bernstein; Morgan Stanley); the H1 2026 margin beat and FCF turning positive; the below-EBITA drags (Deutsche Bank's EUR 15m of extra interest and -4% FY27 EPS from the ORNANE refinancing; Morgan Stanley's -5% FY26 EPS for tax). The second set's deliberate departures from the first set (an all-in tax rate of 32.5% to 33% rather than 30% to 31%; year-end 2026 net debt about EUR 1.74bn; a 27% decremental margin in a sudden shock; a 7% to 10% EPS gap to consensus rather than 2% to 4%) are all refinements in one direction and are consistent with the evidence in the own-2, own-3 and T-D2 reports. I agree with each of them.
  2. Incongruity one: the ROFA price, and what the sell-side's accretion arithmetic reveals. The first set computed that EUR 682.8m for 100% of a business with about EUR 430m of revenue at a "high single-digit" margin (EBITA about EUR 34m to 40m) is 17x to 20x EBITA, not the "high single-digit multiple" the company and Bernstein cite. The own-1 report adds the missing piece: Morgan Stanley's accretion note (2 March 2026) assumed a transaction value of EUR 250m to 330m and only 0.2x of added leverage (about EUR 220m to 250m of EBITDA-equivalent debt), which is exactly 7x to 9x on EUR 35m to 37m of EBITA. The consideration disclosed in the H1 2026 report four months later (EUR 611.8m for 89.7% plus EUR 71.0m for 10.3%) is roughly double the sum the "mid-single-digit EPS accretion" was built on. My reconciliation of the H1 cash flow (net debt up EUR 822m in H1 2026 after FCF of +EUR 25.7m, the final FY25 dividend of about EUR 129m and the EUR 59m buyback) puts net acquisition outflows at about EUR 660m against about EUR 765m to 780m of gross consideration across all H1 deals, so cash acquired across all of them was only about EUR 100m to 120m. Unless the FY26 purchase price allocation shows ROFA carrying far more cash or customer advances than that, ROFA's enterprise value is about EUR 550m to 630m, roughly 15x to 18x EBITA, and first-year EPS accretion after a 4% funding cost is about 1%, not 5%. The sell-side has not reconciled this; no house discusses it (see I-03). It is the single largest unverified number in the case, and it is resolved on 5 March 2027.
  3. Incongruity two: margin guidance and organic guidance are one question, not two. The first set judged the 8.0% margin target "conservative" (13 bps a year against 40 bps delivered) and the 3% to 4% organic target "demanding". The second set's P-01 shows the mechanism that links them: at an 11% to 12% incremental margin, 3% organic growth adds about 11 bps a year and supplies most of the 36 bps needed by 2028; at the 1.8% historical rate the target is missed by about 15 bps. So margin guidance is conservative only conditional on organic delivery; the independent sources of margin (acquired mix from ROFA and SGS, the German grid mix, Central Europe's recovery) are worth perhaps 15 to 20 bps and would carry 8.0% only just. I side with the second set's coupling, with one addition the Consumer should use: the organic target is an average of 3% to 4% over 2025 to 2028, and with 2.0% delivered in 2025 and about 2.0% likely in 2026, 2027 and 2028 would need to average 4% to 6% to meet it. On my base case (3.0% and 3.3%) the four-year average is about 2.6%. The organic target is therefore likely to be missed or quietly reframed at the 2027 Capital Markets Day, while the margin target is met through mix. This matters for I-01.
  4. Incongruity three: the surcharge. The second set treated the lapse of the French exceptional contribution after 2027 as a 50/50 call. The own-2 report shows that the 2026 Finance Act extended it, the draft 2027 Finance Bill (1 October 2026) extends it again for companies with French revenue above EUR 3bn (SPIE France: EUR 3.35bn), Morgan Stanley and Bernstein now model it "ad infinitum", Jefferies embeds it in 2027 and 2028, and fiscal analysts (CaixaBank, Amundi, Deutsche Bank's May 2026 note on the EUR 7bn windfall) judge termination improbable while the deficit stays above 5% of GDP; the 2011 "temporary" surcharge lasted until 2016. Only a Philippe-led government has committed to scrapping it. I put persistence into FY28 at about 60% and change the FY28 to FY30 tax rate accordingly.
  5. Smaller incongruities, for the record. (a) The AlphaSense reports quote SPIE's long-run organic growth as 1.3% (Deutsche Bank, Morgan Stanley: a longer window) and 1.8% (Deutsche Bank, 2015 to 2025): both are right for their periods; use 1.8% for the ten-year comparison. (b) The T-I1 report's executive summary says Bernstein expects a 7.9% margin "as early as 2026", above the 7.8% consensus and the second set's 7.84%; this is one house's view, but it is the direction in which the record leans. (c) The own-6 report repeats Morgan Stanley's "EUR 250m to 330m" consideration as if it were the price; it is the broker's inference from the stated multiple, not a disclosure. (d) The first set's C-83 fair trough multiple (9.9x to 10x) and the model's guideline fair multiple (10.49x, the ten-year 10th-to-35th percentile band) are consistent with each other: the model's 18-month return of +4.8% is a near-trough-multiple return, which the memo should say plainly.
  6. What the congruence means for predictability. Because the sets agree on the operating engine and disagree only in degree below EBITA, the predictability question is cleanly separable: revenue, EBITA and cash are the well-founded, cross-confirmed part; EPS and return on new capital are the part where the sets have moved steadily in one direction (down against consensus) as each layer of evidence arrived, and where the remaining unknowns (ROFA's balance sheet, the final 2027 surcharge rate, the net financial run-rate) are all dated to the 5 March 2027 results.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

D4022nd of 35

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: the sell-side narrative articulates the positive operating dynamics and the French discount comprehensively, articulates the below-EBITA costs partially (two of nine houses have modelled them; the median has not), and does not articulate at all the two findings that most distinguish our assessment: that ROFA's disclosed price is roughly twice the sum its "mid-single-digit accretion" was computed on, and that the margin target and the organic target are one coupled question in which organic is likely to be missed. Investor commentary is effectively unobservable (the T-I2 report, which claims 100% coverage of the nine houses, found no X/Twitter or investor-letter material), so the investor narrative must be inferred from behaviour: a stock that has de-rated 20% on flat estimates, with 85% buy ratings, 40% United States long-only ownership and relative strength in the 17th percentile, is being sold by owners who are not waiting for the sell-side's EPS cuts and are not yet paying for the German grid story. The dynamics that matter most for the next 18 months are therefore known in the wrong order: the French discount is over-known, the German grid story is well known but unrewarded, and the EPS bridge and the ROFA price are under-known.

  1. Coverage and the limits of the evidence. Nine houses cover SPIE (Bernstein, Morgan Stanley, Deutsche Bank, Jefferies, RBC, Oddo BHF, CIC, UBS, Portzamparc); the T-I2 report used 105 of 110 documents and credits views only to documents it cites, but admits the pool "did not contain specific research from UBS, CIC, or RBC detailing their adjustment methodologies", so for four houses (RBC, UBS, CIC, Oddo) the only evidence of what they think is their FactSet numbers (FY27 EPS EUR 3.37 to 3.72; all unadjusted for October). Investor commentary: none found. The practical consequence is that "the narrative" for SPIE is four houses (Bernstein, Morgan Stanley, Deutsche Bank, Jefferies) plus the expert-call network, and the memo should say so.
  2. Dynamic by dynamic, who discusses it and how well it is known.
Dynamic Who, since when, with what view How well known

Rising German installer multiples DNB Carnegie (not a SPIE house: Brookfield's 15 installers), Bernstein (SGS, Robur, Otto in the high single digits), Morgan Stanley (6x to 7x bolt-ons) Known at the sector level; its effect on SPIE's return on new capital is not modelled except by Deutsche Bank
  1. Two dynamics that are not on the T-I2 list and are not known. (a) The coupling of the margin target to organic growth through drop-through, and the likelihood that the 3% to 4% organic average over 2025 to 2028 is missed and reframed at the Capital Markets Day while the margin target is met through mix (D-01, point 3): no house frames it this way; Bernstein's "organic growth remains the hardest part" is the nearest. (b) The company's exclusion of the surcharge from its headline adjusted EPS, which means a EUR 15m to 20m a year cash cost sits outside the number most brokers forecast; only the houses that deduct it (Morgan Stanley, Deutsche Bank) see it, and the own-2 report had to establish the treatment from the interim report's notes.
  2. The investor narrative, inferred. Ownership: employee funds 8.0% of capital (12.8% of votes), BlackRock about 8%, United States long-only funds about 40% after Peugeot Invest and Bpifrance left; no activist; no disclosed short above 0.5%. Behaviour: price -13% over three months and -14.6% over six against a peer basket +5.0%; 50-day below the 150- and 200-day; relative strength percentile 17; forward P/E down 20% since June on flat estimates. Reading: owners have sold SPIE as a French proxy (the "postcode trade"), not because of anything in the company's numbers, and have not been persuaded by Jefferies' upgrade or Bernstein's top-pick status. The absence of a visible retail or social-media narrative means there is no crowd to disappoint and no crowd to recruit; the marginal buyer is an institutional generalist who will respond to the French election, to the reported-EPS normalisation in March, and to the 2027 roadmap, in that order.
  3. What this means for the next 18 months. The dynamics that will drive the next two results events are known asymmetrically. The positives (Q3 organic, H2 margin, FCF, German acceleration) are fully known, so they will be "in line" at best. The EPS cuts are known to two houses and unknown to the median, so they will register as news. The ROFA price will become visible with the purchase price allocation on 5 March 2027 and is known to no one. The French factor is known to everyone and is the only one with a catalyst date. The order of discovery is therefore: EPS cuts (November to March), ROFA (March), election (April to May), roadmap (H2 2027). The memo should expect the narrative to get worse before it gets better, and should set the entry case around that sequence rather than around the operating confirmations.
  4. 18 months to three years. The unmodelled M&A (Deutsche Bank, Jefferies) and the 2030 roadmap are known as "upside scenarios" by the two houses that quantify them, and the RP5 regulatory step-up, TSO capex to 2030 and the fund's 2027 disbursement are fully known; the capital-return option is not known; the surcharge's permanence is known to the French-contractor analysts and not to the installer analysts. The longer-horizon narrative is therefore better aligned with our assessment than the 18-month one, which is the usual shape of a case whose near-term problem is accounting rather than operating.
