Each layer's own material

What each layer was asked, what its scores mean, and the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 25 October 2026, 11:26 to 11:47 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
SPIE SA (SPIE-FR): answers to the first set of questions (foundational components)

Written 25-Oct-26 for the Investment Analyst answering the second set (the operating and financial model, 18 months to four years).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

How to read these answers, and the conventions used

  • Money and bases. EUR. "EBITA" is the company's adjusted EBITA (FY25 EUR 793.5m, 7.6% margin), which excludes amortisation of acquired intangibles and IFRS 2 share-based charges (EUR 59.8m in FY25). "EPS" means the brokers' adjusted basis (FY25 actual EUR 2.69; FactSet consensus EUR 3.11 FY26, 3.46 FY27, 3.73 FY28 at 21-Oct-26) unless stated. Reported basic EPS was EUR 1.05 in FY25 (depressed by a EUR 132.9m non-cash ORNANE fair-value charge). Leverage is the company's net debt excluding IFRS 16 over EBITDA (1.3x end-2025, 2.1x end-June 2026) unless labelled FactSet (which includes leases: 1.88x and 2.80x).
  • Price and valuation. EUR 40.76 on 23-Oct-26; 24-month-forward P/E 11.55x (28th percentile of five years, 16th of two years; about the median of ten years); 52-week low EUR 40.10.
  • Data conflicts in the files that the Consumer should not be tripped by. (1) Germany H1 2026 EBITA margin: the earnings review's company table gives 5.8% against 5.9% a year earlier; one digest says "+20 bps". Use 5.8%. (2) Q2 2026 group organic growth: company 3.1%; one broker 3.3%. Use 3.1%. (3) The brokers'-basis EPS series jumps from EUR 1.44 (FY23) to EUR 2.50 (FY24): this is a basis break, not 74% growth; the peer table's "+74%" for SPIE is an artefact. (4) FactSet's history "free cash flow" line is operating cash flow less capex less dividends (FY25: 803 - 75 - 176 = 552); the company's FCF (EUR 523.7m FY25) deducts leases, interest and tax and not dividends. Never compare the two. (5) The fibre headwind is quoted both as a EUR 40m peak annual revenue decline and as a business of EUR 150m (2025) falling to EUR 120m (2026); these are consistent if read as "decline" and "residual size".

The cross-cutting judgements, as a steer (each is argued in the answers)

  1. SPIE's earnings engine is margin plus acquisitions, not organic volume. Organic growth averaged 1.8% a year over 2015 to 2025 and ranked below the peer median in 18 of 33 quarters; EBITA margin rose 260 bps from 2020 to 2025; acquisitions added 3.2% to revenue in 2025 and about EUR 670m of annualised revenue is being added in 2026. Model the three engines separately.
  2. The organic engine is at a trough and turning, and the turn is better founded than usual because the drag (French fibre run-off, French building selectivity, Middle East suspensions, weather) is either mechanically fading or small, while the driver (German transmission and distribution) is contractually backed for two to three years and publicly funded from 2027. Revenue will lag orders (equipment lead times of 128 to 160 weeks; permitting), so 2027 to 2028 carries more of the acceleration than 2026.
  3. The EBITA path to about EUR 1bn by 2028 is well founded and its cone is narrow (85% of revenue visible at the start of each year, 70% to 75% maintenance, indexation, a labour-variable cost base, an honest-tracker management). The EPS path is where consensus is most exposed: higher interest after two new bonds (EUR 600m at 3.875%; EUR 500m at 4.875%), 3.36m new shares from the October 2026 ORNANE conversion (about 2%), a tax rate drifting above 30%, and share-based dilution of about 1% to 1.5% a year that is only offset by small buybacks. Individual brokers' FY27 EPS spans EUR 3.02 to 3.72 (23%), which signals definitional dispersion; build the EBITA-to-EPS bridge line by line.
  4. The market has de-rated SPIE while estimates held (shares -13% over three months; FY27 EPS revisions flat; buy share 85%). The cause is group- and country-level (French sovereign stress at decade highs, a risk-off regime, money paying "makers of the scarce box" not "the crew"), not a company-specific earnings problem. France is 32% of revenue (48% in 2015), so a whole-ticker French discount overstates the exposure.
  5. The 2026 acquisition step-up changes the mix in a direction the narrative does not discuss: ROFA (about EUR 430m revenue, 80% factory automation, customers in automotive logistics and pharmaceuticals) adds cyclical industrial capex exposure in Germany, the end market where Bilfinger warned in September 2026 and where Western European automotive is shrinking. The stated price (EUR 611.8m for 89.7% plus EUR 71.0m for 10.3%, about EUR 683m for 100%) does not reconcile with the claimed "high single-digit" EBITA multiple if ROFA earns a high single-digit margin (that arithmetic gives roughly 17x to 20x). Treat ROFA's accretion as unverified until the purchase price allocation is published.
  6. The Risks report's "FALSE FRIEND" verdict should carry little weight: the same Analysis of Competing Hypotheses method returned FALSE FRIEND for all four peers in the files (Bravida, Instalco, Burkhalter, Fasadgruppen). It is the vaguest hypothesis and wins fewest-contradictions contests by construction. The specific monitorables it names (working-capital days, receivables ageing, unbilled contract assets) are nonetheless the right ones.

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: External change forces · Group: Internal change forces · Group: Industry and Value Chain · Group: Company - Competitive advantage · Group: Company - Economic strength · Group: Company - Management · Group: Risks · Group: Guidance · Group: Sell-side estimates · Group: Stock market narrative

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: SPIE SA (SPIE-FR, Euronext Paris), a European multi-technical services company (electrical, mechanical and HVAC installation and maintenance, power transmission and distribution, nuclear services, information and communication services, technical facility management and, since 2026, industrial automation) reporting five segments: Germany, France, North-Western Europe, Central Europe and Global Services Energy.

Request: T-P1

Peers: VINCI Energies (part of VINCI SA; the largest European multi-technical peer, selective bidding, Omexom grid business); Equans (part of Bouygues; margin-restoration programme from 2023); Eiffage Énergie Systèmes (part of Eiffage; French multi-technical peer expanding in Germany); Bilfinger (GBF-DE; industrial services peer whose September 2026 profit warning is a recent operating-deleverage precedent); Bravida (BRAV-SE; Nordic installer with a largely variable cost base and 2026 volume growth); Instalco (INSTAL-SE; Nordic installer running a margin turnaround after 2024 capacity cuts); Assemblin Caverion Group (private; merger synergy programme, and Caverion's historical project write-downs); Burkhalter (BRKN-CH; decentralised Swiss installer); Comfort Systems USA (FIX) and EMCOR Group (EME) (US installers whose data-centre work coincided with margin expansion). Inputs: What share of SPIE's cost base is fixed and what share variable, using the FY2025 split of purchases consumed (EUR 1,416.0m), external services (EUR 4,296.1m, including about EUR 1.8bn of subcontracting) and employment costs (EUR 3,677.9m) reported in the 2025 universal registration document, and how the split differs by segment? How did SPIE's EBITA margin and incremental margin move in 2009 to 2010, during the SAG integration (2015 to 2020), in 2020 (revenue down 4.8%), and through the 2022 to 2025 inflation (EBITA margin 6.7% in 2023, 7.2% in 2024, 7.6% in 2025, per company reports)? How much of the 2023 to 2025 margin gain came from mix (Germany, high-voltage grid), pricing and selectivity, acquisitions, and overhead absorption? What drop-through did SPIE achieve in H1 2026 (Deutsche Bank cited 11.9% against analyst assumptions of 9% to 10.5%, September 2026) and what drives the gap between H1 and H2 margins? How does reliance on subcontractors and temporary staff (over 56% of total expenses per the 2025 registration document) affect project margins in data-centre and high-voltage substation work, and what evidence exists of overruns (expert calls in July and October 2026 described setbacks at a Cologne data centre and delays on Dutch high-voltage projects)? How do SPIE's direct-labour ratios compare with VINCI Energies'? What did past cost and synergy programmes deliver against promises (SPIE's Worksphere synergies of at least EUR 9m; Equans' restructuring; Bravida's Swedish merger targeting SEK 65m of savings; Instalco's 2024 programme; Bilfinger's measures)? What synergies has SPIE disclosed for ROFA Industrial Automation and SGS Industrial Services (management described ROFA synergies as mainly commercial, March 2026)? How did margins and working capital of multi-technical groups behave when organic growth turned negative (2009, 2020, Bilfinger in 2026, Fasadgruppen in 2026), and which cost lines (subcontractors, temporary staff, overtime, overheads, core technicians) proved cuttable? Focus: Whether SPIE's largely variable, labour-heavy cost base can keep producing incremental margins high enough to lift the group EBITA margin from 7.6% (2025) to the company's 8.0% target for 2028 and beyond, and how far margins would fall if organic growth turned negative or subcontractor costs rose faster than contract indexation.

Request: T-P2

Peers: VINCI Energies (VINCI SA; same French and German markets, selective pricing); Equans (Bouygues; repriced Dutch and French contracts since 2023); Eiffage Énergie Systèmes (Eiffage; French tertiary and German competitor); Bilfinger (GBF-DE; framework-contract pass-through in industrial maintenance); Bravida (BRAV-SE; Swedish contracts without automatic indexation, recurring service mix); Instalco (INSTAL-SE; 57% cost-plus or variable contracts, tender competition in Sweden); Burkhalter (BRKN-CH; dynamic pass-through of metal costs); Renew Holdings (RNWH-GB; regulated UK infrastructure maintenance under five-year spend periods); Assemblin Caverion Group (private; Nordic competitor). Operating conditions: The German transmission and distribution capex upcycle with scarce high-voltage crews; French political and fiscal instability ahead of the 2027 presidential election, with weak new-build residential and tertiary building demand; wage inflation of about 4% to 4.5% in Germany and 5% to 6% in the Netherlands (Morgan Stanley, March 2025); copper above USD 14,000 a tonne and lead times of about three years for switchgear and transformers; a European recession with negative organic growth (as in 2009 and 2020); a data-centre co-location build-out whose developers face tighter financing; renewal of multi-year maintenance and framework contracts; public procurement under budget pressure. Inputs: What share of SPIE's revenue sits under multi-year indexed contracts, short-cycle maintenance work (repriced within two to three months, per Jefferies) and fixed-price projects, by segment, and what is the typical indexation lag? What evidence shows SPIE's prices rising faster than costs (Deutsche Bank, September 2026: revenue per employee outgrew staff cost per employee and Eurozone wage inflation over five years) and where were prices held or given back between 2022 and 2026? How durable are the 15% to 16% margins Bernstein reported for SPIE's German high-voltage grid services (July and September 2026), and what contract terms support them? What has SPIE's selectivity in French Building Solutions cost in volume and saved in margin (French organic growth -1.6% in 2025 and -0.7% in H1 2026, per company reports), and are residential contractors still bidding aggressively for tertiary work in France? What do customers, competitors and experts say about accepting price rises (an Equans director cited accepted increases of 5% to 10% in the Netherlands, September 2024)? What are SPIE's contract structures, switching costs and retention in maintenance (company: over 90% retention; about 85% of revenue visible at the start of each year) and in EDF nuclear frameworks? How did SPIE's and peers' pricing behave in 2009, 2020 and 2022 to 2023? How much of SPIE's 3% to 4% medium-term organic growth target relies on price (Morgan Stanley said about half relies on pricing discipline and indexation)? What terms (indexation, prepayments, liability caps) does SPIE use on mid-sized data-centre co-location work, given that it avoids very large hyperscaler contracts? Focus: Whether SPIE can keep raising prices above wage and material inflation in grid, nuclear and technical facility management through 2027 to 2029, how much of its organic growth is price, and whether its pricing in French buildings and industrial services erodes during the 2027 French political cycle and a German industrial slowdown.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: SPIE's business model contains interacting loops (negative working capital funding acquisitions, skilled-labour scarcity supporting price but capping volume, grid references winning further grid work) whose direction over 2027 to 2029 will decide whether its margin and cash gains compound or reverse. Peers: Bravida (BRAV-SE; a data-centre reference loop and a cash loop in which customer advances lifted then lowered cash conversion); Comfort Systems USA (FIX; a data-centre reference loop that compounded backlog); EMCOR Group (EME; working-capital swings on large contracts); Instalco (INSTAL-SE; a cash-to-leverage-to-acquisition loop that reversed in 2022 to 2025); Fasadgruppen (FG-SE; an acquisition loop that ran in reverse into a 2026 rights issue); VINCI Energies (VINCI SA; a steady self-funded bolt-on loop); Bilfinger (GBF-DE; momentum that reversed in 2026); Eiffage Énergie Systèmes (Eiffage; a competing German acquirer). Inputs: The self-funding loop: negative working capital (EUR -975.5m, about -34 days of revenue, at end-2025, company) and cash conversion above 100% fund bolt-ons, whose acquired working capital SPIE reportedly optimises by about 1x EBITA within twelve months (Bernstein); what strengthens or stops it (rising acquisition multiples as Brookfield, Eiffage, VINCI Energies and private-equity roll-ups bid for German installers; a revenue decline reversing working capital)? The labour loop: skilled-technician scarcity (Morgan Stanley cited 615 vacancies per 100 unemployed electrical engineers in Germany) supports pricing and margin, which fund recruitment, employee share ownership and referral hiring; where does wage inflation or subcontractor leakage turn it into a balancing loop? The grid reference loop: high-voltage and live-substation references and prequalifications (for example SPIE's five-substation contract for TenneT) win further awards and crews; how do equipment lead times and possible reductions in German grid operators' allowed returns limit it? The French loop: margin-over-volume selectivity and fibre run-off reduce volume; does fixed-cost under-absorption follow, or does the business shrink to a higher-margin core? The reflexive loop: how SPIE's share valuation (about 11.7x earnings in October 2026, per FactSet) affects the choice between buybacks and acquisitions and the competition for targets. The customer-financing loop: how utilities' cost of capital and data-centre developers' access to credit feed into SPIE's order intake and receivables. Focus: Whether SPIE's self-funding acquisition loop and its grid reference loop keep reinforcing margin and cash through 2028, or whether rising acquisition multiples, subcontractor dependence and a working-capital reversal turn them into balancing loops.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: Brokers' adjusted EPS estimates for SPIE diverge widely (FactSet FY2027 estimates ranged from EUR 3.02 to EUR 3.72 on 21 October 2026) and several inputs below EBITA changed in 2026 (two bond issues, the ORNANE conversion, and ROFA, described by Bernstein in March 2026 as SPIE's third-largest acquisition since its 2015 IPO). Peers: Bilfinger (GBF-DE; keeps share-based compensation inside adjusted EBITA, a definitional contrast); Instalco (INSTAL-SE; net financial items and minorities absorbing a large share of EBIT); Bravida (BRAV-SE; consensus noise from one-off gains and restructuring); VINCI SA (bolt-on cadence and acquisition multiples in VINCI Energies); Eiffage (German acquisitions by Eiffage Énergie Systèmes); Burkhalter (BRKN-CH; high payout and accounting contrast). Inputs: Each broker's definition of SPIE's adjusted EPS, including the treatment of amortisation of acquired intangibles (EUR 46m in H1 2026 and EUR 58m in H1 2025, company), IFRS 2 share-based charges (EUR 59.8m in FY2025, cited by Deutsche Bank), ORNANE fair-value effects (a EUR 132.9m non-cash charge in FY2025) and the French exceptional corporate tax contribution (EUR 15.8m in FY2025). The net financial expense path for FY2026 to FY2028 after the EUR 600m 3.875% sustainability-linked bond due 2031 (May 2026), the EUR 500m 4.875% sustainability-linked bonds due March 2032 and the ORNANE refinancing completed on 20 October 2026, the swapped share of the term loan, lease interest and pension interest (pensions EUR 582m per FactSet); Deutsche Bank estimated about EUR 15m of additional annual interest and a 4% cut to FY2027 EPS (October 2026). The share count path: 3.36m new shares from the ORNANE conversion, the annual Share For You plan, performance shares (526,287 under the 2026 to 2028 plan) and anti-dilutive buybacks (1.25m shares for EUR 59m in Q1 2026). The tax rate (normative 30.2% in 2025, company) and the French corporate surtax in the 2026 and 2027 budgets. ROFA's economics: the price (EUR 611.8m for 89.7% in June 2026 and EUR 71.0m for 10.3% in July 2026, company), ROFA's 2025 revenue (about EUR 430m) and EBITA margin, net cash acquired, the purchase price allocation and its amortisation, and the basis for the reported mid-single-digit EPS accretion (Morgan Stanley, March 2026); SGS's price and margin. Minorities and associates. The dividend payout policy (about 40% of adjusted net income). How brokers treat unannounced acquisitions and deleveraging in FY2027 to FY2028. Focus: Pin down the FY2026 to FY2028 bridge from SPIE's adjusted EBITA to adjusted and reported EPS line by line (amortisation, net interest after the 2026 bond issues, tax, share count after the ORNANE conversion and employee plans, minorities), and establish ROFA's actual acquisition multiple and accretion, so that the EPS path can be built from company inputs rather than from a consensus whose definitions differ.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 25 October 2026, 23:29 to 23:48 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
SPIE SA (SPIE-FR): answers to the second set of questions (the operating and financial model, 18 months to four years)

Written 25-Oct-26 for the Investment Analyst answering the third and fourth sets (predictability; guidance, consensus and narrative).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

How to read these answers

  • Money and bases. EUR millions unless stated. "EBITA" is SPIE's adjusted EBITA (excludes amortisation of acquired intangibles, "PPA amortisation", and IFRS 2 share-based charges of EUR 59.8m in FY25). "EBITDA" in my figures is adjusted EBITA plus depreciation and amortisation excluding PPA amortisation; this is the brokers' basis (FY25 EUR 1,077m, 10.4% of revenue), not FactSet's "operating" EBITDA (EUR 1,062m, 10.2%). "Adjusted EPS" is the company's adjusted basis (FY25 EUR 2.69 = adjusted net income EUR 457.6m over about 170.1m shares). "Net debt" is the company's, excluding IFRS 16 leases (EUR 1,145.3m end-2025; EUR 1,967.1m end-June 2026), unless labelled otherwise.
  • The base year I build from (company figures). FY25 revenue EUR 10,379.9m; EBITA EUR 793.5m (7.64%); H1 2025 revenue about EUR 4,977m and EBITA EUR 300.4m (6.0%); H2 2025 revenue about EUR 5,403m and EBITA EUR 493.1m (9.1%); H1 2026 revenue EUR 5,156.6m (+3.6%: organic +1.2%, scope about +2.6%, currency -0.2%) and EBITA EUR 321.4m (6.2%).
  • My base case in one line. Revenue EUR 11,119m (FY26), 12,062m (FY27), 12,940m (FY28), 13,847m (FY29), 14,742m (FY30); EBITA margin 7.84%, 7.93%, 8.05%, 8.12%, 8.18%; adjusted EPS EUR 2.89, 3.13, 3.55, 3.87, 4.19; company free cash flow EUR 565m, 635m, 720m, 790m, 855m. Consensus (FactSet median, 21-Oct-26): EPS EUR 3.11, 3.46, 3.73. My EBITA is at or slightly above consensus; my EPS is 7% (FY26), 10% (FY27) and 5% (FY28) below it. The gap sits entirely below EBITA. My model includes continued bolt-on acquisitions of EUR 350m a year from FY27; consensus models none.
  • The one reconciliation the Consumer must hold on to. In FY25, EUR 793.5m of EBITA became EUR 457.6m of adjusted net income: a conversion of 57.7% (FY24: 59.0%). Consensus has that conversion rising to 60.6% (FY26), 62.1% (FY27) and 65.1% (FY28) while net debt, coupons, the share count and the French tax surcharge all rise. My base case has 57.1%, 57.3% and 60.0%. This single ratio explains most of the EPS gap and is the most testable item in the case.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Revenue. Organic growth about 2.0% in FY2026 (H1 actual 1.2%; H2 about 2.7%), 3.0% in FY2027, 3.3% in FY2028 and FY2029, 3.0% in FY2030, of which price and indexation about 1.5 to 2 pp a year (wage inflation normalising, so volume must supply the rest, mainly German grid work). Acquired growth about 5.3% in FY2026 (ROFA from June, SGS from about September, BLOCK, Worley, Artemys, Invizo, nimeg), about 5.0% in FY2027 (carry-over about EUR 360m plus half a year of new bolt-ons, about EUR 240m), then about 3.5% to 3.7% a year. Currency -0.2% in FY2026, nil after. Consensus models no acquisitions after 2026; I model EUR 350m a year because SPIE has bought every year since 2013 and its 7% to 9% total-growth target assumes it. Without new acquisitions my revenue would be about EUR 11,822m in FY2027 and EUR 12,238m in FY2028.
  • Margin. EBITA margin 7.84%, 7.93%, 8.05%, 8.12%, 8.18%. Drop-through on organic volume at about 11% to 12% (FY2024 10.7%; H1 2026 11.7%) against a 7.8% average adds about 11 bps a year at 3% organic; mix (ROFA, SGS above the group margin; German grid at 15% to 16%), Central Europe's recovery and fading fibre drag add a few bps; I assume no large cost programme (the company has none). EBITDA margin = EBITA margin plus D&A excluding PPA amortisation of 2.75% to 2.8% of revenue (2.73% in FY2025; leases rising with acquisitions).
  • Half-years (Part 3). H2 2026: revenue about EUR 5,962m (+10.4%; organic about 2.7%, scope about +8%), EBITA about EUR 550m (9.23%, +10 bps). H1 2027: revenue about EUR 5,717m (+10.9%; organic about 3.3% against the weather-hit H1 2026, scope about +7.6%), EBITA about EUR 372m (6.5%, +30 bps, half of it from ROFA's flatter seasonality). H2 2027: revenue about EUR 6,345m (+6.4%), EBITA about EUR 584m (9.2%). Part 3 EBITDA margins are on the same basis as Part 1 (adjusted EBITA plus D&A excluding PPA amortisation); my estimates of that basis for H1 2025, H2 2025 and H1 2026 are about 8.8%, 11.8% and 9.1%. FactSet's own "operating" EBITDA half-year history (8.6%, 11.6%, 8.2%) is on a different basis and should not be compared directly.
  • Net financial items (cost of net debt, lease interest, pension interest, discounting and other; excluding ORNANE fair-value effects): about -111 in FY2025 rising to -127 and -140 as ROFA's debt, the EUR 600m 3.875% bond (May 2026) and the EUR 500m 4.875% bond (October 2026, replacing the 2% ORNANE) feed through and lease interest rises (EUR 9.9m in H1 2026 against EUR 6.5m); then falling as cash pays down debt. The EUR 600m term loan due October 2027 (half swapped) is assumed refinanced at about 4% to 4.5%.
  • Tax. All-in rate on adjusted pre-tax profit, including the French exceptional contribution: 32.8% (FY2026; EUR 10.1m already booked in H1 2026, about EUR 18m for the year), 32.5% (FY2027; the 2027 draft budget extends the surcharge), then 30.6% to 30.8% assuming it lapses after the 2027 elections (a 50/50 call; if it persists, FY2028 EPS is about 2.7% lower). The underlying rate drifts up with the German mix (Germany 30.7%).
  • Share count. FY2026 +3.80m other than for cash: 3,365,843 shares from the ORNANE redemption on 20 October 2026 plus 432,638 performance shares (April 2026). FY2027 to FY2030 +0.5m a year of performance shares (526,287 granted under the 2026 to 2028 plan). The Share For You employee plan (about EUR 60m a year at a 20% discount) and the anti-dilutive spring buyback (EUR 59m in Q1 2026) are in Part 2 and roughly cancel in cash. Because employees subscribe at a discount, the actual share count still rises about 0.4m a year more than a cash-neutral entry implies (about 0.25% of EPS a year); my EPS cross-check uses the actual count: average diluted shares of about 172.0m (FY2026), 175.0m, 175.9m, 176.9m, 177.8m.
  • Dividend. 40% of adjusted EPS, rounded up slightly for FY2026 and FY2027 because the company's headline adjusted EPS may add back the French surcharge.
  • Working capital (Part 2) - read this before reading the model's cash flow. I have entered working capital on FactSet's trade definition (receivables plus inventories less payables), which is the definition in the model's history (12.1% of revenue at end-2025). On that definition SPIE's working capital is positive, but the company's own working capital, which also counts contract liabilities, customer advances and tax and social payables, is strongly negative (EUR -975.5m, about -34 days, at end-2025), and growth releases cash. I have therefore set the ratio to fall so that the model's working-capital cash flow equals the company-basis flow I expect (about +EUR 15m in FY2026 and +EUR 40m a year after). The falling ratio is a device to get the cash flow right, not a forecast that trade receivables will fall.
  • Capex and leases. Capex 0.8% of revenue (0.72% in FY2025; consensus 0.8%). Lease payments (principal and interest) about 1.65% to 1.7% of revenue, rising with acquired branches and vehicles.
  • Acquisitions. FY2026: EUR 765m of net assets acquired in H1 (ROFA's 89.7%, BLOCK, Worley, nimeg, Artemys, Invizo) plus about EUR 265m in H2 (ROFA's remaining 10.3% for EUR 71.0m in July, SGS at about EUR 150m to 180m, smaller deals). FY2027 to FY2030: EUR 350m a year, at about 9x EBITA. Minority put options (EUR 215.1m at end-2025) will be paid out of this line over time.

The company's guidance, and how your inputs compare with it

  • FY2026 guidance (reiterated 30 July 2026): "strong total revenue growth and continued EBITA margin expansion" and cash conversion of about 100%. My inputs meet it: revenue +7.1%, EBITA margin 7.84% (+20 bps), free cash flow about 113% of adjusted net income. Consensus: revenue EUR 11,239m (+8.3%), EBIT EUR 874m (7.8%); my revenue is about 1% lower (I assume a smaller H2 scope effect than brokers), my EBITA in line (EUR 872m).
  • 2028 targets (March 2026 Capital Markets Day update): EBITA margin 8.0%, EBITA above EUR 1bn, total growth 7% to 9% a year including 3% to 4% organic, cumulative free cash flow above EUR 2bn over 2025 to 2028, leverage below 2.0x, 40% payout. My FY2028: margin 8.05%, EBITA EUR 1,042m, total growth 7.3% (bottom of the range) with organic 3.3%, cumulative FCF about EUR 2.4bn, leverage about 1.25x. Consensus FY2028 EBIT EUR 988m (7.9%) is below the target because brokers model no acquisitions.
  • No EPS guidance. My adjusted EPS (EUR 2.89, 3.13, 3.55 for FY2026 to FY2028) is 7%, 10% and 5% below consensus (EUR 3.11, 3.46, 3.73). The gap is entirely below EBITA: share count (the October 2026 ORNANE shares and employee-plan creep), higher net financial items, and the recurring French surcharge.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • Revenue. Company revenue (EUR 10,379.9m in FY2025) equals the brokers' basis (EUR 10,380m). FactSet's history line (EUR 10,397m) differs slightly; use growth rates, not levels, across the two.
  • EBITA and EBIT. The company's adjusted EBITA (EUR 793.5m) excludes PPA amortisation (about EUR 100m a year; EUR 46m in H1 2026) and IFRS 2 share-based charges (EUR 59.8m in FY2025). The FactSet consensus EBIT median (EUR 874m for FY2026) is on this adjusted EBITA basis; the consensus mean (EUR 817m) is pulled down by brokers who deduct PPA amortisation or IFRS 2 charges. Bilfinger, by contrast, keeps share-based pay inside its adjusted EBITA.
  • EBITDA. Brokers' basis (EUR 1,077m in FY2025, 10.4%) = adjusted EBITA plus D&A excluding PPA amortisation; FactSet's "operating" EBITDA history (EUR 1,062m, 10.2%) is about EUR 15m lower. My Part 1 margins are on the brokers' basis, so the model may show a step of about 0.2 pp between FY2025 history and FY2026.
  • Adjusted net income and EPS. Reported net income (EUR 176.4m in FY2025) plus PPA amortisation after tax, plus IFRS 2 charges, plus ORNANE fair-value effects (EUR 132.9m in FY2025; ended October 2026), plus integration and restructuring costs, gives adjusted net income (EUR 457.6m); divided by about 170.1m shares gives EUR 2.69. Whether the company also adds back the French exceptional contribution (EUR 15.8m in FY2025) is not settled by the evidence; FY2025's adjusted net income reconciles to EBITA only if that charge, or an equivalent extra financial cost, is deducted. My EPS treats the surcharge as a cost; if the company adds it back, its headline adjusted EPS will be about EUR 0.10 higher than mine in FY2026 and FY2027. Several brokers differ on these items; individual FY2026 EPS ranges from EUR 2.76 (Deutsche Bank, which appears to deduct IFRS 2 charges) to EUR 3.32 (Oddo BHF).
  • Reported EPS (basic): about EUR 2.13 (FY2026), 2.38 (FY2027), 2.82 (FY2028), against EUR 1.05 (FY2025), once PPA amortisation and IFRS 2 charges are deducted and ORNANE fair-value effects disappear.
  • Free cash flow. The company's free cash flow (EUR 523.7m in FY2025) deducts capex, lease payments, interest and tax, not acquisitions or dividends. FactSet's history "free cash flow" (EUR 552m in FY2025) is a different construction (operating cash flow less capex and dividends) and must not be compared with it; the FactSet consensus FCF estimate (EUR 610m for FY2026) is closer to the company's definition.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: SPIE SA (SPIE-FR, Euronext Paris), a European multi-technical services company (electrical, mechanical and HVAC installation and maintenance, power transmission and distribution, nuclear services, information and communication services, technical facility management and, since 2026, industrial automation through ROFA Industrial Automation) reporting five segments: Germany, France, North-Western Europe, Central Europe and Global Services Energy.

Request: T-D1

Peers: VINCI Energies (part of VINCI SA; the largest European multi-technical peer, Omexom grid business, steady self-funded bolt-on programme); Equans (part of Bouygues; margin restoration since 2023, the main former price-setter); Eiffage Énergie Systèmes (part of Eiffage; French competitor building a German platform through Salvia and EQOS); Bilfinger (GBF-DE; industrial services peer whose September 2026 guidance cut is the recent precedent for deferred industrial call-offs); Bravida (BRAV-SE; Nordic installer with a volume inflection and data-centre growth); Instalco (INSTAL-SE; acquisition loop that ran into leverage and a margin reset in 2022 to 2025, now buying in Germany via Fabri); Fasadgruppen (FG-SE; roll-up that ended in a 2026 rights issue); Assemblin Caverion Group (private; merged Nordic competitor with a synergy programme); Burkhalter (BRKN-CH; decentralised Swiss installer); Comfort Systems USA (FIX) and EMCOR Group (EME) (US installers whose data-centre work coincided with margin expansion). Operating conditions: the German transmission and distribution capex upcycle with transformer and switchgear lead times of two to three years and scarce high-voltage crews; French fiscal and political stress before the April to May 2027 presidential election, with weak tertiary building demand and a corporate tax surcharge; normalising wage inflation in Germany and France with a catch-up in the Netherlands; rising coupons after the 2026 bond issues; a German industrial and automotive capex downturn; rising acquisition multiples for German installers as Eiffage, VINCI Energies, Brookfield and Nordic consolidators bid; tighter financing for data-centre co-location developers. Inputs: Can SPIE move organic growth from 2.0% in 2025 and 1.2% in H1 2026 (company) to its 3% to 4% medium-term target, given its organic growth record over 2015 to 2025 and how far that record relied on price rather than volume? Can it lift its EBITA margin from 7.6% (2025) to its 8.0% target for 2028 (raised in March 2026 from at least 7.7%) without a cost programme, through drop-through on volume, grid and nuclear mix, and recovery in Central Europe (H1 2026 EBITA margin 2.4%, company)? Does its demonstrated record of meeting or beating margin targets (6.7% reached in 2023, at least 7.1% delivered at 7.2% in 2024, at least 7.6% delivered in 2025) extend to integrating its largest acquisition cohort since SAG: ROFA Industrial Automation (EUR 611.8m for 89.7% in June 2026 and EUR 71.0m for 10.3% in July 2026, about EUR 430m of revenue, mainly factory automation for automotive logistics and pharmaceuticals) and SGS Industrial Services (about EUR 180m of revenue, margin slightly above 10%), under a CEO in post since 1 May 2026 (Markus Holzke, formerly head of SPIE Germany)? How dependable is the self-funding model (working capital of EUR -975.5m, about -34 days, at end-2025; cash conversion above 100% in most years since 2015) when acquisition prices rise? How much does reliance on subcontractors and temporary labour (over 56% of total expenses, 2025 universal registration document) limit execution in new high-voltage and data-centre work (expert calls in July and October 2026 described a delayed Cologne data-centre project and Dutch high-voltage substation projects running late)? What happened to multi-technical and installation companies that moved up the value chain into factory automation, or that paid well above their usual acquisition multiples for a platform? Focus: Whether SPIE's 2028 margin and EBITA targets rest only on strengths it has demonstrated (selective bidding, pass-through pricing, prequalified grid crews, self-funded bolt-ons), and whether the 2026 move into factory automation, at the price paid and under a new CEO, depends on a capability it has not yet shown (earning a return on a larger, more cyclical platform through cross-selling).

Request: T-D2

Peers: VINCI Energies (VINCI SA; comparable margin and bolt-on model, the benchmark for outcome dispersion); Equans (Bouygues; a wide-cone margin recovery); Eiffage Énergie Systèmes (Eiffage; a ten-year margin climb from about 2% to about 6%); Bilfinger (GBF-DE; a 2026 outcome far below guidance); Bravida (BRAV-SE; restructuring charges and receivables provisions inside a variable cost base); Instalco (INSTAL-SE; net financial items and minorities absorbing a large share of operating profit); Fasadgruppen (FG-SE; negative operating leverage and equity dilution); Caverion and Eltel (historical project write-downs); Burkhalter (BRKN-CH; high-payout comparison); Rexel (RXL-FR; French-listed electrification channel exposed to the same French tax and sovereign-spread conditions). Operating conditions: a sudden demand shock (2009, 2020) against a gradual slowdown; French political and fiscal stress with a recurring corporate surcharge; rising interest costs and bond refinancing; a German industrial capex downturn; wage-inflation spikes with indexation lags; subcontractor cost escalation in tight labour markets; delays in equipment deliveries for grid projects. Inputs: Which few drivers separate SPIE's next 18 months to four years from 2019 to 2025: acquired growth from the 2026 deals; an organic acceleration led by German grid work; margin progression towards 8.0%; and the items below EBITA? On the items below EBITA, test: the net financial expense path after the EUR 600m 3.875% sustainability-linked bond due 2031 (May 2026) and the EUR 500m 4.875% sustainability-linked bond due March 2032 that refinanced the EUR 400m 2% ORNANE (20 October 2026; Deutsche Bank estimated about EUR 15m of additional annual interest and a 4% cut to FY2027 EPS, 15 October 2026); the 3,365,843 new shares from the ORNANE redemption and the annual employee share plan (2,101,883 shares in December 2025) against anti-dilutive buybacks (1.25m shares for EUR 59m in Q1 2026); the French exceptional corporate tax contribution (EUR 15.8m in FY2025; a further EUR 10.1m exceptional tax charge in H1 2026, company); and how FY2025 EBITA of EUR 793.5m became adjusted net income of EUR 457.6m (company) compared with what each broker assumes for FY2026 to FY2028. How wide has the range of outcomes been historically: SPIE's 2020 (revenue -4.8%; EBITA from EUR 416m in 2019 to EUR 339m), Bilfinger's 2026 guidance cut to a 4.6% to 5.0% EBITA margin, Fasadgruppen's Q1 2026 (organic -10.8%, EBITA margin -0.7%), Caverion's and Eltel's write-downs? How often were installer forecasts that rested on one or two drivers (a single large acquisition, a single end market) met? What could widen SPIE's range: ROFA's exposure to automotive capex, execution overruns in high-voltage and data-centre projects, the Middle East (Global Services Energy organic -9.3% in Q2 2026)? Focus: How concentrated the drivers of SPIE's coming EPS are, and in particular how much sits in operations and how much in the items below operating profit (interest, share count, the French surcharge), and how wide and how skewed the range of FY2026 and FY2027 EPS outcomes is once those items are set out line by line on the company's own inputs.

Request: T-I1

Peers: Bilfinger (GBF-DE; guidance cut in September 2026, Q3 2026 results and Program Agile); VINCI SA (VINCI Energies' Q3 2026 revenue, 22 October 2026, and its acquisition pace); Eiffage (Eiffage Énergie Systèmes' Q3 2026 revenue and French tax treatment); Bouygues (Equans' margin guidance); Bravida (BRAV-SE; Q3 2026 results, 23 October 2026); Instalco (INSTAL-SE; Q3 2026 results, 22 to 23 October 2026); Rexel (RXL-FR; French tax surcharge lifting its group rate); Burkhalter (BRKN-CH); Assemblin Caverion Group (private; quarterly bond reporting). Operating conditions: the run-up to and aftermath of the French presidential election (April to May 2027) and the 2027 French budget; German grid and infrastructure-fund spending from 2027; weather-driven quarterly volatility (Q1 2026 organic -0.9%, Q2 2026 +3.1%, company); rising bond yields (Bund at a post-2011 high of 3.44% in September 2026); a German industrial slowdown. Inputs: SPIE's FY2026 guidance ("strong total revenue growth and continued EBITA margin expansion", cash conversion of about 100%, reiterated 30 July 2026) and its 2028 targets (EBITA margin 8.0%, EBITA above EUR 1bn, 7% to 9% total annual growth including 3% to 4% organic, cumulative free cash flow above EUR 2bn over 2025 to 2028, leverage below 2.0x); its record of setting "at least" targets and firming them at the half-year or Q3 update; the next events (Q3 2026 sales on 30 October 2026, FY2026 results on 5 March 2027, H1 2027 results in July 2027, a Capital Markets Day expected in 2027 with a 2030 roadmap). FactSet consensus at 21 October 2026: EPS EUR 3.11 (FY2026), 3.46 (FY2027), 3.73 (FY2028); revenue EUR 11,239m, 12,042m, 12,563m; EBIT EUR 874m, 946m, 988m; individual FY2026 EPS from EUR 2.76 (Deutsche Bank) to EUR 3.32 (Oddo BHF); 85% buy ratings; FY2027 EPS revisions 0% over one month and -1% over three months. Which brokers have updated their share count for the 3,365,843 ORNANE shares and their interest line for the EUR 500m 4.875% bond, and which have not? How does each broker treat the French exceptional tax contribution and IFRS 2 charges (EUR 59.8m in FY2025) in adjusted EPS? Morgan Stanley cut FY2026 EPS about 5% for a higher tax rate; Deutsche Bank cut FY2027 EPS 4% for the refinancing: are others likely to follow, and when? Do brokers model acquisitions or deleveraging after 2026? What did peers' latest results and guidance imply for SPIE's Q4 2026 and 2027? Focus: In which direction SPIE's consensus EPS, EBITA and revenue are likely to be revised over the next 18 months, how much of any revision comes from operations and how much from the October 2026 refinancing, the share issuance and the French surcharge, at which event revisions are most likely, and whether the company's margin and cash guidance is more likely to be met or raised at the FY2026 results and the 2027 Capital Markets Day.

Request: T-I2

Peers: VINCI Energies (VINCI SA); Equans (Bouygues); Eiffage Énergie Systèmes (Eiffage); Bilfinger (GBF-DE); Bravida (BRAV-SE); Instalco (INSTAL-SE); Rexel (RXL-FR; the French-listed electrification channel de-rated alongside SPIE); Renew Holdings (RNWH-GB; regulated grid maintenance with a record order book and a flat share price); Arcadis (ARCAD-NL; engineering re-rating); Comfort Systems USA (FIX; the data-centre reference story). Operating conditions: a risk-off market regime in which French sovereign spreads are at decade highs; money paying makers of scarce electrical equipment (ABB, Siemens Energy) rather than installers and distributors; a rotation into Nordic installers with volume inflections; the French election cycle; tightening credit for AI and data-centre developers. Inputs: (1) German transmission and distribution work (cited at 34% of 2024 revenue, a two-to-three-year high-voltage backlog, German organic growth +7.3% in Q2 2026) converting into faster group organic growth from 2027 as public infrastructure money arrives, and the equipment lead times that delay it; (2) the costs below operating profit that rise in 2026 to 2027: the 3,365,843 ORNANE shares, the EUR 500m 4.875% and EUR 600m 3.875% bonds, the French exceptional tax contribution, and the exclusion of IFRS 2 charges (EUR 59.8m in FY2025) from adjusted EBITA; (3) the price paid for ROFA Industrial Automation (EUR 682.8m for 100% of a business with about EUR 430m of revenue and a high single-digit margin) and its exposure to automotive and pharmaceutical factory capex; (4) the shift in SPIE's half-year margin pattern from consolidating ROFA and SGS; (5) the correlation between SPIE's valuation and French ten-year yields (Deutsche Bank cited -0.88, 15 October 2026) against France's 32% share of revenue; (6) capital allocation at a low valuation: buybacks limited to offsetting employee dilution while acquisitions are stepped up; (7) subcontractor dependence and execution in new high-voltage and data-centre projects; (8) reported EPS losing ORNANE fair-value noise from the fourth quarter of 2026; (9) rising acquisition multiples for German installers. For each: which sell-side analysts (Bernstein, Morgan Stanley, Deutsche Bank, Jefferies, RBC, Oddo BHF, CIC, UBS, Portzamparc) and which investor commentators (including on X/Twitter and in investor letters) discuss it, how prominently, since when and with what view. Focus: Which of these nine dynamics are widely understood by SPIE's owners and sector investors and which are discussed by one or two analysts or by no one, primarily for the next 18 months, with particular attention to the costs below operating profit, the ROFA price and the choice between buybacks and acquisitions.

Request: OWN-1

Layer 3 and Layer 4: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months / Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

Written 27 October 2026, 13:59 to 14:17 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
SPIE SA (SPIE-FR): answers to the third and fourth sets of questions (predictability; guidance, consensus and narrative)

Written 27-Oct-26 for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo for Richard.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

How to read these answers

  • Bases. EUR. "EBITA" is SPIE's adjusted EBITA (FY25 EUR 793.5m, 7.6%). "Adjusted EPS, company basis" is the headline figure SPIE and most brokers publish (FY25 EUR 2.69); the AlphaSense report on the French surcharge (own-2) establishes that this headline excludes the French exceptional corporate tax contribution (the 30% normative rate "excludes the exceptional contribution", interim report note 9, 30 July 2026). "Adjusted EPS, economic basis" is the second set's figure, which treats the surcharge as a cost (about EUR 0.10 a share below the company basis in FY26 and FY27). The Financial Analyst's model (update 1) carries the economic basis: EUR 2.89 (FY26), 3.16 (FY27), 3.58 (FY28). Consensus is the FactSet median at 21-Oct-26: EUR 3.11, 3.46, 3.73, on the company basis.
  • Three things to carry through every answer. (1) The Q3 2026 sales release is on 30 October 2026, three days after this is written; the own-4 report could not cover it, so the next-event expectations below are the brokers' previews (organic about 2.5% to 3%; Germany about 6%; France about -0.5%; scope about +8.8%; Q3 revenue about EUR 2,819m). (2) The model's FY25 EPS of EUR 2.08 is on the "Viewforth definition" while its FY26 to FY30 EPS are on the company-adjusted basis; the model's "+38.8% EPS growth in FY26" is a basis break, not growth, and the memo must not quote it. (3) The share price (EUR 40.76, 23-Oct-26) has fallen 13% in three months and sits 12% below its 200-day average while FY27 consensus EPS is unchanged over six months; the dashboard's three pinned letters are C, C, C.
  • The model's cross-check gaps. The one-page summary shows the second set's EPS 1% below the model's in FY27 to FY30 (EUR 3.13 against 3.16 and so on) and its free cash flow 1% to 3.5% above the model's. Neither is flagged as material, and neither is a forecasting error: the EPS gap is the share count (the model's cash-neutral buyback entry misses about 0.4m shares a year created by the 20% employee discount; I correct the input in inputs-changed.md); the free cash flow gap is definitional (the model deducts the full P&L net financial items, about EUR 20m of which, pension interest and discounting, is not cash, whereas the company's free cash flow deducts cash interest only). The Consumer should use the model's figures for the memo and the second set's figures as the company-definition cross-check.
  • Where I change the inputs. Two lines: the tax rate for FY28 to FY30 (the French surcharge is now more likely than not to persist beyond 2027, so I probability-weight it rather than assume it lapses), and the non-cash share count change for FY27 to FY30. Both are small (FY28 EPS about -2% in total). I do not change net financial items, although the evidence points to the risk being on the high side (see D-03 and D-04).

Appendix Forecast inputs the Investment Analyst changed before the memo

Changed forecast inputs: SPIE SA (SPIE-FR), as at 27-Oct-26 (third and fourth sets)

Money in millions of EUR, except per-share figures; costs and outflows negative. Only the lines changed are shown; every other line of the second set's forecast inputs (as at 25-Oct-26) stands.

Part 1. Profit and loss inputs, one column for each of the five fiscal years

Input FY2026 (year to 31-Dec-26) FY2027 (year to 31-Dec-27) FY2028 (year to 31-Dec-28) FY2029 (year to 31-Dec-29) FY2030 (year to 31-Dec-30)
Tax rate (%) 32.8 32.5 31.7 31.7 31.7

The French exceptional corporate tax contribution (EUR 15.8m in FY2025; EUR 10.1m in H1 2026; extended by the 2026 Finance Act and again in the draft 2027 Finance Bill for companies with French revenue above EUR 3bn, which SPIE France exceeds) is now more likely than not to persist beyond 2027 (Morgan Stanley and Bernstein model it indefinitely; Jefferies embeds it in 2027 and 2028; fiscal analysts judge termination improbable while the deficit exceeds 5% of GDP), so I replace the second set's assumption that it lapses from FY2028 with a probability-weighted rate (about 60% persistence of a EUR 16m charge on adjusted pre-tax profit of about EUR 905m to 1,085m, about 1.1 pp a year) on top of the underlying 30.6% to 30.8%.

Input FY2026 (year to 31-Dec-26) FY2027 (year to 31-Dec-27) FY2028 (year to 31-Dec-28) FY2029 (year to 31-Dec-29) FY2030 (year to 31-Dec-30)
Change in the share count, other than through buybacks or new shares issued for cash (millions of shares) 3.80 0.90 0.90 0.90 0.90

The model's EPS runs about 1% above the second set's cross-check from FY2027 (EUR 3.16 against 3.13) because the Part 2 "buybacks less new shares issued for cash" entry is cash-neutral while employees subscribe at a 20% discount (Share For You 2026 at EUR 35.36 against a market price of about EUR 41), which creates about 0.4m more shares a year than the buyback cancels; adding that 0.4m to the 0.5m of performance shares makes the model's average diluted share count match the actual count (about 175.0m in FY2027 rising to about 177.8m in FY2030), and FY2026 is unchanged because the December 2026 issue barely affects that year's average.

Appendix The share-price co-movement table the Investment Analyst was given

Co-movement of SPIE-FR with its dashboard peers (computed 2026-10-23 from the master dashboard's daily prices)

What this is: daily close-to-close returns over rolling windows of 126 trading days (about six months). Correlation: how closely the daily moves line up (1 = always together, 0 = unrelated). Beta: how far the stock moves for a 1% move in the basket. R-squared: the share of the stock's daily price variance the peer basket explains; the remainder is the stock's own (idiosyncratic) movement. Basket: the peers below, equal-weighted. Prices only; the multiple's own history is in the financials block.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for the memo

Research requests for the next set

Company: SPIE SA (SPIE-FR, Euronext Paris): a European multi-technical services company (electrical, mechanical and HVAC installation and maintenance, power transmission and distribution, nuclear services, information and communication services, technical facility management and, since 2026, industrial automation through ROFA Industrial Automation) reporting five segments: Germany, France, North-Western Europe, Central Europe and Global Services Energy.

Request: OWN-1

Question: For SPIE SA (SPIE-FR), what do ROFA Industrial Automation AG's German statutory filings (Bundesanzeiger or Unternehmensregister annual accounts for 2022, 2023, 2024 and, if filed, 2025) and any other public record show for ROFA's revenue, operating result (EBIT or EBITA), net income, cash and cash equivalents, financial debt, customer prepayments and contract liabilities, equity and order backlog at each year-end; how do those balance-sheet figures reconcile with the consideration SPIE disclosed (EUR 611.8m for 89.7% on 3 June 2026 and EUR 71.0m for the remaining 10.3% on 10 July 2026, per SPIE's 2026 first-half financial report of 29 July 2026) and with the "high single-digit EBITA multiple" SPIE stated on 2 March 2026 and the "mid-single-digit EPS accretion" and "about 0.2x of added leverage" that Morgan Stanley published on 2 March 2026; what has SPIE disclosed since June 2026 about the provisional purchase price allocation (goodwill, brand, customer relationships, backlog) and the cash acquired; and what do German automation-industry sources, trade press and expert calls say about ROFA's order intake, pricing and customer mix (automotive, pharmaceutical, logistics) since the acquisition closed?

Request: OWN-2

Question: What did SPIE SA (SPIE-FR) report at its Q3 2026 sales release on 30 October 2026 (organic, scope and currency growth for the group and for each of the five segments; the contribution of ROFA, SGS Industrial Services, BLOCK, Worley Power Services, Artemys and Clauser; management's comments on French fourth-quarter trends, the fibre roll-out run-off, Building Solutions selectivity, Global Services Energy and the Middle East suspensions, the German high-voltage and data-centre pipeline, second-half margin, working capital and cash, leverage, the ORNANE refinancing completed on 20 October 2026, the Share For You 2026 employee plan, and the wording of FY2026 guidance, including whether a margin floor was stated), and how did each covering broker (Bernstein, Morgan Stanley, Deutsche Bank, Jefferies, RBC, Oddo BHF, CIC, UBS, Portzamparc and any other house) change its revenue, EBITA, net financial expense, tax rate, share count, adjusted EPS, rating and target price in the two weeks after the release, and which of them stated that they had updated for the 3,365,843 new shares and the EUR 500m 4.875% bond?

Request: OWN-3

Question: For SPIE SA (SPIE-FR) and the other French-listed companies with a majority of revenue outside France whose shares have been described as trading with French sovereign risk (VINCI, Eiffage, Bouygues, Rexel, Elis, Edenred, Sodexo and comparable mid-caps), how did share prices and forward price-to-earnings multiples behave in the three, six and twelve months after the April to May 2017 presidential election, the June to July 2024 dissolution and legislative elections, and the budget crises of late 2024 and 2025, relative to the OAT-Bund ten-year spread and to European sector peers; what did sell-side and strategy research say at the time about when and why the French discount on such companies closed or persisted; what correlation between SPIE's valuation and French ten-year yields have brokers measured in 2026 (Deutsche Bank cited -0.88 on 15 October 2026) and in earlier years; and what do sell-side strategists, Bernstein (24 September 2026, on French exposure as a value trap or mispriced political risk) and others now say about how the multiples of French-listed mid-caps with largely non-French revenue would respond to each of the main 2027 presidential election outcomes and to the passage or failure of the 2027 budget?
